The Witt groups of extended quadratic forms over ℤ \mathbb{Z}
Diarmuid Crowley, Csaba Nagy

Abstract
. We study quadratic form parameters Q Q over the integers and extended quadratic forms with values in Q Q , which we call Q Q -forms . Certain form parameters Q Q appeared in Wall’s work on the classification of almost closed ( n − 1 ) (n{-}1) -connected 2 ​ n 2n -manifolds via Q Q -forms. Baues, Ranicki and Schlichting independently developed definitions of extended quadratic forms in more general settings; when restricted to the ring ℤ \mathbb{Z} , each of those definitions is equivalent to those studied here. In this paper we classify all quadratic form parameters Q Q over the integers, determine the category of quadratic form parameters 𝐅𝐏 \mathbf{FP} and compute the Witt group functor, W 0 : 𝐅𝐏 → 𝐀𝐛 , Q ↦ W 0 ​ ( Q ) , W_{0}\colon\mathbf{FP}\to\mathbf{Ab},\quad Q\mapsto W_{0}(Q), where 𝐀𝐛 \mathbf{Ab} is the category of finitely generated abelian groups and W 0 ​ ( Q ) W_{0}(Q) is the Witt group of nonsingular Q Q -forms.



1.
Introduction

Quadratic refinements of
symmetric and anti-symmetric
bilinear forms are important objects in algebra and topology,
and extended quadratic forms provide a general setting for their study.
Let
X
be an abelian group
(in this paper we assume that all groups are finitely generated)
and let

\lambda\colon X\times X\to\mathbb{Z}

be a bilinear form on
X
such that
\lambda(x,y)=\epsilon_{\lambda}\lambda(y,x)
for all
x,y\in X
and some
fixed
\epsilon_{\lambda}\in\{\pm 1\}
.
To define a quadratic refinement of
\lambda

we require the additional structure of a
quadratic form parameter
.
This is a triple
Q=(Q_{e},\textup{h},\textup{p})
, often written

Q_{e}\xrightarrow{~\textup{h}~}\mathbb{Z}\xrightarrow{~\textup{p}~}Q_{e},

where
Q_{e}
is an abelian group and

h
and
p
are homomorphisms
such that

\textup{h}\textup{p}\textup{h}=2\textup{h}
and
\textup{p}\textup{h}\textup{p}=2\textup{p}
.
The equation

\textup{h}\textup{p}=(1{+}\epsilon_{Q})\textup{Id}_{\mathbb{Z}}
defines
\epsilon_{Q}\in\{\pm 1\}
,
the symmetry of
Q
, and we require
\epsilon_{Q}=\epsilon_{\lambda}
.
A quadratic refinement of
\lambda
with values in
Q
is a function
\mu\colon X\to Q_{e}
,
which for all
x,y\in X
satisfies the equations

\mu(x+y)=\mu(x)+\mu(y)+\textup{p}(\lambda(x,y))\qquad\text{and}\qquad\textup{h}(\mu(x))=\lambda(x,x).

The triple
(X,\lambda,\mu)
is a called an
extended quadratic form over
Q
, or simply a
Q
-form
.

Definitions of quadratic
form parameters
Q
and

Q
-forms, equivalent to those above for the ring
\mathbb{Z}
,
were developed independently by Baues
[
Bau1
]
, Ranicki
[
R1
]
and Schlichting
[
S
]
in far broader contexts.
We also refer the reader to
Section
1.4
for a review of the uses of
Q
-forms and their Witt groups
in geometric topology.

1.1.
Form parameters and their Witt groups

We shall usually abbreviate “quadratic form parameter” to “form parameter”.
For
k\in\mathbb{Z}^{+}\cup\{\infty\}

we have the following standard symmetric form parameters,

Q_{+}:=\Bigl(\mathbb{Z}\xrightarrow{2}\mathbb{Z}\xrightarrow{1}\mathbb{Z}\Bigr),\quad\mathbb{Z}^{P}_{k}:=\Bigl(\mathbb{Z}\oplus\mathbb{Z}_{2^{k}}\xrightarrow{(1,0)}\mathbb{Z}\xrightarrow{(2,-1)^{\rm t}}\mathbb{Z}\oplus\mathbb{Z}_{2^{k}}\Bigr),\quad\text{and}\quad Q^{+}:=\Bigl(\mathbb{Z}\xrightarrow{1}\mathbb{Z}\xrightarrow{2}\mathbb{Z}\Bigr),

(1)

where
\mathbb{Z}_{2^{\infty}}:=\mathbb{Z}
. For
k\geq 2
an integer we have the following standard anti-symmetric form parameters,

Q_{-}:=\Bigl(\mathbb{Z}_{2}\xrightarrow{0}\mathbb{Z}\xrightarrow{1}\mathbb{Z}_{2}\Bigr),\quad\mathbb{Z}^{\Lambda}_{k}:=\Bigl(\mathbb{Z}_{2^{k}}\xrightarrow{~~0~~}\mathbb{Z}\xrightarrow{2^{k-1}}\mathbb{Z}_{2^{k}}\Bigr)\quad\text{and}\quad Q^{-}:=\Bigl(0\xrightarrow{}\mathbb{Z}\xrightarrow{}0\Bigr).

(2)

In addition, given a form parameter
Q=(Q_{e},\textup{h},\textup{p})
and an abelian group
G
, we can construct a new form parameter, the sum of
Q
and
G
, which is called a
split form parameter
and is defined by

Q\oplus G:=\Bigl(Q_{e}\oplus G\xrightarrow{(\textup{h},0)}\mathbb{Z}\xrightarrow{(\textup{p},0)^{\rm t}}Q_{e}\oplus G\Bigr).

(3)

In fact, every form parameter is isomorphic to
a split form parameter with
Q
standard,
as we now explain.
A morphism

\alpha\colon P\to Q
in
\mathbf{FP}
, the category of form parameters, is a homomorphism

\alpha\colon P_{e}\to Q_{e}
such that
\textup{h}_{P}=\textup{h}_{Q}\circ\alpha
and
\textup{p}_{Q}=\alpha\circ\textup{p}_{P}
. It is an isomorphism if the map
P_{e}\to Q_{e}
is
an isomorphism.
A
splitting
of a form parameter
P
is an isomorphism
\alpha\colon P\to Q\oplus G
.
If
P
admits a splitting with
G
non-trivial, then
P
is
decomposable
; if not
P
is
indecomposable
.
A splitting
P\cong Q\oplus G
is called
maximal
if
Q
indecomposable.
In Theorem
1.4
we prove that the standard form parameters are indecomposable and that they form the complete set of indecomposable form parameters up to isomorphism.
Theorem
1.4
also proves that every form parameter
P
has a maximal splitting
P\cong Q\oplus G
, in which
Q
and
G
are well-defined up to isomorphism.

The
Witt group
of a form parameter
Q
is defined as follows.
A
Q
-form
(X,\lambda,\mu)
is

nonsingular
if
(X,\lambda)
is nonsingular
(in which case
X
is free), and it is

metabolic
if it admits a
lagrangian
; i.e. a half rank summand
L<X

such that
\lambda(L\times L)=0
and
\mu(L)=0
.
There are natural notions of isometry and orthogonal
sum of
Q
-forms (see Section
2.2
),
and two nonsingular
Q
-forms are called
Witt equivalent

if they become isometric after the addition of nonsingular metabolic
Q
-forms.
The Witt group of
Q
is the set of Witt equivalence class of nonsingular
Q
-forms under the operation of orthogonal sum:

W_{0}(Q):=\left(\bigl\{[X,\lambda,\mu]\,\big|\,\text{$(X,\lambda,\mu)$ is a nonsingular $Q$-form}\bigr\},\oplus\right)

For example, the Witt-groups of

Q_{+},Q^{+},Q_{-}
and
Q^{-}
are equal
to the
L
-groups of
\mathbb{Z}
as follows:

Q

Q_{+}

Q^{+}

Q_{-}

Q^{-}

W_{0}(Q)

L_{0}(\mathbb{Z})\cong 8\mathbb{Z}

L^{0}(\mathbb{Z})\cong\mathbb{Z}

L_{2}(\mathbb{Z})\cong\mathbb{Z}_{2}

L^{2}(\mathbb{Z})=0

If
\alpha\colon P\to Q
is a morphism of form parameters and
(X,\lambda,\mu)
is a

P
-form, then
(X,\lambda,\alpha\circ\mu)
is a
Q
-form. It follows that

\alpha
induces a homomorphism
W_{0}(\alpha)\colon W_{0}(P)\to W_{0}(Q)
,
and so
taking Witt groups defines a functor

W_{0}\colon\mathbf{FP}\to\mathbf{Ab},\quad Q\mapsto W_{0}(Q),

where
\mathbf{Ab}
is the category of finitely generated abelian groups.
The main aim of this paper is to compute these Witt-groups and the Witt-group functor,
and we achieve this in Theorems
1.1
,
1.2
and
1.5
.

Theorem 1.1
.

For
k\in\mathbb{N}\cup\{\infty\}

and
l\in\mathbb{N}
,
the Witt groups of
indecomposable
form parameters are given by

\sigma\colon W_{0}(Q_{+})\xrightarrow{\cong}8\mathbb{Z},\qquad\sigma\oplus\rho_{k}\colon W_{0}(\mathbb{Z}^{P}_{k+1})\xrightarrow{\cong}\mathbb{Z}\oplus\mathbb{Z}_{2^{k}},\qquad\sigma\colon W_{0}(Q^{+})\xrightarrow{\cong}\mathbb{Z},

c\colon W_{0}(Q_{-})\xrightarrow{\cong}\mathbb{Z}_{2},\qquad W_{0}(\mathbb{Z}^{\Lambda}_{l+2})=0\qquad\text{and}\qquad W_{0}(Q^{-})=0.

Here
\sigma
is the signature homomorphism,

\infty{+}1:=\infty
,

\rho_{k}\colon W_{0}(\mathbb{Z}^{P}_{k+1})\to\mathbb{Z}_{2^{k}}

is a homomorphism defined using the squares of certain characteristic elements
(see Definitions
4.1
and
4.2
) and
c
is the Arf invariant.

Remark.

In Theorem
4.3
we compute
W_{0}
on the full subcategory of indecomposable form parameters.

For a split form parameter
Q\oplus G
, there are obvious morphisms

i\colon Q\to Q\oplus G
and
r\colon Q\oplus G\to Q
, with
ri=\textup{Id}_{Q}
,
and this ensures that

W_{0}(Q\oplus G)=W_{0}(Q)\oplus W^{Q}_{0}(G),

(4)

where
W^{Q}_{0}(G):=\mathrm{Ker}(W_{0}(r))
is the
reduced Witt group
of
Q\oplus G
.
A morphism
\alpha\colon Q_{1}\oplus G_{1}\to Q_{2}\oplus G_{2}
between split form parameters is called
split
, if
\alpha=\alpha_{q}\oplus\alpha_{l}

for a morphism
\alpha_{q}\colon Q_{1}\to Q_{2}

and a homomorphism
\alpha_{l}\colon G_{1}\to G_{2}
,
and it is clear that the homomorphisms induced on Witt groups by split morphisms preserve the splitting in (
4
).
To compute reduced Witt groups
we will use the quadratic tensor product,
which is a covariant functor,

\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\colon\mathbf{Ab}\times\mathbf{FP}\to\mathbf{Ab},\quad(G,Q)\mapsto G\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}Q,

defined by Baues
[
Bau1
, §4]
,
and which we recall in Section
4.2
.
For example, there are natural isomorphisms

G\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}Q^{+}\to\Gamma(G)\qquad\text{and}\qquad G\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}Q_{-}\to\Lambda_{1}(G),

where
\Gamma(G)
is Whitehead’s universal quadratic functor
[
Wh
]
,
and
\Lambda_{1}(G):=(G\otimes G)/(g\otimes h+h\otimes g)
.
We review the computation of
G\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}Q
for all indecomposable form parameters

Q
in Section
4.4
and
in
Section
4.3

we define a homomorphism, called the

split norm
,
\tilde{F}\colon W_{0}(Q\oplus G)\to G\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}Q
,
which is natural in
Q
and
G
.

Theorem 1.2
.

For a split form parameter
Q\oplus G
,
the restriction of the split norm
to
W^{Q}_{0}(G)
,

\tilde{F}|_{W^{Q}_{0}(G)}\colon W^{Q}_{0}(G)\to G\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}Q,

is an isomorphism.
Hence the map

H:=(W_{0}(r),\tilde{F})\colon W_{0}(Q\oplus G)\to W_{0}(Q)\oplus(G\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}Q)

is an isomorphism,
natural in
Q
and
G
,
such that for any split morphism

\alpha=\alpha_{q}\oplus\alpha_{l}\colon Q_{1}\oplus G_{1}\to Q_{2}\oplus G_{2}
we have

H\circ W_{0}(\alpha)\circ H^{-1}=W_{0}(\alpha_{q})\oplus(\alpha_{l}\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\alpha_{q})\colon W_{0}(Q_{1})\oplus(G_{1}\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}Q_{1})\to W_{0}(Q_{2})\oplus(G_{2}\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}Q_{2}).

For an arbitrary form parameter
P
, Theorems
1.1
and
1.2
provide an effective procedure to compute the Witt-group
W_{0}(P)
by choosing a maximal splitting
P\cong Q\oplus G
. The details of this procedure are given in Section
4.4
.

To understand
the Witt group functor on all morphisms,
we first classify the category
\mathbf{FP}
.
If there is a morphism
\alpha\colon P\to Q
,
then
\epsilon_{P}=\epsilon_{Q}
and so
\mathbf{FP}
splits as the disjoint union of

\mathbf{FP}_{+}
and
\mathbf{FP}_{-}
, the categories of symmetric and anti-symmetric form parameters.
We will show that each of
\mathbf{FP}_{+}
and
\mathbf{FP}_{-}
is equivalent to a simpler category. The
linearisation
of a form parameter
Q
is the abelian group
SQ:=Q_{e}/\textup{p}(\mathbb{Z})
, and when
Q
is symmetric, we consider the homomorphism induced by
\textup{h}\colon Q_{e}\to\mathbb{Z}
,

v_{Q}\colon SQ\to\mathbb{Z}_{2},\quad[q]\mapsto\textup{h}(q)~\mathrm{mod}~2.

When
Q
is anti-symmetric,
p
induces a homomorphism

v^{\prime}_{Q}\colon\mathbb{Z}_{2}\to Q_{e},\quad[n]\mapsto\textup{p}(n).

We call the homomorphism
v_{Q}
or
v^{\prime}_{Q}
the
quasi-Wu class
of
Q
;
this terminology is justified by Proposition
7.1
.
Let
\mathbf{Ab}/\mathbb{Z}_{2}
and
\mathbb{Z}_{2}/\mathbf{Ab}
denote the categories of abelian groups with homomorphisms to and from
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
, respectively. A morphism
\alpha\colon P\to Q
of symmetric form parameters induces a homomorphism
S\alpha\colon SP\to SQ
such that
v_{P}=v_{Q}\circ S\alpha
, and if
\alpha\colon P\to Q
is a morphism of anti-symmetric form parameters, then
v^{\prime}_{Q}=\alpha\circ v^{\prime}_{P}
, showing that the quasi-Wu classes are functorial. We have the following

Theorem 1.3
.

The functors

v_{(-)}\colon\mathbf{FP}_{+}\to\mathbf{Ab}/\mathbb{Z}_{2},\quad Q\mapsto v_{Q}\qquad\text{and}\qquad v^{\prime}_{(-)}\colon\mathbf{FP}_{-}\to\mathbb{Z}_{2}/\mathbf{Ab},\quad Q\mapsto v^{\prime}_{Q}

are equivalences of categories.

By combining Theorem
1.3
with the classification of the categories
\mathbf{Ab}/\mathbb{Z}_{2}
and
\mathbb{Z}_{2}/\mathbf{Ab}
, we obtain an explicit classification of form parameters.

Theorem 1.4
.

a) The standard form parameters in (
1
) and (
2
) are indecomposable and pairwise non-isomorphic. Any indecomposable form parameter is isomorphic to one of them.

b) Every form parameter
P
has a maximal splitting
P\cong Q\oplus G
. Moreover,
Q
and
G
are each well-defined up to isomorphism.

To describe the functor
W_{0}
completely for general form parameters and morphisms,
we define the
extended symmetrisation
and
extended quadratic lift
morphisms

\textup{es}\colon P\to Q^{+}\oplus SP\qquad\text{and}\qquad\textup{eql}\colon Q_{-}\oplus P_{e}\to P,

for symmetric and anti-symmetric form parameters
P
respectively.
Applying Theorem
1.3
,
we note that for
\epsilon=\pm 1
,
the form parameter
Q_{\epsilon}
is an initial object in
\mathbf{FP}_{\epsilon}
, while
Q^{\epsilon}
is terminal.
So for any
\epsilon
-symmetric form parameter
Q
there are unique morphisms

Q_{\epsilon}\xrightarrow{\textup{i}}Q\xrightarrow{\textup{s}}Q^{\epsilon},

where
\textup{s}\circ\textup{i}\colon Q_{\epsilon}\to Q^{\epsilon}
is the canonical morphism.
Then
\textup{es}\colon P\to Q^{+}\oplus SP
is defined as the sum of
s
and the linearisation map
P_{e}\to SP
and
\textup{eql}\colon Q_{-}\oplus P_{e}\to P
is the sum of
i
and the identity
P_{e}\to P_{e}
; see Definitions
2.14
and
2.15
.

By Theorem
1.2
there are natural isomorphisms
W_{0}(Q^{+}\oplus SP)\cong\mathbb{Z}\oplus\Gamma(SP)
and
W_{0}(Q_{-}\oplus P_{e})\cong\mathbb{Z}_{2}\oplus\Lambda_{1}(P_{e})
, and we will fix these identifications. We will show that
W_{0}(\textup{es})
is always injective and
W_{0}(\textup{eql})
is always surjective
(see Lemmas
5.2
and
5.12
). We will then get a natural description of
W_{0}(P)
by identifying the image of
W_{0}(\textup{es})
or the kernel of
W_{0}(\textup{eql})
as a natural subgroup of
\mathbb{Z}\oplus\Gamma(SP)
or
\mathbb{Z}_{2}\oplus\Lambda_{1}(P_{e})
. For this we require some additional definitions.
Given an abelian group
A
and a homomorphism
v\colon A\to\mathbb{Z}_{2}
, we define (see Definition
5.3
) the subgroup

\Sigma(v):=\left<(1,x\otimes 1),(0,[k_{1},k_{2}]\otimes 1),(8,0)\bigm|x\in v^{-1}(1),k_{1},k_{2}\in\mathrm{Ker}(v)\right>\leq\mathbb{Z}\oplus\Gamma(A).

Given a homomorphism
v^{\prime}\colon\mathbb{Z}_{2}\to A
, we define (see Definition
5.14
) the subgroup

K(v^{\prime}):=\bigl\langle{(1,v^{\prime}(1)\mathop{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{}_{\wedge}}}{{\otimes}}}\nolimits v^{\prime}(1)),(0,x\mathop{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{}_{\wedge}}}{{\otimes}}}\nolimits x+x\mathop{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{}_{\wedge}}}{{\otimes}}}\nolimits v^{\prime}(1))\mid x\in A}\bigr\rangle\leq\mathbb{Z}_{2}\oplus\Lambda_{1}(A)

and the quotient group

\Lambda(v^{\prime}):=(\mathbb{Z}_{2}\oplus\Lambda_{1}(A))/K(v^{\prime}).

It is elementary to check that the assignments
v\mapsto\Sigma(v)
,
v^{\prime}\mapsto K(v^{\prime})
and
v^{\prime}\mapsto\Lambda(v^{\prime})
define functors
\Sigma\colon\mathbf{Ab}/\mathbb{Z}_{2}\rightarrow\mathbf{Ab}
and
\Lambda\colon\mathbb{Z}_{2}/\mathbf{Ab}\rightarrow\mathbf{Ab}
. We will also include more explicit descriptions of these groups, see the discussion after Theorems
5.8
and
5.20
.
Our next result gives a natural identification of the Witt group functor (see Corollaries
5.6
and
5.18
).

Theorem 1.5
.

There are natural isomorphisms, induced by
W_{0}(\textup{es})
and
W_{0}(\textup{eql})
respectively:

\displaystyle W_{0}\big|_{\mathbf{FP}_{+}}

\displaystyle\cong\Sigma\circ v_{(-)}\colon\mathbf{FP}_{+}\to\mathbf{Ab}

\displaystyle W_{0}\big|_{\mathbf{FP}_{-}}

\displaystyle\cong\Lambda\circ v^{\prime}_{(-)}\colon\mathbf{FP}_{-}\to\mathbf{Ab}

Theorem
1.5
allows us to determine
the induced map
W_{0}(\alpha)
for
any morphism of form parameters
\alpha
.

Corollary 1.6
.

a) For any symmetric form parameter
P
,

W_{0}(\textup{es})\colon W_{0}(P)\to\mathbb{Z}\oplus\Gamma(SP)
is injective with image
\Sigma(v_{P})
,
and so for any morphism
\alpha\colon P_{1}\to P_{2}
of symmetric form parameters there is a commutative diagram

\textstyle{~~~~W_{0}(P_{1})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\cong}
\scriptstyle{W_{0}(\textup{es})}
\scriptstyle{W_{0}(\alpha)}
\textstyle{\Sigma(v_{P_{1}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\Sigma(S\alpha)}
\textstyle{\mathbb{Z}\oplus\Gamma(SP_{1})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\textup{Id}\oplus\Gamma(S\alpha)}
\textstyle{~~~~W_{0}(P_{2})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\cong}
\scriptstyle{W_{0}(\textup{es})}
\textstyle{\Sigma(v_{P_{2}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathbb{Z}\oplus\Gamma(SP_{2}).}

b) For any anti-symmetric form parameter
P
,
W_{0}(\textup{eql})\colon\mathbb{Z}_{2}\oplus\Lambda_{1}(P_{e})\to W_{0}(P)

is surjective with kernel
K(v^{\prime}_{P})
,
hence there is an induced isomorphism
W_{0}(\textup{eql})\colon\Lambda(v^{\prime}_{P})\to W_{0}(P)
.
So for any morphism

\alpha\colon P_{1}\to P_{2}
of anti-symmetric form parameters,
there is a commutative diagram

\textstyle{\mathbb{Z}_{2}\oplus\Lambda_{1}(P_{1e})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
