\scriptstyle{W_{0}(\textup{eql})}
\textstyle{\Lambda(v^{\prime}_{P_{1}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\Lambda(\alpha)}
\scriptstyle{\cong}
\textstyle{W_{0}(P_{1})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{W_{0}(\alpha)}
\textstyle{\mathbb{Z}_{2}\oplus\Lambda_{1}(P_{2e})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{W_{0}(\textup{eql})}
\textstyle{\Lambda(v^{\prime}_{P_{2}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\cong}
\textstyle{W_{0}(P_{2}).}

1.2.
Grothendieck-Witt groups and absorbing forms

The study of Witt groups of extended quadratic forms has recently received impetus from the breakthrough work of Calmés, Dotto, Harpaz, Hebestreit, Land, Moi, Nardin, Nikolaus and Steimle on Grothendieck-Witt groups, which defines spaces and spectra in a very general setting whose group of connected components are Witt groups of certain form parameters
[
C et al.
]
.

Grothendieck-Witt groups

The Grothendieck-Witt group
GW_{0}(Q)
of a form parameter
Q
can be defined prior to the Witt group
W_{0}(Q)
.
Let
\underline{\mu}=(X,\lambda,\mu)
be a nonsingular
Q
-form,

\{\underline{\mu}\}
denote the isometry class of
\underline{\mu}
and
\mathrm{Hom}^{\rm ns}_{\mathbb{Z}}(Q):=\big\{\{\underline{\mu}\}\big\}
be the set of isometry classes of nonsingular
Q
-forms, which is a commutative monoid under the operation of orthogonal sum.
The Grothendieck-Witt group of
Q
is the Grothendieck group of this monoid,

GW_{0}(Q):={\mathcal{G}r}(\mathrm{Hom}^{\rm ns}_{\mathbb{Z}}(Q),\oplus),

and there is a natural surjective homomorphism

\pi\colon GW_{0}(Q)\to W_{0}(Q),\quad(\{\underline{\mu}_{0}\},\{\underline{\mu}_{1}\})\mapsto[\underline{\mu}_{0}]-[\underline{\mu}_{1}].

Taking the rank of the underlying abelian group of a
Q
-form defines a homomorphism

\textup{rk}\colon GW_{0}(Q)\to\mathbb{Z}
,
and we let
\sigma\colon W_{0}(Q)\to\mathbb{Z}
denote signature homomorphism
(by convention,
\sigma=0
if
Q
is anti-symmetric),

\varrho_{2}\colon\mathbb{Z}\to\mathbb{Z}_{2}

denote reduction mod
2
and set
\sigma_{2}:=\varrho_{2}\circ\sigma
.

Theorem 1.7
.

For all form parameters
Q
, there is a short exact sequence

0\to GW_{0}(Q)\xrightarrow{\textup{rk}\oplus\pi}\mathbb{Z}\oplus W_{0}(Q)\xrightarrow{(\varrho_{2},\sigma_{2})}\mathbb{Z}_{2}\to 0,

the surjection
\pi\colon GW_{0}(Q)\to W_{0}(Q)
is split and there is an isomorphism

GW_{0}(Q)\cong 2\mathbb{Z}\oplus W_{0}(Q),

where
\textup{rk}|_{\ker(\pi)}\colon\ker(\pi)\to 2\mathbb{Z}
is also an isomorphism.
Moreover, the splitting

GW_{0}(Q)\cong 2\mathbb{Z}\oplus W_{0}(Q)
is natural
on
\mathbf{FP}_{-}
and on the full sub-category of
\mathbf{FP}_{+}
consisting of symmetric form
parameters
Q
with
v_{Q}=0
.

Absorbing forms

Given an nonsingular
Q
-form
\underline{\mu}=(X,\lambda,\mu)
, the group of automorphisms of
\underline{\mu}
,

\textup{Aut}(\underline{\mu})=\{A\in\textup{Aut}(X)\mid\lambda=\lambda\circ(A\times A)~\text{and}~\mu=\mu\circ A\},

is an important object of study in algebra and topology.
For
k\underline{\mu}:=\underline{\mu}\oplus\dots\oplus\underline{\mu}
, the
k
-fold orthogonal sum of
\underline{\mu}
with itself, we often know more about
\textup{Aut}(k\underline{\mu})
and

\lim_{k\to\infty}\textup{Aut}(k\underline{\mu})
than we do about
\textup{Aut}(\underline{\mu})
, and for this and other reasons,
it can be useful
to know whether a nonsingular
Q
-form
\underline{\mu}
is

absorbing
, which means that
for every nonsingular
Q
-form
\underline{\mu}^{\prime}
,
there is a primitive embedding

\underline{\mu}^{\prime}\hookrightarrow k\underline{\mu}

for some positive integer
k
.
In this case there is an isomorphism
k\underline{\mu}\cong\underline{\mu}^{\prime}\oplus\underline{\mu}^{{}^{\prime}\perp}
,
where
\underline{\mu}^{{}^{\prime}\perp}
is the orthogonal complement to an embedding of
\underline{\mu}^{\prime}\hookrightarrow k\underline{\mu}
,
and so an injection
\textup{Aut}(\underline{\mu}^{\prime})\to\textup{Aut}(k\underline{\mu})
.
To determine whether a
Q
-form
\underline{\mu}=(X,\lambda,\mu)
is absorbing we need two properties of
Q
-forms.
We say that
\underline{\mu}
is
indefinite
if
(X,\lambda)
is indefinite
and that
\underline{\mu}
is
full
if
S\mu\colon X\to SQ
is onto.

Theorem 1.8
.

For any form parameter
Q
, a nonsingular
Q
-form
\underline{\mu}
is absorbing if and only if
\underline{\mu}
is indefinite and full.

Remark 1.9
.

If a
Q
-form
\underline{\mu}=(X,\lambda,\mu)
is not full, then there is a unique form parameter
Q^{\prime}\subset Q
with
linearisation
SQ^{\prime}=(S\mu)(X)
and such
that
\underline{\mu}
lifts uniquely to a full
Q^{\prime}
-form.
To define
Q^{\prime}
, consider the surjection

Q_{e}\to SQ
and the subgroup
(S\mu)(X)\subset SQ
,
and let
Q^{\prime}_{e}
be the pre-image of

(S\mu)(X)
.
Then
\textup{p}_{Q}(\mathbb{Z})\subseteq Q^{\prime}_{e}
and
Q^{\prime}:=(Q^{\prime}_{e},\textup{h}_{Q}|_{Q^{\prime}_{e}},\textup{p}_{Q})
is
the desired form parameter.

1.3.
Algebraic Poincaré cobordism

Witt groups of extended quadratic forms appear in Ranicki’s theory of algebraic surgery
[
R1
]
, as we now quickly recall.
A chain bundle
(B_{*},\beta)
over
\mathbb{Z}
is a chain complex
B_{*}
of free abelian groups, which is bounded below, together with a degree
0
hyper-quadratic structure
\beta
on the dual of
B_{*}
.
Chain bundles were introduced by Weiss
[
We
]
as algebraic models of stable vector bundles, or more generally, stable spherical fibrations.
Some symmetric Poincaré complexes can
be enriched with
(B_{*},\beta)
-structures and the bordism group of such complexes
is the
\beta
-twisted
L
-group
L^{n}(B_{*},\beta)
.
For each natural number
q
, a chain bundle

(B_{*},\beta)
defines a
(-1)^{q}
-symmetric form parameter
Q_{\beta}(q)
with

SQ_{\beta}(q)=H_{q}(B_{*})

[
R1
, §10]
,
and by
[
R1
, §9]
there is a natural homomorphism

\eta_{\beta}\colon W_{0}(Q_{\beta}(q))\to L^{2q}(B_{*},\beta).

(5)

In Conjecture
7.6
, we propose that
\eta_{\beta}
is split injective if
H_{q-1}(B_{*})=0
, and
an isomorphism if
H_{i}(B_{*})=0
for
i<q
.

In Proposition
7.1
we show that the algebraic Wu class
\widehat{v}_{2k}(\beta)\in H^{2k}(B_{*};\mathbb{Z}_{2})
of
(B_{*},\beta)
(defined by Ranicki in
e.g.
[
R1
, §4]
), determines the quasi-Wu classes of both
Q_{\beta}(2k)
and
Q_{\beta}(2k{-}1)
.
Applying Theorem
1.3
, it follows that
\widehat{v}_{2k}(\beta)
determines the isomorphism classes of the form parameters
Q_{\beta}(2k)
and
Q_{\beta}(2k{-}1)
,
and so the isomorphism classes of the Witt groups
W_{0}(Q_{\beta}(2k))
and

W_{0}(Q_{\beta}(2k{-}1))
.
Hence Conjecture
7.6
and Theorem
1.5
give a conjectural calculation of

L^{2q}(B_{*},\beta)
when
H_{i}(B_{*})=0
for all
i<q
.

1.4.
Q
-forms and the classification of simply-connected manifolds

Q
-forms appeared at the very start of the modern classification of manifolds in Wall’s paper classifying
(q{-}1)
-connected
2q
-dimensional manifolds with connected boundary for
q\geq 3

[
Wa1
]
.
In this subsection we give an historical and selective overview
of the use of
Q
-forms in the classification of simply-connected oriented manifolds.

The classification of
(q{-}1)
-connected
2q
-manifolds for
q\geq 3

We follow Baues
[
Bau2
, §8]
in stating
Wall’s main classification result from
[
Wa1
]
by first defining the form parameter

\pi_{q-1}\{SO_{q}\}:=\bigl(\pi_{q-1}(SO_{q})\xrightarrow{~\textup{h}~}\mathbb{Z}\xrightarrow{~\textup{p}~}\pi_{q-1}(SO_{q})\bigr).

Here
\textup{h}(\gamma)\in\mathbb{Z}=H^{q}(S^{q})
is the Euler class of an oriented rank
q
vector bundle over
S^{q}

whose clutching function is in the homotopy class
\gamma
, and
\textup{p}(1)=\tau_{q}
, the homotopy class of the clutching function
of the tangent bundle of the
q
-sphere.
Now let
W
be a compact, oriented,
(q{-}1)
-connected
2q
-manifold with connected
boundary
\partial W
and
q
th integral
homology group
H_{q}(W)
.
The intersection form of
W
is a
(-1)^{q}
-symmetric bilinear form

\lambda_{W}\colon H_{q}(W)\times H_{q}(W)\to\mathbb{Z}.

Applying results of Haefliger
[
H
]
, Wall
[
Wa1
]
proved
that
there is a function

\mu_{W}\colon H_{q}(W)\to\pi_{q-1}(SO_{q})

such that the triple

\underline{\mu}_{W}:=(H_{q}(W),\lambda_{W},\mu_{W})
is a
\pi_{q-1}\{SO_{q}\}
-form,
and that
the diffeomorphism classes of
(q{-}1)
-connected
2q
-manifolds with connected boundaries are in bijection with the isomorphism classes of their
\pi_{q-1}\{SO_{q}\}
-forms.
(When
q=2
the topological situation is vastly more complicated,
but the algebra is as simple as possible since
\pi_{1}\{SO_{2}\}=Q^{+}
and so
\pi_{1}\{SO_{2}\}
-forms are
nothing but symmetric bilinear forms.)

The Witt groups and Grothendieck-Witt groups of
\pi_{q-1}\{SO_{q}\}
-forms appear when

\partial W
is a homotopy sphere, for this is equivalent to
\underline{\mu}_{W}
being nonsingular.
Wall
[
Wa1
, Theorem 2]
computed the Grothendieck-Witt groups of

\pi_{q-1}\{SO_{q}\}
-forms and implicitly also their Witt groups, bearing
Theorem
1.7
in mind.
Moreover,
[
Wa1
, Theorem 3]
states that the diffeomorphism class of
\partial W
only depends on the Witt class of
\underline{\mu}_{W}
in
W_{0}\bigl(\pi_{q-1}\{SO_{q}\}\bigr)
, and Wall proved that there is a well-defined homomorphism

\partial_{2q}\colon W_{0}\bigl(\pi_{q-1}\{SO_{q}\}\bigr)\to\Theta_{2q-1},

where
\Theta_{2q-1}
denotes the group of homotopy
(2q{-}1)
-spheres and
\partial_{2q}

maps
[X,\lambda,\mu]
the diffeomorphism class of the homotopy sphere
\Sigma
with
\Sigma=\partial W
and

[H_{q}(W),\lambda_{W},\mu_{W}]=[X,\lambda,\mu]

(the homomorphism
\partial_{2q}
appears explicitly in
[
Wa2
, §17]
).
Wall
[
Wa1
, Theorem 4]

initiated the study of the homomorphism
\partial_{2q}
, which is a very delicate problem with a long history.
For example, when
q=2^{k}{-}1
and
k\geq 2
, computing
\partial_{2q}
requires solving the Kervaire-Invariant-One problem in dimension

2^{k+1}{-}2
.
Following Wall, many authors worked on the homomorphism
\partial_{2q}
, with this effort recently culminating in a papers of Burklund and Senger
[
B-S
]
, which computes
\partial_{2q}
, except for the case of
\partial_{126}
,
and Lin, Wang and Xu
[
L-W-X
]
, gives that

\partial_{126}=0
.

If
\partial_{2q}([\underline{\mu}_{M}])=0
, then there are diffeomorphisms
f\colon\partial W\cong S^{2q-1}
,
W
can be completed to a closed
(q{-}1)
-connected
2q
-manifold
M=W\cup_{f}D^{2q}
, and Wall
[
Wa1
]
also proved that such manifolds are classified up to the action of
\Theta_{2q}
via connected sum by their
\pi_{q-1}\{SO_{q}\}
-forms. This reduced the classification of closed
(q{-}1)
-connected
2q
-manifolds to the determination of their inertia groups, a task which was recently finally completed by Senger and Zhang
[
S-Z
]
.

Classical, modified and extended surgery

Wall’s classification of
(q{-}1)
-connected
2q
-manifolds was one of the precursors to
the development of the surgery classification of manifolds in general.
A key idea in surgery is to study normal maps from manifolds, not just manifolds
on their own. Let
\xi\colon B\to BO
be a fibration, where
B
is homotopy equivalent to a simply-connected
CW
-complex. In what follows, we shall abuse notation and also use
B
to denote one of
its
CW
-approximations.
For
M
a compact smooth manifold,
a
normal map
from
M
over
(B,\xi)
,
denoted
\overline{\nu}\colon M\to B
, is a map of stable vector bundles from the stable normal bundle of
M
to
\xi
.
An important example of the above comes from classical surgery,
when
B=X
is a
2q
-dimensional Poincaré complex and the normal map
\overline{\nu}
has degree one and is
q
-connected.
Then for
\epsilon=(-1)^{q}
,
Browder
[
Browd2
, Ch. III]
proved that the surgery kernel

K_{q}(\overline{\nu}):=H_{q+1}(X,M)

supports a nonsingular
Q_{\epsilon}
-form
(K_{q}(\overline{\nu}),\lambda,\mu_{\overline{\nu}})
,
where we use
\overline{\nu}
to regard
M
as a subspace of
X
and
\lambda
is induced by the intersection form of
M
.
Moreover, by
[
Browd2
, Theorem 2.1 Ch. IV]
for
q\geq 3
the normal map
(M,\overline{\nu})
is
(X,\xi)
-bordant to a normal homotopy equivalence if and only if

[K_{q}(\overline{\nu}),\lambda,\mu_{\overline{\nu}}]=0\in W_{0}(Q_{\epsilon})=L_{1-\epsilon}(\mathbb{Z}).

In the more general setting of modified surgery,
one seeks to classify
q
-connected
2q
-dimensional normal maps

\overline{\nu}\colon M\to B
, which are called
normal
(q{-}1)
-smoothings
. An important result of
Kreck
[
K
, Corollary 3]
states that
(B,\xi)
-bordant normal

(q{-}1)
-smoothings are stably diffeomorphic, where a stable diffeomorphism between two
2q
-manifolds
M_{0}
and
M_{1}
with the same Euler characteristic is a diffeomorphism
M_{0}\sharp_{r}(S^{q}\times S^{q})\to M_{1}\sharp_{r}(S^{q}\times S^{q})
.
Kreck also proved that cancellation of any
(S^{q}\times S^{q})
-summands is always possible when
q
is odd and also when
q
is even,
provided the genus of either manifold is at least 1,

[
K
, Theorem 5]
.
Here cancellation is the phenomenon where the existence of a diffeomorphism
M_{0}\sharp_{r}(S^{q}\times S^{q})\to M_{1}\sharp_{r}(S^{q}\times S^{q})
implies the existence of a diffeomorphism
M_{0}\to M_{1}
,
and the
genus
of an oriented
2q
-manifold
M
, as defined by Galatius and Randal-Williams
[
G-RW
, Definition 3.1]
, is the largest natural number
r
such that there is a diffeomorphism
M\to N\sharp_{r}(S^{q}\times S^{q})
, for some other oriented
2q
-manifold
N
.
The above demonstrates the importance of finding invariants which can distinguish stably-diffeomorphic
4k
-manifolds of genus zero.
While Kreck
[
K
, Proposition 6]
generalised Browder’s result above by showing that a
Q
-form
(K_{q}(\overline{\nu}),\lambda,\mu_{\overline{\nu}})
is defined on
K_{q}(\overline{\nu})
, where
Q=Q_{\epsilon}
if
v_{q+1}(\pi_{q+1}(B))=0
and
Q=Q^{-}
if
v_{q+1}(\pi_{q+1}(B))\neq 0
,
when
B
is not a
2q
-dimensional Poincaré complex,
the
Q
-form

(K_{q}(\overline{\nu}),\lambda,\mu_{\overline{\nu}})
may be singular and this can significantly complicate the algebra of surgery obstructions; see
[
K
, §5]
.

One approach to handling the more complicated algebra associated to normal
(q{-}1)
-smoothings
\overline{\nu}\colon M\to B
is via the theory of chain bundles, which provides more general form parameters
Q
and
Q
-forms defined on
H_{q}(M)
.
Specifically, if we
assume that
M
admits a
CW
-structure such that
\overline{\nu}\colon M\to B
is cellular, then the stable vector bundle
(B,\xi)
defines a chain bundle
(B_{*},\beta)
over
C_{*}(B)
, the cellular chain complex of
B
,
and the normal map
\overline{\nu}\colon M\to B
equips
C_{*}(M)
, the cellular chain complex of
M
, with a normal
(B_{*},\beta)
-structure by
[
We
, Theorem 3.4]
.
Moreover, as we saw in Section
1.3
, the chain bundle
(B_{*},\beta)
defines a form parameter
Q_{\beta}(q)
and Ranicki shows
[
R1
, §10 p. 250]
how the normal
(B_{*},\beta)
-structure on
C_{*}(M)

defines a
Q_{\beta}(q)
-form

\underline{\mu}_{\overline{\nu}}:=(H_{q}(M),\lambda_{M},\mu_{\overline{\nu}})

on
H_{q}(M)

1
1

1

Ranicki assumes that
H_{q}(M)
is torsion free, but this is to ensure the
Q
-form is nonsingular, and is not used in the definition.
,
which is thus a
(B,\xi)
-diffeomorphism invariant of
\overline{\nu}\colon M\to B
.
In
[
N2
, Theorem 1.2]
the second author proves that when
q=2k\geq 4
is even, any
(B,\xi)
-bordism between
4k
-dimensional
(2k{-}1)
-smoothings with isomorphic
Q_{\beta}(q)
-forms is
(B,\xi)
-bordant to a
(B,\xi)
-h-cobordism,
and so for
k\geq 2
,
4k
-dimensional

(B,\xi)
-bordant
(2k{-}1)
-smoothings with isomorphic
Q_{\beta}(q)
-forms are diffeomorphic.
In
[
CCPS
]
for
k\geq 2
,
Q_{\beta}(q)
-forms are used to distinguish
4k
-manifolds with the same Euler characteristic,
which are stably diffeomorphic but not diffeomorphic.

1.5.
A conjecture on cancellation for
Q
-forms

In this subsection we consider the role of the Witt group
W_{0}(Q)
in the classification of full nonsingular
Q
-forms.
Recall that a
Q
-form
\underline{\mu}=(X,\lambda,\mu)
is full if its linearisation
S\mu\colon X\to SQ
is surjective.
We regard full
Q
-forms as algebraic analogues of closed
2q
-dimensional normal
(q{-}1)
-smoothings and
W_{0}(Q)
as an analogue of the corresponding bordism group of closed normal
(q{-}1)
-smoothings.
Hence we define the genus of a nonsingular full
Q
-form
\underline{\mu}
to be the largest natural number
r
such that there is an isomorphism

\underline{\mu}\to\underline{\mu}^{\prime}\oplus H_{\epsilon_{Q}}(\mathbb{Z}^{r})
,
where
\underline{\mu}^{\prime}
is a nonsingular full
Q
-form
and
H_{\epsilon_{Q}}(\mathbb{Z}^{r})
is the standard hyperbolic form
on
\mathbb{Z}^{2r}
; see Example
2.21
.
In the light of Kreck’s stable diffeomorphism and cancellation
theorems
[
K
, Corollary 3 & Theorem 5]
reviewed in Section
1.4
above,
we make the following

Conjecture 1.10
.

Let
\underline{\mu}_{0}
and
\underline{\mu}_{1}
be nonsingular full
Q
-forms of the same rank, such that
[\underline{\mu}_{0}]=[\underline{\mu}_{1}]\in W_{0}(Q)
. Then

a)

For some integer non-negative integer
m
,
there is an isomorphism

\underline{\mu}_{0}\oplus H_{\epsilon_{Q}}(\mathbb{Z}^{m})\to\underline{\mu}_{1}\oplus H_{\epsilon_{Q}}(\mathbb{Z}^{m})
;

b)

If
\epsilon_{Q}=1
and
\mathrm{g}(\underline{\mu}_{0})\geq 1
,
or if
\epsilon_{Q}=-1
, then then
\underline{\mu}_{0}
and
\underline{\mu}_{1}
are isomorphic.

Remark 1.11
.

Conjecture
1.10
is well-known for the classical form parameters
Q_{\pm}
and
Q^{\pm}
; see e.g.
[
M-H
, Theorem 5.3]
for the symmetric case and
[
L-M
, Theorem 8.104]
for the anti-symmetric case. For metabolic forms over general form parameters, Part a) of Conjecture
1.10
is proven in Lemma
6.4
.

1.6.
Form parameters over more general rings

We hope that the results of this paper may be a source of ideas for future work over more general rings.
The theory of form parameters over group rings
\mathbb{Z}[\pi]
and more generally rings with involution goes back to Bak
[
Bak
]
in special cases, and general theories were developed by Baues
[
Bau1
]
and Ranicki
[
R1
]
and Schlichting
[
S
]
.
For applications to non-simply-connected manifolds, one would like to develop the theory of form parameters
Q
over group rings
\mathbb{Z}[\pi]
and of
Q
-forms defined on
\mathbb{Z}[\pi]
-modules. In particular, it seems likely that “quasi-Wu-classes” can be defined more generally, and that an appropriate analogue of Theorem
1.3
holds.
In this direction, Wolfgang Steimle informs us that
results in Sections 1 & 4 of
[
C et al.
]
may
give another proof of Theorem
1.3

and also analogous results for other rings.
We also expect that analysing form parameters over general rings via indecomposable and linear parts will be a useful point of view for computing their Witt groups, but it seems likely that splittings
P\cong Q\oplus G
will be replaced by short exact sequences
Q\to P\to G
.

Organisation

The rest of this paper is organised as follows.
In Section
2
we present basic definitions and examples of form parameters,
Q
-forms and Witt groups.
In Section
3
we classify
form parameters via their quasi-Wu classes, proving Theorems
1.3
and
1.4
. In Section
4
we determine the Witt groups of indecomposable and split form parameters, proving
Theorems
1.1
and
1.2
,
and in Section
5
we give a natural computation of Witt groups, proving Theorem
1.5
.
In Section
6
we turn our attention to Grothendieck-Witt groups of form parameters, and prove
Theorems
1.7
and
1.8
.
Finally, in Section
7
we discuss Witt groups of form parameters in the context of Algebraic surgery over chain bundles.

2.
Preliminaries

In this section we establish basic definitions and results for form parameters and extended quadratic forms and their Witt groups. We also give
examples to illustrate these ideas. For ease of exposition, we will repeat some definitions from the Introduction.
We defer similar preliminaries for Grothendieck-Witt groups to Section
6
, where Grothendieck-Witt groups are treated separately.

2.1.
Quadratic form parameters

Quadratic form parameters provide the structure needed to define the quadratic refinements
in extended quadratic forms.
The material in this subsection is adapted from, and builds on, Baues
[
Bau1
, §2 & §5]
,
[
Bau2
, §8]
and Ranicki
[
R1
, §10]
.

2.1.1.
Definitions and examples

We begin directly with the definition of a quadratic form parameter, discuss it, define morphisms of form parameters and give some
important examples.

Definition 2.1
.

An
oriented quadratic form parameter

Q=(Q_{e},{\rm h},{\rm p})
(over the ground ring
\mathbb{Z}
) is a (finitely generated) abelian group
Q_{e}
together with two homomorphisms
{\rm h}
and
{\rm p}
,

Q_{e}\xrightarrow{~\rm h~}\mathbb{Z}\xrightarrow{~\rm p~}Q_{e}\text{\,,}

such that

{\rm hph}=2{\rm h}\quad\text{~and~}\quad{\rm php}=2{\rm p}.

The
symmetry
of
Q
,
\epsilon_{Q}\in\{\pm 1\}
, is determined by the equation
{\rm hp}-{\rm Id}_{\mathbb{Z}}=\epsilon_{Q}{\rm Id}_{\mathbb{Z}}
.
(To see that
\textup{h}\textup{p}(1)=0
or
2
,
note that if
\textup{h}\textup{p}(1)=d\neq 0
, then
p(1)\in Q_{e}
much have infinite order and the equation
\textup{p}\textup{h}\textup{p}(1)=2\textup{p}(1)=d\textup{p}(1)
ensures that
d=2
.) If
\epsilon_{Q}=1
we call
Q

symmetric
and if
\epsilon_{Q}=-1
we call
Q

anti-symmetric
.

Remark 2.2
.

An oriented quadratic form parameter
Q
is a special case of a quadratic module as defined by Baues
[
Bau1
, §2]
, with
Q_{ee}=\mathbb{Z}
:
Q=(Q_{e},\mathbb{Z},{\rm h},{\rm p})=(Q_{e},Q_{ee},{\rm h},{\rm p})
. Henceforth we shall not explicitly mention
{\rm h}
and
{\rm p}
, the ground ring
\mathbb{Z}
or the orientation and so we use the letter
Q
to refer to an oriented quadratic form parameter over
\mathbb{Z}
, which we now simply call “a form parameter”. We have used the term “oriented” since we use the based abelian group
\mathbb{Z}
and not simply an infinite cyclic group.

Remark 2.3
.

An oriented quadratic form parameter
Q
is a special case of a form parameter for the ring
\mathbb{Z}
with the trivial involution, as defined by
Schlichting
[
S
, Definition 3.3]
.
In Schlichting’s notation
(I,\sigma)=(\mathbb{Z},\epsilon_{Q}),\Lambda=Q,\tau=\textup{p},\rho=\textup{h}
, and we discuss the quadratic action of
\mathbb{Z}
on
Q
in Section
2.1.5
.

We gave a list of standard form parameters in (
1
) and (
2
) and we give a classification of all form parameters in Section
3
. Next we mention some examples of form parameters from topology and algebraic surgery.

Example 2.4
.

Let
SO_{q}
be the special orthogonal group on
\mathbb{R}^{q}
, so that
\pi_{q-1}(SO_{q})
classifies isomorphism classes of oriented rank
q
vector bundles over
S^{q}
. For
\xi\in\pi_{q-1}(SO_{q})
, let
V_{\xi}\to S^{q}
be a corresponding oriented vector bundle and let
\tau_{q}\in\pi_{q-1}(SO_{q})
denote the homotopy class of the clutching function of the tangent bundle of the
q
-sphere. We fix an orientation on
S^{q}
and following Baues
[
Bau2
, (8.6)]
we define the
form parameter

\pi_{q-1}\{SO_{q}\}:=\bigl(\pi_{q-1}(SO_{q}),{\rm h},{\rm p}\bigr),

where
{\rm h}(\xi)=e(V_{\xi})\in\mathbb{Z}
is the Euler number of
V_{\xi}
and
{\rm p}(a)=a\tau_{q}
. Elementary properties of the Euler class and
\tau_{q}
show that
\pi_{q-1}\{SO_{q}\}
is a
form parameter with symmetry
(-1)^{q}
.

Example 2.5
.

A chain bundle over the integers,
(B_{*},\beta)
(see
[
R1
, §10]
), defines for each integer
q
a
form parameter
Q_{\beta}(q)
, with symmetry
(-1)^{q}
.

Definition 2.6
.

A morphism of
form parameters
\alpha\colon(P_{e},{\rm h}_{P},{\rm p}_{P})\to(Q_{e},{\rm h}_{Q},{\rm p}_{Q})
is a homomorphism
\alpha\colon P_{e}\to Q_{e}
such that
{\rm h}_{R}\circ\alpha={\rm h}_{Q}
and
\alpha\circ{\rm p}_{Q}={\rm p}_{R}
.
Form parameters form a category, which we denote by
\mathbf{FP}
,
and we denote the full subcategories of symmetric and anti-symmetric form parameters will be denoted
by
\mathbf{FP}_{+}
and
\mathbf{FP}_{-}
respectively.

Remark 2.7
.

Since morphisms preserve the symmetry of
form parameters,
\mathbf{FP}
is the disjoint union of
\mathbf{FP}_{+}
and
\mathbf{FP}_{-}
.

2.1.2.
Linearisation and quasi-Wu classes

We next define some isomorphism invariants of form parameters.

Definition 2.8
.

Let
Q=(Q_{e},{\rm h},{\rm p})
be a
form parameter. The
linearisation of
Q
is the abelian group
SQ:=Q_{e}/\mathrm{Im}(\mathrm{p})
. The quotient map will be denoted
\pi\colon Q_{e}\to SQ
.

Definition 2.9
.

a)

For a symmetric
form parameter
Q
, let
v_{Q}\colon SQ\to\mathbb{Z}_{2}
be the homomorphism induced by
\textup{h}\colon Q_{e}\to\mathbb{Z}
.
The homomorphism
v_{Q}
is well-defined since
{\rm hp}=2{\rm Id}_{\mathbb{Z}}
.

b)

If
Q
is an anti-symmetric
form parameter, then
{\rm p}\colon\mathbb{Z}\to Q_{e}
factors though the quotient homomorphism

\mathbb{Z}\to\mathbb{Z}_{2}
and we let
v^{\prime}_{Q}\colon\mathbb{Z}_{2}\to Q_{e}
denote the homomorphism induced by
{\rm p}
.

The homomorphism
v_{Q}
or
v^{\prime}_{Q}
is called the
quasi-Wu class
of
Q
.

If
Q=(Q_{e},{\rm h},{\rm p})
is a symmetric
form parameter, then
{\rm h}\circ{\rm p}=2\textup{Id}_{\mathbb{Z}}
(hence
{\rm p}
is injective), so we immediately get the following

Lemma 2.10
.

If
Q
is a symmetric
form parameter, then there is a commutative diagram of short exact sequences

\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathbb{Z}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{{\rm p}}
\textstyle{Q_{e}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\pi}
\scriptstyle{{\rm h}}
\textstyle{SQ\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{v_{Q}}
\textstyle{0}
\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathbb{Z}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{2}
\textstyle{\mathbb{Z}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\varrho_{2}}
\textstyle{\mathbb{Z}_{2}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0,}

where
\varrho_{2}
denotes reduction mod
2
. In particular the
right hand square of this diagram is a pullback square.
∎

2.1.3.
Splittings of form parameters and their morphisms

Recall from (
3
) that given a form parameter
Q=(Q_{e},{\rm h},{\rm p})
and abelian group
G
, we write
Q\oplus G
for the
split
form parameter
(Q_{e}\oplus G,{\rm h}\oplus 0,{\rm p}\oplus 0)
.

Definition 2.11
.

Let
P
be a form parameter.

a)

A
splitting
of
P
is an isomorphism
P\cong Q\oplus G
for some form parameter
Q
and abelian group
G
. Equivalently, it is an isomorphism
P_{e}\cong Q_{e}\oplus G
for some abelian groups
Q_{e}
and
G
such that under this identification
\textup{h}\big|_{0\oplus G}=0
and
\textup{p}(1)\in Q_{e}\oplus 0
.

b)

We say that
P
is
decomposable
if there is a splitting
P\cong Q\oplus G
with
G\not\cong 0

and
indecomposable
otherwise.

c)

A splitting
P\cong Q\oplus G
of
P
is called
maximal
if
Q
is indecomposable.

Definition 2.12
.

a)

A morphism
\alpha\colon Q_{1}\oplus G_{1}\to Q_{2}\oplus G_{2}
between split form parameters is called
split
if
\alpha=\alpha_{q}\oplus\alpha_{l}\colon Q_{1e}\oplus G_{1}\to Q_{2e}\oplus G_{2}
for some homomorphisms
\alpha_{q}\colon Q_{1e}\to Q_{2e}
and
\alpha_{l}\colon G_{1}\to G_{2}
.

b)

A morphism
\alpha\colon P_{1}\to P_{2}
is called
splittable
if there are splittings
\alpha_{1}\colon P_{1}\to Q_{1}\oplus G_{1}
and
\alpha_{2}\colon P_{2}\to Q_{2}\oplus G_{2}
such that the composition
\alpha_{2}\circ\alpha\circ\alpha^{-1}_{1}\colon Q_{1}\oplus G_{1}\to Q_{2}\oplus G_{2}
is split.

The following example shows that not all morphisms of form parameters are splittable (in a non-trivial way).

Example 2.13
.

Let
\alpha\colon\mathbb{Z}^{P}_{\infty}\to Q^{+}\oplus\mathbb{Z}_{3}
be the morphism of form parameters given by
\left(\begin{smallmatrix}1&0\\
1&2\end{smallmatrix}\right)\colon\mathbb{Z}\oplus\mathbb{Z}\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}\oplus\mathbb{Z}_{3}
(so that
S\alpha=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}1\\
1\end{smallmatrix}\right)\colon\mathbb{Z}\to\mathbb{Z}_{2}\oplus\mathbb{Z}_{3}
). This homomorphism is surjective, hence
\alpha
is not splittable.

Let
\alpha\colon\mathbb{Z}^{\Lambda}_{2}\to\mathbb{Z}^{\Lambda}_{3}\oplus\mathbb{Z}_{2}
be the morphism of form parameters given by

\alpha\colon\mathbb{Z}_{4}\to\mathbb{Z}_{8}\oplus\mathbb{Z}_{2},[a]\mapsto([2a],[a])
.
Then
\alpha
is not splittable since
\alpha(\mathbb{Z}_{4})
is not contained in any proper summand of
\mathbb{Z}_{8}\oplus\mathbb{Z}_{2}
.

2.1.4.
Extended symmetrisation and the
extended quadratic lift

We introduce the morphisms which will play a key role in the natural description of the Witt group functor in Section
5
.

Definition 2.14
.

Let
Q=(Q_{e},{\rm h},{\rm p})
be a symmetric
form parameter. The
extended symmetrisation morphism
,

\textup{es}_{Q}\colon Q\rightarrow Q^{+}\oplus SQ
, is given by

(\textup{h},\pi)\colon Q_{e}\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}\oplus SQ
.
Unless it is required, we will drop the subscript “
Q
” from the notation, writing “
es
” in place of “
\textup{es}_{Q}
”.

Note that by Lemma
2.10

(\textup{h},\pi)
determines an isomorphism between
Q_{e}
and the pullback of the diagram

\textstyle{SQ\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{v_{Q}}
\textstyle{\mathbb{Z}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\varrho_{2}}
\textstyle{\mathbb{Z}_{2}}

that is,
\{(n,x)\in\mathbb{Z}\times SQ\mid\varrho_{2}(n)=v_{Q}(x)\}
.

Definition 2.15
.

Let
Q=(Q_{e},{\rm h},{\rm p})
be an anti-symmetric form parameter.
The
extended quadratic lift
is the morphism

\textup{eql}_{Q}\colon Q_{-}\oplus Q_{e}\rightarrow Q
, which is given by

v^{\prime}_{Q}\oplus\textup{Id}\colon\mathbb{Z}/2\oplus Q_{e}\rightarrow Q_{e}
.
Unless it is required, we will drop the subscript “
Q
” from the notation,
writing “
eql
” in place of “
\textup{eql}_{Q}
”.

2.1.5.
\mathbb{Z}
-groups, quadratic
\mathbb{Z}
-modules and Schlichting’s definition of a form parameter

A form parameter defines a “quadratic action” of
\mathbb{Z}
on
Q_{e}
, which is both a particular case of an “
R
-group action” as defined by Ranicki
[
R1
, §4]
, and gives
Q_{e}
the structure of a quadratic
\mathbb{Z}
-module, a notion which is an essential part of Schlichting’s definition of a form parameter
[
S
, Definition 3.3]
.

Definition 2.16
.

A
\mathbb{Z}
-group

U
is an abelian group together with an action

\mathbb{Z}\times U\to U,\quad(a,u)\to a\cdot u,

such that for all
a,b\in\mathbb{Z}
and
u,v\in\mathbb{Z}

a\cdot(u+v)=a\cdot u+a\cdot v,\qquad(ab)\cdot u=a\cdot(b\cdot u),\qquad 1\cdot u=u.

A
linear
\mathbb{Z}
-group
, or
\mathbb{Z}
-module, i.e. abelian group, is a
\mathbb{Z}
-group such that

(a+b)\cdot u=a\cdot u+b\cdot u.

In the future we will write
au
for the linear action of
a\in\mathbb{Z}
on an abelian group
U\ni u
.
A map of
\mathbb{Z}
-groups is a homomorphism of the underlying abelian groups which commutes with the

\mathbb{Z}
-actions.

A form parameter
Q
gives
Q_{e}
the structure of a
\mathbb{Z}
-group (c.f.
[
S
, Remark 2.3]
), where
1\in\mathbb{Z}
acts by the identity and the action of
a\in\mathbb{Z}
is inductively defined by

(a+b)\cdot q:=a\cdot q+b\cdot q+{\rm p}(a{\rm h}(q)b)=a\cdot q+b\cdot q+ab{\rm h}(q){\rm p}(1),

where
a,b\in\mathbb{Z}
.
If
C^{a}_{2}:=\frac{a(a-1)}{2}
for
a\in\mathbb{Z}
, then induction shows that for all
a\in\mathbb{Z}
and
q\in Q_{e}

a\cdot q=aq+C^{a}_{2}{\rm h}(q){\rm p}(1).

(6)

It follows that a form parameter
Q
is equivalent to a form parameter
((I,\sigma),\Lambda)
, as defined, by Schlichting
[
S
, Definition 3.3]
, under the following identifications.
The ambient ring with with involution is
\mathbb{Z}
with the trivial involution, the
\mathbb{Z}
-bimodule with involution is
(I,\sigma)=(\mathbb{Z},\epsilon_{Q})
,
the abelian group is
\Lambda=Q_{e}
,
\tau=\textup{p}\colon\mathbb{Z}\to Q_{e}
,
\rho=\textup{h}\colon Q_{e}\to\mathbb{Z}

and the multiplicative
\mathbb{Z}
-linear left action
Q
on
\Lambda
is given by (
6
).

2.2.
Extended quadratic forms over
Q

In this subsection we define extended quadratic forms over a
form parameter
Q
. For brevity, we call such forms
Q
-forms.

A
Q
-form is an enrichment of a symmetric or anti-symmetric bilinear form and we first review these notions.

Let
X
be a finitely generated abelian group.
We define the
dual
of
X
to be the group
X^{*}:={\rm Hom}(X,\mathbb{Z})
.
The canonical map
X\to X^{**}
is defined by
x\mapsto(\phi\mapsto\phi(x))
for

x\in X
and
\phi\in X^{*}
. It is surjective with kernel
TX
, the torsion subgroup of
X
.
We will be largely interested in the torsion free case, i.e. when
TX=0
.

Definition 2.17
.

For
\epsilon=\pm 1
,
an
\epsilon
-symmetric bilinear form

(X,\lambda)

consists of an abelian group
X
and a pairing

\lambda\colon X\times X\to\mathbb{Z},

which for
x,x^{\prime},y\in X
and
a,b\in\mathbb{Z}
, satisfies

\lambda(ax,by)=b\lambda(x,y){a},\quad\lambda(x+x^{\prime},y)=\lambda(x,y)+\lambda(x^{\prime},y)\quad\text{and}\quad\lambda(x,y)=\epsilon{\lambda(y,x)}.

(7)

Equivalently,

\lambda
defines and is defined by its
adjoint homomorphism
,

\widehat{\lambda}\colon X\to X^{*},\quad x\mapsto\bigl(y\mapsto\lambda(x,y)\bigr).

When
X
is torsion free,
the conditions in (
7
) are equivalent to

\widehat{\lambda}
being a homomorphism
such that
\widehat{\lambda}^{*}=\epsilon\widehat{\lambda}
,
where
\widehat{\lambda}^{*}\colon X^{**}=X\to X^{*}
.
If
\widehat{\lambda}
is an isomorphism then
(X,\lambda)

is called
nonsingular
.

Definition 2.18
.

Let
Q=(Q_{e},{\rm h},{\rm p})
be a
form parameter with symmetry
\epsilon_{Q}
.

An extended quadratic form with values in
Q
,

or simply a
Q
-form
, is a triple
(X,\lambda,\mu)
consisting of
a bilinear form
(X,\lambda)
and a function
\mu\colon X\to Q_{e}
such that for all
x,y\in X

\mu(x+y)=\mu(x)+\mu(y)+{\rm p}(\lambda(x,y))\qquad\text{and}\qquad\lambda(x,x)={\rm h}(\mu(x)).

Note that these conditions imply that
\lambda
is symmetric if
\epsilon_{Q}=1
,
or anti-symmetric if
\epsilon_{Q}=-1
.
A
Q
-form
(X,\lambda,\mu)
is
nonsingular
when the underlying form
(X,\lambda)
is nonsingular.

Remark 2.19
.

For a
Q
-form
(X,\lambda,\mu)
we have
\mu(ax)=a\cdot\mu(x)=a\mu(x)+C^{a}_{2}\lambda(x,x){\rm p}(1)
for all
a\in\mathbb{Z}
and
x\in X
. This is a consequence of
the discussion in Section
2.1.5
above.

Remark 2.20
.

In computations an extended quadratic form
(X,\lambda,\mu)
will be written as

\left(X,M_{\lambda},\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\mu(x_{1})\\
\vdots\\
\mu(x_{k})\end{array}\right)\right),

where
x_{1},\ldots,x_{k}
is a basis of
X
and
M_{\lambda}
is the matrix of
\lambda
in this basis. Note that, as
\mu
is not linear, the vector in the third component should not be interpreted as a homomorphism
X\rightarrow Q_{e}
(but the value of
\mu
on an arbitrary element of
X
can be unambiguously computed from this data using the properties of
\mu
). Conversely, a triple

\left(X,M,\left(\begin{array}[]{c}q_{1}\\
\vdots\\
q_{k}\end{array}\right)\right),

where
X
is a free abelian group with a fixed basis
x_{1},\ldots,x_{k}
,
M\in\mathbb{Z}^{k\times k}
and
q_{1},\ldots,q_{k}\in Q_{e}
, denotes a
Q
-form on
X
if and only if
M
is
\epsilon_{Q}
-symmetric and
M_{ii}={\rm h}(q_{i})
for every
i
.

If
X=0
is the trivial group, we write
\underline{0}
for the unique
Q
-form defined on
0
.

Example 2.21
.

For a natural number
m
,
the standard rank-
2m
hyperbolic form over an
\epsilon
-symmetric
form parameter
Q
is

H_{\epsilon}(\mathbb{Z}^{m})=\left(\mathbb{Z}^{m}\oplus\mathbb{Z}^{m},\left[\begin{array}[]{cc}0&I_{m}\\
\epsilon I_{m}&0\end{array}\right],\left(\begin{array}[]{c}0\\
\vdots\\
0\end{array}\right)\right)

where
I_{m}
denotes the
m\times m
identity matrix.
If
m=0
, then
H_{\epsilon}(0)=\underline{0}
.

We briefly review some essential concepts and terminology related to
Q
-forms.
When it is convenient we shall denote the
Q
-form
(X,\lambda,\mu)
simply by
\underline{\mu}
.
The rank of
\underline{\mu}
, denoted by
\textup{rk}(\underline{\mu})
, is the rank of the abelian group
X
. If
Q
is symmetric, then the signature of
\underline{\mu}
,
\sigma(\underline{\mu})
, is the signature of the symmetric bilinear form
(X,\lambda)
.

Definition 2.22
.

Following
[
Bau1
, §5]
,
we denote the set of
Q
-forms on
X

by
\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathbb{Z}}(X,Q)

and the subset of nonsingular
Q
-forms by
\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathbb{Z}}^{\rm ns}(X,Q)\subset\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathbb{Z}}(X,Q)
.
We recall from Section
1.2
that
\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathbb{Z}}^{\rm ns}(Q)
denotes the set of isomorphism classes of all nonsingular
Q
-forms.

Elementwise addition (given by
(\lambda+\lambda^{\prime})(x,y)=\lambda(x,y)+\lambda^{\prime}(x,y)
and
(\mu+\mu^{\prime})(x)=\mu(x)+\mu^{\prime}(x)
) gives
\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathbb{Z}}(X,Q)
the structure of an abelian group with zero element the zero form,
(X,0,0)
, and inverse defined by
-(X,\lambda,\mu):=(X,-\lambda,-\mu)
, where
(-\lambda)(x,y)=-\lambda(x,y)
and
(-\mu)(x)=-\mu(x)
.

Definition 2.23
.

a)

Given a
Q
-form
(Y,\lambda,\mu)
on
Y
and homomorphism
f\colon X\to Y
, the
pullback
of
(Y,\lambda,\mu)
along
f
is the
Q
-form
(X,f^{*}\lambda,f^{*}\mu)\in\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathbb{Z}}(X,Q)
, where
f^{*}\lambda(x,y)=\lambda(f(x),f(y))
and
f^{*}\mu(x)=\mu(f(x))
.

b)

If
(X,\lambda,\mu)\in\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathbb{Z}}(X,Q)
,
(Y,\phi,\psi)\in\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathbb{Z}}(Y,Q)
and
f\colon X\to Y
is a homomorphism then
f
defines a
morphism
of
Q
-forms if
(X,f^{*}\phi,f^{*}\psi)=(X,\lambda,\mu)
.

c)

A morphism of
Q
-forms is called an
isometry
if it is an isomorphism of the underlying abelian groups.

Remark 2.24
.

The assignment
X\mapsto\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathbb{Z}}(X,Q)
defines a contravariant
quadratic functor

\mathcal{F}_{Q}\colon\mathbf{Ab}\to\mathbf{Ab},

in the sense of Baues
[
Bau1
, §1]
.

Similarly, for a fixed
X
the assignment
Q\mapsto\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathbb{Z}}(X,Q)
defines a covariant functor
\mathbf{FP}\rightarrow\mathbf{Ab}
.
Specifically, for a morphism
\alpha\colon Q\rightarrow Q^{\prime}
for form parameters, we have the induced the homomorphism

\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathbb{Z}}(X,\alpha)\colon\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathbb{Z}}(X,Q)\to\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathbb{Z}}(X,Q^{\prime}),\quad(X,\lambda,\mu)\mapsto(X,\lambda,\alpha\circ\mu).

Definition 2.25
.

The direct sum of the
Q
-forms
(X,\lambda,\mu)
and
(X^{\prime},\lambda^{\prime},\mu^{\prime})
is the
Q
-form
(X\oplus X^{\prime},\lambda\oplus\lambda^{\prime},\mu\oplus\mu^{\prime})
, where
(\lambda\oplus\lambda^{\prime})((x,x^{\prime}),(y,y^{\prime}))=\lambda(x,y)+\lambda^{\prime}(x^{\prime},y^{\prime})
and
(\mu\oplus\mu^{\prime})(x,x^{\prime})=\mu(x)+\mu^{\prime}(x^{\prime})
.

Definition 2.26
.

Let
\underline{\mu}=(X,\lambda,\mu)
be a
Q
-form.

a)

The composition of
\mu\colon X\to Q_{e}
with the quotient map
Q_{e}\to SQ
is a homomorphism
S\mu\colon X\to SQ
,
which is called the
linearisation of
\underline{\mu}
.

b)

The
Q
-form
\underline{\mu}
is called
full
if its linearisation
S\mu\colon X\to SQ
is onto.

Definition 2.27
.

Let
(X,\lambda)
be a symmetric bilinear form,
and
f\colon X\to C
a homomorphism to a cyclic group
with mod
2
reduction
f_{2}\colon X\to C/2C\subseteq\mathbb{Z}_{2}
.
Then
f
is called
characteristic for
\lambda
, if for all
x\in X
we have

\varrho_{2}\circ\lambda(x,x)=f_{2}(x).

We next discuss the homomorphism of groups of forms,

\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathbb{Z}}(X,\textup{es})\colon\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathbb{Z}}(X,Q)\to\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathbb{Z}}(X,Q^{+}\oplus SQ),

where
Q
is a symmetric form parameter,
X
is an abelian group
and
\textup{es}\colon Q\to Q^{+}\oplus SQ
is the extended symmetrisation morphism.
Note that if
(X,\lambda,(\mu_{0},\mu_{1}))
is a
(Q^{+}\oplus G)
-form for some abelian group
G
,
then
\mu_{0}(x)=\lambda(x,x)
for every
x
and
\mu_{1}
is a homomorphism by Definition
2.18
.
Moreover, if a
(Q^{+}\oplus G)
-form
(X,\lambda,(\mu_{0},\mu_{1}))
is the extended symmetrisation of a
Q
-form
(X,\lambda,\mu)
,
then
G=SQ
and
\mu_{1}=S\mu
.

Definition 2.28
.

Let
Q
be a symmetric form parameter
and define
\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathbb{Z}}^{\rm ch}(X,Q^{+}\oplus SQ)\subset\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathbb{Z}}(X,Q^{+}\oplus SQ)
to be the subgroup of
(Q^{+}\oplus SQ)
-forms

(X,\lambda,(\mu_{0},\mu_{1}))
such that
v_{Q}\circ\mu_{1}
is characteristic for
\lambda
.

Lemma 2.29
.

Let
Q
be a symmetric form parameter and
X
an abelian group.

a) For every
Q
-form
(X,\lambda,\mu)
, the composition

v_{Q}\circ S\mu\colon X\to\mathbb{Z}_{2}
is characteristic for
\lambda
;
i.e.
\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathbb{Z}}(X,\textup{es})
takes values in
\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathbb{Z}}^{\rm ch}(X,Q^{+}\oplus SQ)
.

b)
The map
\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathbb{Z}}(X,\textup{es})\colon\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathbb{Z}}(X,Q)\to\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathbb{Z}}^{\rm ch}(X,Q^{+}\oplus SQ)

is an isomorphism.

Proof.

a) Let
Q=(Q_{e},{\rm h},{\rm p})
.
Let
\pi\colon Q_{e}\rightarrow SQ
denote the quotient map so that the extended symmetrisation map is given by
({\rm h},\pi)
. By Lemma
2.10

({\rm h},\pi)
is an isomorphism between
Q_{e}
and the subgroup
\left\{(z,y)\in\mathbb{Z}\oplus SQ\mid\varrho_{2}(z)=v_{Q}(y)\right\}
. This means that if
(X,\lambda,\mu)\in\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathbb{Z}}(X,Q)
and its image is
(X,\lambda,(\mu_{0},\mu_{1}))
(i.e.
(\mu_{0},\mu_{1})=({\rm h},\pi)\circ\mu
), then
v_{Q}\circ\mu_{1}(x)=\varrho_{2}\circ\mu_{0}(x)=\varrho_{2}\circ\lambda(x,x)
.

b) Now suppose that
(X,\lambda,(\mu_{0},\mu_{1}))\in\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathbb{Z}}(X,Q^{+}\oplus SQ)
is such that
\varrho_{2}\circ\lambda(x,x)=v_{Q}\circ\mu_{1}(x)
for every
x\in X
. By the above
(\mu_{0},\mu_{1})(x)\in\mathrm{Im}({\rm h},\pi)
for every
x\in X
, and so there is a unique function
\mu\colon X\rightarrow Q_{e}
such that
(\mu_{0},\mu_{1})=({\rm h},\pi)\circ\mu
. We will prove that
(X,\lambda,\mu)
is
Q
-form.
First,
{\rm h}(\mu(x))=\mu_{0}(x)=\lambda(x,x)
. Second,

\displaystyle\mu(x+y)

\displaystyle=({\rm h},\pi)^{-1}\circ(\mu_{0},\mu_{1})(x+y)

\displaystyle=({\rm h},\pi)^{-1}(\lambda(x+y,x+y),\mu_{1}(x+y))

\displaystyle=({\rm h},\pi)^{-1}((\lambda(x,x),\mu_{1}(x))+(\lambda(y,y),\mu_{1}(y))+(2\lambda(x,y),0))

\displaystyle=({\rm h},\pi)^{-1}(\mu_{0}(x),\mu_{1}(x))+({\rm h},\pi)^{-1}(\mu_{0}(y),\mu_{1}(y))+({\rm h},\pi)^{-1}(2\lambda(x,y),0)

\displaystyle=\mu(x)+\mu(y)+{\rm p}(\lambda(x,y)).

Therefore
(X,\lambda,\mu)\in\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathbb{Z}}(X,Q)
.
Since the assignment
(X,\lambda,(\mu_{0},\mu_{1}))\mapsto(X,\lambda,\mu)
is the inverse of the map induced by the extended symmetrisation map, part b) follows.
∎

Remark 2.30
.

We briefly discuss the relationship between the terminology and notation of this
paper and those of

[
N1
, Chapter 4]
and
[
N2
]
.
In
[
N1
, Chapter 4]
and
[
N2
]

the second author
introduces (symmetric)
extended quadratic forms over an abelian group
G
. Such a form is a triple
(X,\lambda,f)
, where
(X,\lambda)
is a symmetric bilinear form and
f\colon X\to G
is a homomorphism. It corresponds to the
(Q^{+}\oplus G)
-form
(X,\lambda,(\mu_{0},f))
(where
\mu_{0}(x)=\lambda(x,x)
). Given a homomorphism
t\colon G\to\mathbb{Z}_{2}
, the form is called
geometric
with respect to
t
if
t\circ f\colon X\to\mathbb{Z}_{2}
is characteristic for
\lambda
. Thus for a symmetric form parameter
Q
, it follows from Lemma
2.29
that
Q
-forms are equivalent to extended quadratic forms over the abelian group
SQ
that are geometric with respect to
v_{Q}
.

2.3.
The Witt group
W_{0}(Q)

Let
Q
be a form parameter with symmetry
\epsilon_{Q}
.
In this subsection, we assume that all
Q
-forms

(X,\lambda,\mu)
are nonsingular,
and recall that
\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathbb{Z}}^{\rm ns}(Q)
is the set of isomorphism classes of nonsingular
Q
-forms.
Of course, if
(X,\lambda,\mu)
is nonsingular
then
X
is torsion free.

A summand,
L\subset X
, is called a
lagrangian
of
(X,\lambda,\mu)
if
\textup{rk}(X)=2\textup{rk}(L)
and the inclusion
i\colon L\hookrightarrow X
satisfies
i^{*}(X,\lambda,\mu)=(L,0,0)
.
For any
Q
-form
(X,\lambda,\mu)
, the
Q
-form
(X\oplus X,\lambda\oplus-\lambda,\mu\oplus-\mu)
has the diagonal,

\Delta:=\left\{(x,x)\,\big|\,x\in X\right\}\subset X\oplus X,

as a lagrangian.
A
Q
-form which admits a lagrangian is called
metabolic
. We warn the reader that we depart from
[
R1
, §10]
where the term
hyperbolic
is used
for metabolic
Q
-forms.

We say that
Q
-forms
\underline{\mu_{0}}
and
\underline{\mu_{1}}
are
Witt equivalent
if there are metabolic

Q
-forms
\underline{\phi_{0}}
and
\underline{\phi_{1}}
such that

\underline{\mu_{0}}\oplus\underline{\phi_{0}}\cong\underline{\mu_{1}}\oplus\underline{\phi_{1}}
, and write
\underline{\mu}_{0}\sim_{\rm W}\underline{\mu}_{1}
.
We denote the Witt equivalence class of a
Q
-form
\underline{\mu}
by
[\underline{\mu}]
, and recall the following definition
from the Introduction.

Definition 2.31
.

The
Witt group

W_{0}(Q)
is the abelian group formed by the set of Witt equivalence classes of nonsingular
Q
-forms, with addition given by direct sum:

W_{0}(Q):=\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathbb{Z}}^{\rm ns}(Q)/\!\!\sim_{\rm W}~=\left(\bigl\{[X,\lambda,\mu]\,\big|\,\text{$(X,\lambda,\mu)$ is a nonsingular $Q$-form}\bigr\},\oplus\right)

The zero element of
W_{0}(Q)
is the equivalence class of metabolic forms and
the inverse of
[\underline{\mu}]
is
[-\underline{\mu}]
since
\underline{\mu}\oplus(-\underline{\mu})
is metabolic.

Remark 2.32
.

By definition, a
Q
-form
\underline{\mu}
represents the zero element of
W_{0}(Q)
if and only if it is
stably metabolic
; i.e. there are metabolic
Q
-forms
\underline{\mu}_{0}
and
\underline{\mu}_{1}

such that
\underline{\mu}\oplus\underline{\mu}_{0}\cong\underline{\mu}_{1}
.
In Remark
4.4
, we observe that for all
k\geq 2
, there are stably metabolic forms over
Q=\mathbb{Z}^{\Lambda}_{k}
, which are not metabolic, and also that there is a stably metabolic form over
Q=\mathbb{Z}^{P}_{1}
, which is not metabolic.

Now suppose that
\alpha\colon Q_{1}\to Q_{2}
is a morphism of form parameters.
Then
\alpha
induces the map

\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathbb{Z}}^{\rm ns}(X,\alpha)\colon\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathbb{Z}}^{\rm ns}(X,Q_{1})\to\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathbb{Z}}^{\rm ns}(X,Q_{2}),\quad[X,\lambda,\mu]\mapsto[X,\lambda,\alpha\circ\mu].

Since
\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathbb{Z}}^{\rm ns}(X,\alpha)
preserves the zero form (on any subgroup of
X
), it also preserves metabolic forms,
and so
\alpha
descends to define a homomorphism of Witt groups.

Definition 2.33
.

Let
\alpha\colon Q_{0}\to Q_{1}
be a morphism of form parameters. Define

W_{0}(\alpha)\colon W_{0}(Q_{1})\to W_{0}(Q_{2}),\quad[X,\lambda,\mu]\mapsto[X,\lambda,\alpha\circ\mu].

Hence taking the Witt group of a form parameter defines a functor,

W_{0}\colon\mathbf{FP}\to\mathbf{Ab},\quad Q\mapsto W_{0}(Q),

from the category of form parameters to the category of abelian groups. As we stated in the Introduction, the main aim of this paper is to compute the Witt group functor
W_{0}\colon\mathbf{FP}\to\mathbf{Ab}
.

3.
The classification of quadratic form parameters

In this section we classify the category oriented form parameters over
\mathbb{Z}
.
In particular,
we prove that every form parameter has a maximal splitting, that is, it is isomorphic to the sum of an indecomposable form parameter and an abelian group (see Definition
2.11
). We also prove that (
1
) and (
2
) give a complete list of (isomorphism classes of) indecomposable form parameters. Finally, we will explicitly describe the possible morphisms between indecomposable form parameters.

We begin by recalling the list of standard form parameters from (
1
) and (
2
).

Definition 3.1
.

We have the following standard form parameters (where
\mathbb{Z}^{P}
was previously denoted
\mathbb{Z}^{P}_{\infty}
):

\displaystyle\underline{\quad\epsilon=+1\quad}

\displaystyle\underline{\quad\epsilon=-1\quad}

\displaystyle Q_{+}

\displaystyle=\Bigl(\mathbb{Z}\xrightarrow{2}\mathbb{Z}\xrightarrow{1}\mathbb{Z}\Bigr)

\displaystyle Q_{-}

\displaystyle=\Bigl(\mathbb{Z}_{2}\xrightarrow{0}\mathbb{Z}\xrightarrow{1}\mathbb{Z}_{2}\Bigr)

\displaystyle\mathbb{Z}^{P}

\displaystyle=\Bigl(\mathbb{Z}\oplus\mathbb{Z}\xrightarrow{(1,0)}\mathbb{Z}\xrightarrow{\left(\!\begin{smallmatrix}2\\
-1\end{smallmatrix}\!\right)}\mathbb{Z}\oplus\mathbb{Z}\Bigr)

\displaystyle\mathbb{Z}^{P}_{k}

\displaystyle=\Bigl(\mathbb{Z}\oplus\mathbb{Z}_{2^{k}}\xrightarrow{(1,0)}\mathbb{Z}\xrightarrow{\left(\!\begin{smallmatrix}2\\
-1\end{smallmatrix}\!\right)}\mathbb{Z}\oplus\mathbb{Z}_{2^{k}}\Bigr)\quad(k\geq 1)\quad\quad

\displaystyle\mathbb{Z}^{\Lambda}_{k}

\displaystyle=\Bigl(\mathbb{Z}_{2^{k}}\xrightarrow{~~0~~}\mathbb{Z}\xrightarrow{2^{k-1}}\mathbb{Z}_{2^{k}}\Bigr)\quad(k\geq 2)

\displaystyle Q^{+}

\displaystyle=\Bigl(\mathbb{Z}\xrightarrow{1}\mathbb{Z}\xrightarrow{2}\mathbb{Z}\Bigr)

\displaystyle Q^{-}

\displaystyle=\Bigl(0\xrightarrow{}\mathbb{Z}\xrightarrow{}0\Bigr)

Remark 3.2
.

We warn the reader that our notation differs somewhat from Baues’ notation
[
Bau1
,
Bau2
]
, where
Q_{+}
is called

\mathbb{Z}^{S}
,
Q^{+}
is called
\mathbb{Z}^{\Gamma}
,
Q_{-}
is called
\mathbb{Z}^{\Lambda}_{1}
and
Q^{-}
is called

\mathbb{Z}^{\Lambda}
. Baues’ notation is based on the quadratic functors corresponding to
these quadratic modules;
see Example
4.7
. In particular the quadratic functors corresponding to
\mathbb{Z}^{S},\mathbb{Z}^{P}
and
\mathbb{Z}^{\Lambda}_{1}
are respectively the symmetric tensor product,
the universal quadratic functor and the skewsymmetric tensor product.
Where there is an overlap in notation, our notation is designed to fit with Ranicki’s notation in algebraic surgery.

Recall that given a form parameter
Q=(Q_{e},{\rm h},{\rm p})
and an abelian group
G
,
the split form parameter
Q\oplus G
is
(Q_{e}\oplus G,{\rm h}\oplus 0,{\rm p}\oplus 0)
, and
that a splitting of
P
is an isomorphism
P\cong Q\oplus G
for some form parameter
Q
and abelian group
G
.
A form parameter
P
is decomposable if there is a splitting
P\cong Q\oplus G
with
G\not\cong 0

and indecomposable otherwise,
a splitting
P\cong Q\oplus G
of
P
is called maximal if
Q
is indecomposable.
The following result restates Theorem
1.4
from the introduction.

Theorem 3.3

(The classification of quadratic form parameters)
.

a) The form parameters in Definition
3.1
are indecomposable and pairwise non-isomorphic. Any indecomposable form parameter is isomorphic to one of them.

b) Every form parameter
P
has a maximal splitting
P\cong Q\oplus G
. Moreover,
Q
and
G
are each well-defined up to isomorphism.

Following Theorem
3.3
we can define the following invariants of a form parameter (cf. Theorems
3.19
and
3.20
).

Definition 3.4
.

Let
P=Q\oplus G
be a maximal splitting of a form parameter.

a)

The
height
of an indecomposable form parameter,

\textup{ht}(Q)\in\mathbb{N}_{0}\cup\{\infty\}
is defined by

\textup{ht}(Q_{+})=\textup{ht}(Q^{-})=0,\qquad\textup{ht}(Q^{+})=\textup{ht}(Q_{-})=1,\qquad\textup{ht}(\mathbb{Z}^{P}_{k})=\textup{ht}(\mathbb{Z}^{\Lambda}_{k+1})=k{+}1\quad\text{and}\quad\textup{ht}(\mathbb{Z}^{P})=\infty.

b)

The height of
P
is defined by

\textup{ht}(P)=\textup{ht}(Q)
.

c)

The
complement
of
P
,
[G_{P}]
, is defined to be the isomorphism class of
G
.

As a direct consequence of
Theorem
3.3
we have

Corollary 3.5
.

Form parameters are classified up to isomorphism by their symmetry, height and complement;
i.e.
P_{1}\cong P_{2}
if and only if

(\epsilon_{P_{1}},\textup{ht}(P_{1}),[G_{P_{1}}])=(\epsilon_{P_{2}},\textup{ht}(P_{2}),[G_{P_{2}}])
. ∎

To prove Theorem
3.3
, we will identify
\mathbf{FP}_{+}
and
\mathbf{FP}_{-}
with categories of simpler objects. We start by introducing these categories in Section
3.1
, and then describe the identifications in Section
3.2
. The proof of Theorem
3.3

is given at the end of Section
3.2
.

3.1.
The slice and coslice categories
\mathbf{Ab}/\mathbb{Z}_{2}
and
\mathbb{Z}_{2}/\mathbf{Ab}

In this subsection we consider the target categories in the equivalences of Theorem
1.3
. We will prove the analogue of Theorem
3.3
for these categories, see Theorem
3.14
.

Definition 3.6
.

We recall the following notation from the Introduction:

a)

\mathbf{Ab}
is the category of finitely generated abelian groups.

b)

\mathbf{Ab}/\mathbb{Z}_{2}
denotes the slice category whose objects are homomorphisms
v\colon A\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}_{2}
for finitely generated abelian groups
A
, and whose morphisms from
v_{1}\colon A_{1}\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}_{2}
to
v_{2}\colon A_{2}\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}_{2}
are homomorphisms
f\colon A_{1}\to A_{2}
such that
the following diagram commutes:

\textstyle{A_{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{v_{1}}
\scriptstyle{f}
\textstyle{A_{2}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{v_{2}}
\textstyle{\mathbb{Z}_{2}}

c)

\mathbb{Z}_{2}/\mathbf{Ab}
denotes the coslice category whose objects are homomorphisms
v^{\prime}\colon\mathbb{Z}_{2}\rightarrow A
for finitely generated abelian groups
A
, and whose morphisms from
v^{\prime}_{1}\colon\mathbb{Z}_{2}\rightarrow A_{1}
to
v^{\prime}_{2}\colon\mathbb{Z}_{2}\rightarrow A_{2}
are homomorphisms
f\colon A_{1}\to A_{2}
such that
the following diagram commutes:

\textstyle{\mathbb{Z}_{2}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{v^{\prime}_{1}}
\scriptstyle{v^{\prime}_{2}}
\textstyle{A_{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f}
\textstyle{A_{2}}

Definition 3.7
.

Given a homomorphism
v_{0}\colon B\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}_{2}
and an abelian group
C
we write
v_{0}\oplus C\colon B\oplus C\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}_{2}
for the composition of the projection
B\oplus C\rightarrow B
and
v_{0}
.

Given a homomorphism
v^{\prime}_{0}\colon\mathbb{Z}_{2}\rightarrow B
and an abelian group
C
we write
v^{\prime}_{0}\oplus C\colon\mathbb{Z}_{2}\rightarrow B\oplus C
for the composition of
v^{\prime}_{0}
and the inclusion
B\rightarrow B\oplus C
.

Definition 3.8
.

Let
v\colon A\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}_{2}
(resp.
v^{\prime}\colon\mathbb{Z}_{2}\rightarrow A
) be a homomorphism.

a)

A
splitting
of
v
(resp.
v^{\prime}
) is an isomorphism
v\cong v_{0}\oplus C
(resp.
v^{\prime}\cong v^{\prime}_{0}\oplus C
) for some homomorphism
v_{0}\colon B\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}_{2}
(resp.
v^{\prime}_{0}\colon\mathbb{Z}_{2}\rightarrow B
) and abelian group
C
.

b)

We say that
v
(resp.
v^{\prime}
) is
decomposable
if there is a splitting
v\cong v_{0}\oplus C
(resp.
v^{\prime}\cong v^{\prime}_{0}\oplus C
) with
C\not\cong 0

and
indecomposable
otherwise.

c)

A splitting
v\cong v_{0}\oplus C
(resp.
v^{\prime}\cong v^{\prime}_{0}\oplus C
) is called
maximal
if
v_{0}
(resp.
v^{\prime}_{0}
) is indecomposable.

Example 3.9
.

We have the following homomorphisms, as objects in
\mathbf{Ab}/\mathbb{Z}_{2}
and
\mathbb{Z}_{2}/\mathbf{Ab}
:

\displaystyle\underline{\quad\mathbf{Ab}/\mathbb{Z}_{2}\quad}

\displaystyle\underline{\quad\mathbb{Z}_{2}/\mathbf{Ab}\quad}

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle\colon 0\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}_{2}

\displaystyle\iota_{1}

\displaystyle\colon\mathbb{Z}_{2}\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}_{2}

\displaystyle 1_{\infty}

\displaystyle\colon\mathbb{Z}\xrightarrow{1}\mathbb{Z}_{2}\quad\quad\quad\quad

\displaystyle 1_{k+1}

\displaystyle\colon\mathbb{Z}_{2^{k+1}}\xrightarrow{1}\mathbb{Z}_{2}\quad(k\geq 1)\quad

\displaystyle\iota_{k}

\displaystyle\colon\mathbb{Z}_{2}\xrightarrow{2^{k-1}}\mathbb{Z}_{2^{k}}\quad(k\geq 2)

\displaystyle 1_{1}

\displaystyle\colon\mathbb{Z}_{2}\xrightarrow{1}\mathbb{Z}_{2}

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle\colon\mathbb{Z}_{2}\xrightarrow{}0

In particular
1_{1}=\iota_{1}=\textup{Id}_{\mathbb{Z}_{2}}
.

Proposition 3.10
.

a) The homomorphisms listed in Example
3.9
are indecomposable and pairwise non-isomorphic.

b) Every homomorphism
v\colon A\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}_{2}
(resp.
v^{\prime}\colon\mathbb{Z}_{2}\rightarrow A
) is isomorphic to some
v_{0}\oplus C\colon B\oplus C\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}_{2}
(resp.
v^{\prime}_{0}\oplus C\colon\mathbb{Z}_{2}\rightarrow B\oplus C
), where
v_{0}\colon B\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}_{2}
(resp.
v^{\prime}_{0}\colon\mathbb{Z}_{2}\rightarrow B
) is one of the homomorphisms listed in Example
3.9
and
C
is an abelian group.

c) Every indecomposable homomorphism is isomorphic to one of the homomorphisms listed in Example
3.9
.

d) If
v\colon A\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}_{2}
(resp.
v^{\prime}\colon\mathbb{Z}_{2}\rightarrow A
) is isomorphic to
v_{0}\oplus C\colon B\oplus C\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}_{2}
(resp.
v^{\prime}_{0}\oplus C\colon\mathbb{Z}_{2}\rightarrow B\oplus C
) for an indecomposable
v_{0}\colon B\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}_{2}
(resp.
v^{\prime}_{0}\colon\mathbb{Z}_{2}\rightarrow B
), then the isomorphism classes of
v_{0}
(resp.
v^{\prime}_{0}
) and
C
are determined by
v
(resp.
v^{\prime}
).

Proof.

a) This is straightforward to check.

b) First we consider the slice category. If
v=0
, then
v\cong 0\oplus A
. If
v\big|_{\textup{Tor}(A)}\neq 0
, then by Lemma
3.11

A\cong\mathbb{Z}_{2^{k}}\oplus H
for some
k\geq 1
and
H\leq\mathrm{Ker}(v)
, and
v\cong 1_{k}\oplus H\colon\mathbb{Z}_{2^{k}}\oplus H\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}_{2}
. Finally, if
v\neq 0
but
v\big|_{\textup{Tor}(A)}=0
, then
v
factors through a non-zero map
A/\textup{Tor}(A)\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}_{2}
, and by Lemma
3.12
that map is isomorphic to
1_{\infty}\oplus H\colon\mathbb{Z}\oplus H\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}_{2}
for some
H\leq A/\textup{Tor}(A)
. Therefore
v\cong 1_{\infty}\oplus(H\oplus\textup{Tor}(A))
.

Next we consider the coslice category. If
v^{\prime}=0
, then
v^{\prime}\cong 0\oplus A
. If
v^{\prime}\neq 0
, then
v^{\prime}(1)\in A
is an element of order
2
. Let
l=\max\left\{m\mid\exists x\in A:v^{\prime}(1)=2^{m}x\right\}\geq 0
and choose an
x
such that
v^{\prime}(1)=2^{l}x
. By Lemma
3.13
the subgroup
(x)\subseteq A
is a direct summand, so
A\cong(x)\oplus(A/(x))
, and hence
v^{\prime}\cong\iota_{l+1}\oplus(A/(x))
.

c) Follows by applying Part b) to the indecomposable homomorphism.

d) Suppose that
v\cong v_{0}\oplus C
for a
v_{0}
listed in Example
3.9
and an abelian group
C
. If
v_{0}=0
, then
v=0
. If
v_{0}=1_{\infty}
, then
v\neq 0
, but
\textup{Tor}(A)\leq\mathrm{Ker}(v)
. If
v_{0}=1_{k}
, then
\min\left\{\text{order of $x$}\mid x\in\textup{Tor}(A),v(x)=1\right\}=2^{k}
.

Similarly suppose that
v^{\prime}\cong v^{\prime}_{0}\oplus C
for a
v^{\prime}_{0}
listed in Example
3.9
and an abelian group
C
. If
v^{\prime}_{0}=0
, then
v^{\prime}=0
. If
v^{\prime}_{0}=\iota_{k}
, then
\max\left\{m\mid\exists x\in A:v^{\prime}(1)=2^{m}x\right\}=k-1
.

The above observations imply that for a fixed
v\colon A\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}_{2}
(resp.
v^{\prime}\colon\mathbb{Z}_{2}\rightarrow A
) there is at most one homomorphism
v_{0}
(resp.
v^{\prime}_{0}
) listed in Example
3.9
such that there exists an isomorphism
v\cong v_{0}\oplus C
(resp.
v^{\prime}\cong v^{\prime}_{0}\oplus C
). By Part c) this means that in a maximal splitting
v\cong v_{0}\oplus C
(resp.
v^{\prime}\cong v^{\prime}_{0}\oplus C
) the indecomposable homomorphism
v_{0}\colon B\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}_{2}
(resp.
v^{\prime}_{0}\colon\mathbb{Z}_{2}\rightarrow B
) is determined up to isomorphism. This also determines the isomorphism class of
C
, because
A\cong B\oplus C

∎

We used the following lemmas in the proof of Proposition
3.10
:

Lemma 3.11
.

Let
G
be a finitely generated abelian group and
f\colon G\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}_{2}
a homomorphism such that
f\big|_{\textup{Tor}(G)}\neq 0
. Let
a=\min\left\{\text{order of $x$}\mid x\in\textup{Tor}(G),f(x)=1\right\}
, and let
g\in G
be an element such that
f(g)=1
and
ag=0
. Then
a
is a power of
2
and there is a subgroup
H\leq\mathrm{Ker}(f)
such that
G=(g)\oplus H
.

Proof.

If
a
is not a power of
2
, then
a=2^{b}c
for some
c>1
odd, so
f(cg)=1
and
cg
has order
2^{b}<a
contradicting the minimality of
a
. Therefore
a=2^{k}
for some
k
. We get similarly that if
f(x)=1
for some
x\in\textup{Tor}(G)
, then
2^{k}
divides the order of
x
.

Consider the short exact sequence

0\rightarrow(g)\rightarrow G\rightarrow G/(g)\rightarrow 0.

We will construct the subgroup
H
as the image of a splitting map
G/(g)\rightarrow G
. We write
G/(g)
as a direct sum of cyclic groups, then for each generator
x
we need to find an element
y\in G
in the inverse image of
x
such that
f(y)=0
and if
x
has finite order
r
, then
ry=0
. Fix a
z\in G
that gets mapped to
x
. If
x
has infinite order then we can choose
y=z
or
y=z{+}g
depending on whether
f(z)=0
or
f(z)=1
. If
x
has finite order
r
, then
rz\in(g)
. If
rz=0
and
f(z)=0
, then we can choose
y=z
. If
f(z)=1
, then
2^{k}
divides
r
, so
r(z+g)=0
, and since
f(z+g)=0
, we can choose
y=z{+}g
. If
rz\neq 0
, then
rz=sg
for some non-zero
s\in\mathbb{Z}_{2^{k}}
. Write
r=2^{b_{1}}c_{1}
and
s=2^{b_{2}}c_{2}
, where
c_{1}
and
c_{2}
are odd and
b_{2}<k
. If
b_{1}>b_{2}
, then
2^{b_{2}}(2^{b_{1}-b_{2}}c_{1}z-c_{2}g)=0
and
f(2^{b_{1}-b_{2}}c_{1}z-c_{2}g)=2^{b_{1}-b_{2}}f(c_{1}z)-c_{2}f(g)=1
, which contradicts the minimality of
a
. If
b_{1}\leq b_{2}
, then
s\equiv 2^{b_{2}-b_{1}}c_{2}c_{3}r
mod
2^{k}
, where
c_{3}\in\mathbb{Z}_{2^{k}}
is such that
c_{1}c_{3}\equiv 1
mod
2^{k}
. Then
r(z-2^{b_{2}-b_{1}}c_{2}c_{3}g)=rz-sg=0
. Moreover,
2^{k}
does not divide
r
, so
f(z-2^{b_{2}-b_{1}}c_{2}c_{3}g)=0
. Therefore we can set
y=z-2^{b_{2}-b_{1}}c_{2}c_{3}g
.
∎

Lemma 3.12
.

Let
G
be a finitely generated free abelian group and
f\colon G\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}_{2}
a non-trivial homomorphism. Then there is a
g\in G
and a subgroup
H\leq\mathrm{Ker}(f)
such that
f(g)=1
and
G=(g)\oplus H
.

Proof.

Fix a basis of
G
, then there is a basis element
g
such that
f(g)=1
(otherwise
f=0
). For each of the other basis elements
h
we define
h^{\prime}\in G
by
h^{\prime}=h
if
f(h)=0
and
h^{\prime}=h+g
otherwise. Then the subgroup generated by these elements
h^{\prime}
is a suitable
H
.
∎

Lemma 3.13
.

Let
G
be a finitely generated abelian group, and
g\in G
an element of order
p
for some prime
p
. Let
a:=\max\left\{b\mid\exists h\in G:g=p^{b}h\right\}
and choose an
h
such that
g=p^{a}h
. Then the subgroup
(h)\subseteq G
is a direct summand.

Proof.

We need to construct a splitting map
G/(h)\rightarrow G
for the short exact sequence

0\rightarrow(h)\rightarrow G\rightarrow G/(h)\rightarrow 0.

Since
G/(h)
is a direct sum of cyclic groups, it is enough to find for each generator
x
an element
y\in G
in the inverse image of
x
such that
ry=0
, where
r
is the order of
x
(if it is infinite, then there is no condition on
y
). Fix a
z\in G
that gets mapped to
x
, then
rz\in(h)
. If
rz=0
, then we can set
y=z
. If
rz\neq 0
, then
g=srz
for some non-zero
s\in\mathbb{Z}_{p^{a+1}}
(because
g
is an order-
p
element in
(h)\cong\mathbb{Z}_{p^{a+1}}
). Write
r=p^{c_{1}}d_{1}
and
s=p^{c_{2}}d_{2}
, where
d_{1}
and
d_{2}
are not divisible by
p
. Then
g=p^{c_{1}+c_{2}}d_{1}d_{2}z
, so
c_{1}+c_{2}\leq a
. Also,
p^{a}h=p^{c_{2}}d_{2}rz
means that
rz=p^{a-c_{2}}d_{3}h
for a
d_{3}\in\mathbb{Z}_{p^{a+1}}
with
d_{2}d_{3}\equiv 1
mod
p
. Therefore
rz=rv
, where
v=p^{a-c_{1}-c_{2}}d_{3}d_{4}h\in(h)
for a
d_{4}\in\mathbb{Z}_{p^{a+1}}
with
d_{1}d_{4}\equiv 1
mod
p^{c_{2}+1}
. So we can choose
y=z-v
.
∎

The statements of Proposition
3.10
are summarised in the following theorem:

Theorem 3.14

(The classification of
abelian groups over or under
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
)
.

a) Example
3.9
gives a complete list of indecomposable homomorphisms in
\mathbf{Ab}/\mathbb{Z}_{2}
and
\mathbb{Z}_{2}/\mathbf{Ab}
(up to isomorphism).

b) Every homomorphism in
\mathbf{Ab}/\mathbb{Z}_{2}
or
\mathbb{Z}_{2}/\mathbf{Ab}
has a maximal splitting, and the components of a maximal splitting are unique up to isomorphism.
∎

3.2.
The categories
\mathbf{FP}_{+}
and
\mathbf{FP}_{-}

In this section we will identify
\mathbf{FP}_{+}
and
\mathbf{FP}_{-}
with
\mathbf{Ab}/\mathbb{Z}_{2}
and
\mathbb{Z}_{2}/\mathbf{Ab}
(up to equivalence and isomorphism, respectively).

Recall that in Definition
2.9
we defined the homomorphism
v_{Q}\colon SQ\to\mathbb{Z}_{2}
(induced by
{\rm h}
) if
Q
is a symmetric form parameter, and the homomorphism
v^{\prime}_{Q}\colon\mathbb{Z}_{2}\to Q_{e}
(induced by
{\rm p}
) if
Q
is an anti-symmetric form parameter.

Example 3.15
.

The following is the list of homomorphisms
v_{Q}
and
v^{\prime}_{Q}
for the form parameters listed in Definition
3.1
:

\displaystyle\underline{\quad\epsilon=+1\quad}

\displaystyle\underline{\quad\epsilon=-1\quad}

\displaystyle v_{Q_{+}}

\displaystyle\cong 0\colon 0\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}_{2}

\displaystyle v^{\prime}_{Q_{-}}

\displaystyle=\iota_{1}\colon\mathbb{Z}_{2}\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}_{2}

\displaystyle v_{\mathbb{Z}^{P}}

\displaystyle\cong 1_{\infty}\colon\mathbb{Z}\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}_{2}

\displaystyle v_{\mathbb{Z}^{P}_{k}}

\displaystyle\cong 1_{k+1}\colon\mathbb{Z}_{2^{k+1}}\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}_{2}\quad\quad\quad\quad

\displaystyle v^{\prime}_{\mathbb{Z}^{\Lambda}_{k}}

\displaystyle=\iota_{k}\colon\mathbb{Z}_{2}\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}_{2^{k}}

\displaystyle v_{Q^{+}}

\displaystyle\cong 1_{1}\colon\mathbb{Z}_{2}\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}_{2}

\displaystyle v^{\prime}_{Q^{-}}

\displaystyle=0\colon\mathbb{Z}_{2}\rightarrow 0

Remark 3.16
.

Note that if
P\cong Q\oplus G
is a splitting of a symmetric form parameter
P
, then we have
SP\cong SQ\oplus G
and
v_{P}\cong v_{Q}\oplus G\colon SP\cong SQ\oplus G\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}_{2}
. Similarly, if
P\cong Q\oplus G
is a splitting of an anti-symmetric form parameter
P
, then
v^{\prime}_{P}\cong v^{\prime}_{Q}\oplus G\colon\mathbb{Z}_{2}\rightarrow P_{e}\cong Q_{e}\oplus G
.

Remark 3.17
.

The height of a homomorphism to or from
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
can be defined in such a way that the height of a form parameter from Definition
3.4
is
equal to the height of its quasi-Wu class; we leave the details to the reader.

Proposition 3.18
.

The assignment
P\mapsto v_{P}
induces a functor
v_{(-)}\colon\mathbf{FP}_{+}\rightarrow\mathbf{Ab}/\mathbb{Z}_{2}
. Similarly, the assignment
P\mapsto v^{\prime}_{P}
induces a functor
v^{\prime}_{(-)}\colon\mathbf{FP}_{-}\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}_{2}/\mathbf{Ab}
.

Proof.

A morphism
\alpha\colon P\rightarrow P^{\prime}
of
symmetric
form parameters induces a homomorphism
S\alpha\colon SP\rightarrow SP^{\prime}
such that
v_{P^{\prime}}\circ S\alpha=v_{P}
, so we can set
v_{\alpha}=S\alpha
. Similarly, a morphism
\alpha\colon P\rightarrow P^{\prime}
of anti-symmetric
form parameters is a homomorphism
\alpha\colon P_{e}\rightarrow P^{\prime}_{e}
such that
v^{\prime}_{P^{\prime}}=\alpha\circ v^{\prime}_{P}
, so we can set
v^{\prime}_{\alpha}=\alpha
.
∎

Theorem 3.19
.

The functor
v^{\prime}_{(-)}\colon\mathbf{FP}_{-}\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}_{2}/\mathbf{Ab}
is an isomorphism of categories.

Proof.

To a finitely generated abelian group
G
with homomorphism
f\colon\mathbb{Z}_{2}\rightarrow G
(an object in
\mathbb{Z}_{2}/\mathbf{Ab}
) we assign the anti-symmetric form parameter
(G,0,{\rm p}_{f})
, where
{\rm p}_{f}(1)=f(1)
. A homomorphism
g\colon G\rightarrow G^{\prime}
which is a morphism between
f\colon\mathbb{Z}_{2}\rightarrow G
and
f^{\prime}\colon\mathbb{Z}_{2}\rightarrow G^{\prime}
(i.e.
g\circ f=f^{\prime}
) is also a morphism between
(G,0,{\rm p}_{f})
and
(G^{\prime},0,{\rm p}_{f^{\prime}})
. The functor defined this way is inverse to
v^{\prime}_{(-)}
.
∎

Theorem 3.20
.

The functor
v_{(-)}\colon\mathbf{FP}_{+}\rightarrow\mathbf{Ab}/\mathbb{Z}_{2}
is an equivalence of categories.

Proof.

By Proposition
3.10
b) every homomorphism
v\colon A\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}_{2}
is isomorphic to some
v_{0}\oplus C
where
v_{0}
is one of the homomorphisms listed in Example
3.9
and
C
is an abelian group. It follows from Example
3.15
that there is a symmetric form parameter
Q
such that
v_{0}\cong v_{Q}
. Then by Remark
3.16
we have
v\cong v_{0}\oplus C\cong v_{Q}\oplus C\cong v_{Q\oplus C}
. This shows that
v_{(-)}
is essentially surjective. By Lemma
3.21
below
v_{(-)}
is also full and faithful, hence it is an equivalence of categories.
∎

Lemma 3.21
.

For any pair of symmetric form parameters,
P
and
P^{\prime}
, the map
\textup{Hom}(P,P^{\prime})\rightarrow\textup{Hom}(v_{P},v_{P^{\prime}})
given by the functor
v_{(-)}
is a bijection.

Proof.

We will describe an inverse. Let
f\colon SP\rightarrow SP^{\prime}
be a morphism between
v_{P}
and
v_{P^{\prime}}
. Let
\pi_{P}\colon P_{e}\rightarrow SP
and
\pi_{P^{\prime}}\colon P^{\prime}_{e}\rightarrow SP^{\prime}
denote the quotient maps (see the diagram below).

\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathbb{Z}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{{\rm p}_{P}}
\textstyle{P_{e}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\alpha_{f}}
\scriptstyle{\pi_{P}}
\scriptstyle{{\rm h}_{P}}
\textstyle{SP\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f}
\scriptstyle{v_{P}}
\textstyle{0}
\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}

\textstyle{\mathbb{Z}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{2}
\textstyle{\mathbb{Z}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\varrho_{2}}
\textstyle{\mathbb{Z}_{2}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0}
\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathbb{Z}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{{\rm p}_{P^{\prime}}}
\textstyle{P^{\prime}_{e}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\pi_{P^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{{\rm h}_{P^{\prime}}}
\textstyle{SP^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{v{\vphantom{h}}_{P^{\prime}}}
\textstyle{0}

Since
\varrho_{2}\circ{\rm h}_{P}=v_{P}\circ\pi_{P}=v_{P^{\prime}}\circ f\circ\pi_{P}
, it follows from Lemma
2.10
(applied to
P^{\prime}
) that there is a unique homomorphism
\alpha_{f}\colon P_{e}\rightarrow P^{\prime}_{e}
such that
{\rm h}_{P^{\prime}}\circ\alpha_{f}={\rm h}_{P}
and
\pi_{P^{\prime}}\circ\alpha_{f}=f\circ\pi_{P}
. We have
{\rm h}_{P^{\prime}}\circ\alpha_{f}\circ{\rm p}_{P}={\rm h}_{P}\circ{\rm p}_{P}=2\textup{Id}_{\mathbb{Z}}={\rm h}_{P^{\prime}}\circ{\rm p}_{P^{\prime}}
and
\pi_{P^{\prime}}\circ\alpha_{f}\circ{\rm p}_{P}=f\circ\pi_{P}\circ{\rm p}_{P}=0=\pi_{P^{\prime}}\circ{\rm p}_{P^{\prime}}
, hence
\alpha_{f}\circ{\rm p}_{P}={\rm p}_{P^{\prime}}
. Therefore
\alpha_{f}
is a morphism between
P
and
P^{\prime}
.

Since
S\alpha_{f}=f
for any morphism
f
between
v_{P}
and
v_{P^{\prime}}
, and
\alpha_{S\beta}=\beta
for any morphism
\beta\colon P\rightarrow P^{\prime}
, the assignment
f\mapsto\alpha_{f}
is the inverse of the map
\textup{Hom}(P,P^{\prime})\rightarrow\textup{Hom}(v_{P},v_{P^{\prime}})
given by the functor
v_{(-)}
, therefore both of them are bijections.
∎

Proposition 3.22
.

A symmetric (resp. anti-symmetric) form parameter
P
is indecomposable if and only if
v_{P}
(resp.
v^{\prime}_{P}
) is indecomposable.

Proof.

It follows from Remark
3.16
that if
P
is decomposable, then
v_{P}
(resp.
v^{\prime}_{P}
) is decomposable too. On the other hand, if
v_{P}
(resp.
v^{\prime}_{P}
) has a splitting
v_{P}\cong v_{0}\oplus C
(resp.
v^{\prime}_{P}\cong v^{\prime}_{0}\oplus C
), then it follows from Theorem
3.20
(resp.
3.19
) and Remark
3.16
that
v_{0}\cong v_{Q}
(resp.
v^{\prime}_{0}\cong v^{\prime}_{Q}
) for some
Q
, so
v_{P}\cong v_{Q}\oplus C\cong v_{Q\oplus C}
(resp.
v^{\prime}_{P}\cong v^{\prime}_{Q}\oplus C\cong v^{\prime}_{Q\oplus C}
) and hence
P\cong Q\oplus C
.
∎

Proof of Theorem
3.3
.

By Theorems
3.20
and
3.19
the functors
v_{(-)}
and
v^{\prime}_{(-)}
are both equivalences of categories. By Example
3.15
they send the form parameters of Definition
3.1
to the homomorphisms in Example
3.9
, and they preserve splittings and indecomposability (see Proposition
3.22
). Therefore all statements follow immediately from the corresponding parts of Theorem
3.14
.
∎

3.3.
The category of indecomposable form parameters

Now we consider morphisms between form parameters. We will give an explicit description of the morphisms between indecomposable form parameters, equivalently, of the full subcategory of indecomposable form parameters. In the symmetric case we will use Theorem
3.20
to first consider the morphisms in
\mathbf{Ab}/\mathbb{Z}_{2}
, and then take the corresponding morphisms between form parameters.

Definition 3.23

(Standard morphisms)
.

Consider the diagram

\textstyle{\mathbb{Z}\,\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\left(\!\begin{smallmatrix}2\\
-1\end{smallmatrix}\!\right)}
\textstyle{\mathbb{Z}\oplus\mathbb{Z}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}

\textstyle{\mathbb{Z}}

(where we have a homomorphism
\left(\begin{smallmatrix}1&0\\
n&(2n+1)\end{smallmatrix}\right)\colon\mathbb{Z}\oplus\mathbb{Z}\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}\oplus\mathbb{Z}
for every
n\in\mathbb{Z}
). It determines a diagram

\textstyle{Q_{+}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}

\textstyle{Q^{+}}

of indecomposable symmetric form parameters and morphisms. Similarly, the diagram

\textstyle{\mathbb{Z}_{2}\,\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}

\scriptstyle{2}

\textstyle{\ldots\vphantom{\mathbb{Z}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0}

determines a diagram

\textstyle{Q_{-}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathbb{Z}^{\Lambda}_{2}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}

of indecomposable anti-symmetric form parameters and morphisms. The morphisms between the form parameters in these two diagrams will be called
standard morphisms
.

Theorem 3.24
.

Every morphism
\alpha\colon P\rightarrow P^{\prime}
between the indecomposable form parameters
P=(P_{e},{\rm h},{\rm p})
and
P^{\prime}=(P^{\prime}_{e},{\rm h}^{\prime},{\rm p}^{\prime})
is the composition of standard morphisms and isomorphisms.

Proof.

First we consider the symmetric case. By Theorem
3.20
it is enough to prove the analogous statement in
\mathbf{Ab}/\mathbb{Z}_{2}
. Using the bijection from Lemma
3.21
, we need to consider the diagram

\textstyle{0\,\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathbb{Z}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{2n+1}
\scriptstyle{1}
\scriptstyle{1}
\scriptstyle{1}
\textstyle{\,\,\vphantom{\mathbb{Z}^{2}_{2}}\ldots\,\,\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathbb{Z}_{2^{4}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{1}
\textstyle{\mathbb{Z}_{2^{3}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{1}
\textstyle{\mathbb{Z}_{2^{2}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{1}
\textstyle{\mathbb{Z}_{2}}

(where we have a homomorphism
2n{+}1\colon\mathbb{Z}\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}
for every
n\in\mathbb{Z}
), which determines a diagram

\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{1_{\infty}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{}\vphantom{\mathbb{Z}}\ldots\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{1_{4}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{1_{3}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{1_{2}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{1_{1}}

between the standard indecomposable homomorphisms in
\mathbf{Ab}/\mathbb{Z}_{2}
. We will show that any morphism
v_{1}\rightarrow v_{2}
between indecomposable homomorphisms in
\mathbf{Ab}/\mathbb{Z}_{2}
is the composition of some morphisms in the above diagram and isomorphisms.

This is obvious if
v_{1}
is initial (isomorphic to
0\colon 0\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}_{2}
), so we will assume that
v_{1}\not\cong 0
. Then
v_{1}
is surjective, so
v_{2}
is also surjective. Therefore both
v_{1}
and
v_{2}
are isomorphic to either
1_{\infty}
or
1_{k}
for some
k\geq 1
.

If
v_{1}
and
v_{2}
are both isomorphic to
1_{\infty}
, then we can fix isomorphisms
v_{1}\cong 1_{\infty}
and
v_{2}\cong 1_{\infty}
. Under these identifications the morphism
v_{1}\rightarrow v_{2}
corresponds to a homomorphism
\mathbb{Z}\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}
that is compatible with the surjective map
1_{\infty}\colon\mathbb{Z}\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}_{2}
, equivalently, it is given by multiplication with some odd integer
2n+1
.

If
v_{1}\cong 1_{k}
for some
k\geq 1
and
v_{2}\cong 1_{l}
for some
l>k
(or
v_{2}\cong 1_{\infty}
), then the morphism
v_{1}\rightarrow v_{2}
corresponds to a homomorphism
\mathbb{Z}_{2^{k}}\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}_{2^{l}}
(or
\mathbb{Z}_{2^{k}}\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}
). The image of any such homomorphism is contained in
\mathrm{Ker}(v_{2})
, but
v_{1}\neq 0
, showing that no morphism
v_{1}\rightarrow v_{2}
can exist in this case.

If
v_{1}\cong 1_{k}
for some
k\geq 1
(or
v_{1}\cong 1_{\infty}
) and
v_{2}\cong 1_{l}
for some
l\leq k
, then the morphism
v_{1}\rightarrow v_{2}
corresponds to a homomorphism
\mathbb{Z}_{2^{k}}\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}_{2^{l}}
(or
\mathbb{Z}\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}_{2^{l}}
) compatible with the maps
1_{k}
(or
1_{\infty}
) and
1_{l}
. Equivalently, this homomorphism sends
1
to some odd
p\in\mathbb{Z}_{2^{l}}
, so it is the composition of the standard map
1\colon\mathbb{Z}_{2^{k}}\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}_{2^{l}}
(or
1\colon\mathbb{Z}\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}_{2^{l}}
) and the automorphism of
\mathbb{Z}_{2^{l}}
given by multiplication with
p
.

Now consider the anti-symmetric case. Again, the statement is obvious if
P
is initial (isomorphic to
Q_{-}
) or
P^{\prime}
is terminal (isomorphic to
Q^{-}
). So we assume that
P\not\cong Q_{-}
(so
{\rm p}(1)
is divisible by
2
, therefore
{\rm p}^{\prime}(1)
is divisible by
2
, hence
P^{\prime}\not\cong Q_{-}
) and
P^{\prime}\not\cong Q^{-}
(which means that
{\rm p}^{\prime}\neq 0
, so
{\rm p}\neq 0
, therefore
P\not\cong Q^{-}
).

If
P\cong\mathbb{Z}^{\Lambda}_{k}
and
P^{\prime}\cong\mathbb{Z}^{\Lambda}_{l}
with
k>l
, then
{\rm p}(1)
is divisible by
2^{k-1}\geq 2^{l}
, so the same is true for
{\rm p}^{\prime}(1)
, so
{\rm p}^{\prime}(1)=0
. This contradiction shows that
\mathbb{Z}^{\Lambda}_{k}\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}^{\Lambda}_{l}
morphisms with
k>l
do not exist.

If
P\cong\mathbb{Z}^{\Lambda}_{k}
and
P^{\prime}\cong\mathbb{Z}^{\Lambda}_{l}
with
k\leq l
, then
\alpha
corresponds to a homomorphism
\mathbb{Z}_{2^{k}}\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}_{2^{l}}
that sends
{\rm p}(1)=2^{k-1}
to
{\rm p}^{\prime}(1)=2^{l-1}
. This means that
1\in\mathbb{Z}_{2^{k}}
has to be sent into
2^{l-k}p
for some odd
p\in\mathbb{Z}_{2^{l}}
. Since multiplication by
p
is an isomorphism of
