\mathbb{Z}^{\Lambda}_{l}
, we can describe
\alpha
as the composition of the inclusion
2^{l-k}\colon\mathbb{Z}_{2^{k}}\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}_{2^{l}}
(which is the composition of
(l-k)
standard morphisms) and
3
isomorphisms.
∎

Definition 3.25
.

{\rm Aut}(Q)\subseteq{\rm Aut}(Q_{e})
is the group of automorphisms of the form parameter
Q
.

Theorem 3.26
.

The symmetric form parameters
Q_{+}
and
Q^{+}
have no non-trivial automorphisms.
In contrast,
{\rm Aut}(\mathbb{Z}^{P})=\mathbb{Z}_{2}(\beta)
(i.e. it is a group isomorphic to
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
, generated by
\beta
) and

{\rm Aut}(\mathbb{Z}^{P}_{k})\cong\begin{cases}\mathbb{Z}_{2}(\beta_{k})&\text{if $k=1$}\\
\mathbb{Z}_{2}(\beta_{k})\oplus\mathbb{Z}_{2^{k-1}}(\gamma_{k})&\text{if $k\geq 2$,}\end{cases}

where the generators are

\beta=\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\
-1&-1\end{pmatrix}\in{\rm Aut}(\mathbb{Z}\oplus\mathbb{Z})\text{\,,}\quad\beta_{k}=\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\
-1&-1\end{pmatrix}\in{\rm Aut}(\mathbb{Z}\oplus\mathbb{Z}_{2^{k}})\quad\text{and}\quad\gamma_{k}=\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\
1&3\end{pmatrix}\in{\rm Aut}(\mathbb{Z}\oplus\mathbb{Z}_{2^{k}}).

In the anti-symmetric case
Q_{-}
and
Q^{-}
have no non-trivial automorphisms and for
k\geq 2

{\rm Aut}(\mathbb{Z}^{\Lambda}_{k})\cong{\rm Aut}(\mathbb{Z}_{2^{k}})\cong\begin{cases}\mathbb{Z}_{2}(-1)&\text{if $k=2$,}\\
\mathbb{Z}_{2}(-1)\oplus\mathbb{Z}_{2^{k-2}}(3)&\text{if $k\geq 3$.}\end{cases}

Proof.

Initial and terminal objects do not have non-trivial automorphisms.

In the remaining anti-symmetric case, an automorphism of
\mathbb{Z}^{\Lambda}_{k}
is an automorphism of the group
\mathbb{Z}_{2^{k}}
that preserves
{\rm p}(1)
, the order-
2
element. Since this is true for all automorphisms of
\mathbb{Z}_{2^{k}}
, we get that
{\rm Aut}(\mathbb{Z}^{\Lambda}_{k})\cong{\rm Aut}(\mathbb{Z}_{2^{k}})
. This is isomorphic to
\mathbb{Z}_{2^{k}}^{\times}
, the multiplicative group of odd elements of
\mathbb{Z}_{2^{k}}
, which is
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
if
k=2
and
\mathbb{Z}_{2}\oplus\mathbb{Z}_{2^{k-2}}
(generated by
-1
and
3
) if
k\geq 3
.

Now we consider the symmetric case. By Theorem
3.20
we have
{\rm Aut}(P)\cong{\rm Aut}(v_{P})
. We start with
{\rm Aut}(\mathbb{Z}^{P})\cong{\rm Aut}(v_{\mathbb{Z}^{P}})\cong{\rm Aut}(1_{\infty})
. From the proof of Theorem
3.24
we see that the monoid of morphisms
1_{\infty}\rightarrow 1_{\infty}
is isomorphic to the multiplicative monoid of odd integers. The group of automorphisms of
1_{\infty}
is the group of invertible elements in this monoid, so it is isomorphic to
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
, generated by
-1
. The generator corresponds to the matrix
\beta
under the bijection of Lemma
3.21
.

Next we consider
{\rm Aut}(\mathbb{Z}^{P}_{k})\cong{\rm Aut}(1_{k+1})
. As we saw in the proof of Theorem
3.24
, this group is isomorphic to
{\rm Aut}(\mathbb{Z}_{2^{k+1}})\cong\mathbb{Z}_{2^{k+1}}^{\times}
. The generator
-1\in\mathbb{Z}_{2^{k+1}}^{\times}
corresponds to
\beta_{k}
, and (if
k\geq 2
)
3
corresponds to
\gamma_{k}
.
∎

4.
Witt groups of split form parameters

In this section we study the Witt groups
W_{0}(P)
of form parameters
P
. First we compute the Witt groups of indecomposable form parameters and the homomorphisms induced by morphisms of such form parameters, giving a complete description of the Witt group functor restricted to the subcategory of indecomposable form parameters.
Then, given a split form parameter
P=Q\oplus G
, where
Q
is a form parameter and
G
is an abelian group, we use the fact that the Witt group
W_{0}(Q\oplus G)
decomposes as the direct sum of
W_{0}(Q)
and the reduced Witt group
W_{0}^{Q}(G)
. We prove that
W_{0}^{Q}(G)
is naturally isomorphic to Baues’ quadratic tensor product
G\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}Q

[
Bau1
, §4]
, and by our earlier classification of form parameters this allows us to determine the Witt group
W_{0}(P)
for any form parameter
P
.
Finally, we give some examples of computations of Witt groups and
induced homomorphisms between Witt groups.

4.1.
Witt groups of indecomposable form parameters

Let
Q
be an indecomposable form parameter.
Recall that by Theorem
3.3
, Definition
3.1
gives a complete list of indecomposable form parameters (up to isomorphism). First we will define the invariants that classify the elements of the groups
W_{0}(Q)
.

For a symmetric form parameter
Q
the signature induces a homomorphism

\sigma\colon W_{0}(Q)\to\mathbb{Z}.

Recall that for any symmetric form parameter
Q

there is an extended symmetrisation map

\textup{es}\colon Q\rightarrow Q^{+}\oplus SQ
(see Definition
2.14
). It induces a homomorphism

\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathbb{Z}}(X,\textup{es})\colon\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathbb{Z}}(X,Q)\rightarrow\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathbb{Z}}(X,Q^{+}\oplus SQ)
for any abelian group
X
, which sends
(X,\lambda,\mu)
to
(X,\lambda,(\mu_{0},\omega_{0}))
, where
\mu_{0}(x)=\lambda(x,x)
and
v_{Q}\circ\omega_{0}\colon X\to\mathbb{Z}_{2}
is characteristic for
\lambda
, i.e.
\varrho_{2}\circ\lambda(x,x)=v_{Q}\circ\omega_{0}(x)
(see Lemma
2.29
). We will use this map
\omega_{0}
(more precisely a lift
\omega\colon X\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}
of
\omega_{0}
) to define an invariant of
Q
-forms for
Q=\mathbb{Z}^{P}
and
Q=\mathbb{Z}^{P}_{k}
.

If
Q=\mathbb{Z}^{P}
, then
v_{\mathbb{Z}^{P}}=1_{\infty}=\varrho_{2}\colon S\mathbb{Z}^{P}\cong\mathbb{Z}\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}_{2}
, and we define
\omega=\omega_{0}
, it satisfies

\lambda(x,x)\equiv\omega(x)\text{${\rm~mod}~2$.}

If
\lambda
is nonsingular with adjoint
\widehat{\lambda}
, then for the element
\widehat{\omega}:=\widehat{\lambda}^{-1}(\omega)\in X
it is well known that
\widehat{\omega}^{2}:=\lambda(\widehat{\omega},\widehat{\omega})\in\mathbb{Z}
is congruent to
\sigma(\lambda)
mod
8
(see e.g.
[
M-H
, II/§5]
).
Recall that
\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathbb{Z}}^{\rm ns}(Q)
denotes the set of isomorphism classes of nonsingular
Q
-forms.

Definition 4.1
.

We define the map
\rho_{\infty}\colon\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathbb{Z}}^{\rm ns}(\mathbb{Z}^{P})\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}
by

\rho_{\infty}(X,\lambda,\mu)=\frac{\sigma(\lambda)-\widehat{\omega}^{2}}{8}.

It is easy to see that this is a monoid homomorphism, and that
\rho_{\infty}
vanishes on metabolic forms, so it induces a homomorphism
\rho_{\infty}\colon W_{0}(\mathbb{Z}^{P})\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}
.

If
Q=\mathbb{Z}^{P}_{k}
, then
S\mathbb{Z}^{P}_{k}\cong\mathbb{Z}_{2^{k+1}}
and
v_{\mathbb{Z}^{P}_{k}}=1_{k+1}\colon\mathbb{Z}_{2^{k+1}}\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}_{2}
, so we have
\lambda(x,x)\equiv\omega_{0}(x)
mod
2
. Now lift
\omega_{0}
to a homomorphism
\omega\colon X\to\mathbb{Z}
, then
\lambda(x,x)\equiv\omega(x)
mod
2
, and consider
\widehat{\omega}^{2}
. Again we have
\widehat{\omega}^{2}\equiv\sigma(\lambda)
mod
8
, and it is easy to check that
\widehat{\omega}^{2}
mod
2^{k+2}
is independent of the choice of the lift
\omega
.

Definition 4.2
.

We define the map
\rho_{k}\colon\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathbb{Z}}^{\rm ns}(\mathbb{Z}^{P}_{k})\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}_{2^{k-1}}
by

\rho_{k}(X,\lambda,\mu)=\frac{\sigma(\lambda)-\widehat{\omega}^{2}}{8}\text{ mod $2^{k-1}$}.

It induces a homomorphism
\rho_{k}\colon W_{0}(\mathbb{Z}^{P}_{k})\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}_{2^{k-1}}
.

In the anti-symmetric case we will denote the Arf invariant by

c\colon W_{0}(Q_{-})\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}_{2}.

The following result entails and extends Theorem
1.1
.

Theorem 4.3
.

Applying the functor
W_{0}
to the diagrams in Definition
3.23
gives the following diagrams of abelian groups, where we use the duals of the invariants detecting each summand to name generators:

\textstyle{\mathbb{Z}(8\sigma^{*})\,\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\left(\!\begin{smallmatrix}8\\
1\end{smallmatrix}\!\right)}
\textstyle{\mathbb{Z}(\sigma^{*})\oplus\mathbb{Z}(\rho_{\infty}^{*})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\left(\!\begin{smallmatrix}1&0\\
-n(n+1)/2&(2n+1)^{2}\end{smallmatrix}\!\right)}
\scriptstyle{\left(\!\begin{smallmatrix}1&0\\
0&1\end{smallmatrix}\!\right)}
\scriptstyle{\left(\!\begin{smallmatrix}1&0\\
0&1\end{smallmatrix}\!\right)}
\scriptstyle{(1,0)}
\textstyle{\ldots\vphantom{\mathbb{Z}^{2}_{2}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathbb{Z}(\sigma^{*})\oplus\mathbb{Z}_{2^{2}}(\rho_{3}^{*})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\left(\!\begin{smallmatrix}1&0\\
0&1\end{smallmatrix}\!\right)}
\textstyle{\mathbb{Z}(\sigma^{*})\oplus\mathbb{Z}_{2}(\rho_{2}^{*})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{(1,0)}
\textstyle{\mathbb{Z}(\sigma^{*})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{1}
\textstyle{\mathbb{Z}(\sigma^{*})}

\textstyle{\mathbb{Z}_{2}(c^{*})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\ldots\vphantom{\mathbb{Z}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0}

In the symmetric case, the generators of the automorphism groups of the form parameters (see Theorem
3.26
) induce the following automorphisms of the Witt groups:
W_{0}(\beta)={\rm Id}
,
W_{0}(\beta_{k})={\rm Id}
and
W_{0}(\gamma_{k})=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}1&0\\
-1&9\end{smallmatrix}\right)
.

Proof.

First we consider the Witt groups in the symmetric case. It is a classical result that taking the signature of a symmetric form defines an isomorphism
\sigma\colon W_{0}(Q^{+})\cong\mathbb{Z}
and that
W_{0}(Q_{+})\to W_{0}(Q^{+})
is injective with image the subgroup of index
8
; see, for example,
[
M-H
, II Corollary 4.4, Lemma 5.2 & Lemma 6.2]
.
By
[
Wa1
, Theorem 2, (2)]
we have
W_{0}(\mathbb{Z}^{P})\cong\mathbb{Z}\oplus\mathbb{Z}
, where the isomorphism is given by the signature
\sigma\colon W_{0}(\mathbb{Z}^{P})\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}
and
\rho_{\infty}
.

The standard morphism
\mathbb{Z}^{P}\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}^{P}_{k}
given by
(\textup{Id},\varrho_{2^{k}})\colon\mathbb{Z}\oplus\mathbb{Z}\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}\oplus\mathbb{Z}_{2^{k}}
induces a homomorphism
W_{0}(\mathbb{Z}^{P})\rightarrow W_{0}(\mathbb{Z}^{P}_{k})
that commutes with the signature maps. It follows from the definitions of
\rho_{\infty}
and
\rho_{k}
that the diagram

\textstyle{W_{0}(\mathbb{Z}^{P})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\rho_{\infty}}
\textstyle{W_{0}(\mathbb{Z}^{P}_{k})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\rho_{k}}
\textstyle{\mathbb{Z}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\varrho_{2^{k-1}}}
\textstyle{\mathbb{Z}_{2^{k-1}}}

commutes.
This implies that
\mathrm{Ker}(W_{0}(\mathbb{Z}^{P})\rightarrow W_{0}(\mathbb{Z}^{P}_{k}))\leq\mathrm{Ker}((\textup{Id}_{\mathbb{Z}}\oplus\varrho_{2^{k-1}})\circ(\sigma\oplus\rho_{\infty}))=(\sigma\oplus\rho_{\infty})^{-1}(0\oplus 2^{k-1}\mathbb{Z})
.
Now consider the
\mathbb{Z}^{P}_{k}
-form
\underline{\phi}
and
\mathbb{Z}^{P}
-form
\underline{\phi}^{\prime}
:

\underline{\phi}=\left(\mathbb{Z}^{2}(x,y),\left[\begin{array}[]{cc}1&1\\
1&0\end{array}\right],\left(\begin{array}[]{c}(1,2^{k-1})\\
(0,0)\end{array}\right)\right)\quad\quad\underline{\phi}^{\prime}=\left(\mathbb{Z}^{2}(x,y),\left[\begin{array}[]{cc}1&1\\
1&0\end{array}\right],\left(\begin{array}[]{c}(1,2^{k-1})\\
(0,2^{k})\end{array}\right)\right).

For
\underline{\phi}^{\prime}
we have
\omega=(1+2^{k},2^{k+1})
,
\widehat{\omega}=\bigl(\begin{smallmatrix}2^{k+1}\\
1-2^{k}\end{smallmatrix}\bigr)
and
\widehat{\omega}^{2}=2^{k+2}
, so
(\sigma\oplus\rho_{\infty})([\underline{\phi}^{\prime}])=(0,-2^{k-1})
.
The image of
[\underline{\phi}^{\prime}]
in
W_{0}(\mathbb{Z}^{P}_{k})
is
[\underline{\phi}]=0
, because
\underline{\phi}
is metabolic, showing that
\mathrm{Ker}(W_{0}(\mathbb{Z}^{P})\rightarrow W_{0}(\mathbb{Z}^{P}_{k}))=(\sigma\oplus\rho_{\infty})^{-1}(0\oplus 2^{k-1}\mathbb{Z})
. Finally, by Lemma
2.29
a
\mathbb{Z}^{P}_{k}
-form corresponds to some
(Q^{+}\oplus\mathbb{Z}_{2^{k+1}})
-form
(X,\lambda,(\mu_{0},\omega_{0}))
such that
\varrho_{2}\circ\omega_{0}\colon X\to\mathbb{Z}_{2}
is characteristic for
\lambda
. For any lift
\omega\colon X\to\mathbb{Z}
of
\omega_{0}
,
\varrho_{2}\circ\omega
is also characteristic for
\lambda
, so
(X,\lambda,(\mu_{0},\omega))
corresponds to a
\mathbb{Z}^{P}
-form. Its image is the
\mathbb{Z}^{P}_{k}
-form we started with, showing that the map
W_{0}(\mathbb{Z}^{P})\rightarrow W_{0}(\mathbb{Z}^{P}_{k})
is surjective. Therefore
W_{0}(\mathbb{Z}^{P}_{k})\cong W_{0}(\mathbb{Z}^{P})/\mathrm{Ker}(W_{0}(\mathbb{Z}^{P})\rightarrow W_{0}(\mathbb{Z}^{P}_{k}))\cong(\mathbb{Z}\oplus\mathbb{Z})/(0\oplus 2^{k-1}\mathbb{Z})=\mathbb{Z}\oplus\mathbb{Z}_{2^{k-1}}
, and the isomorphism is given by
(\sigma\oplus\rho_{k})
.

In the anti-symmetric case it is a classical result that the Arf invariant defines an isomorphism
W_{0}(Q_{-})\cong\mathbb{Z}_{2}
(see
[
K-M
]
), and that
W_{0}(Q^{-})\cong 0
(as every
Q^{-}
-form is hyperbolic). It remains to show that
W_{0}(\mathbb{Z}^{\Lambda}_{k})\cong 0
if
k>1
. This is a matter of a small number of simple calculations. Firstly, recall that every nonsingular anti-symmetric form
(X,\lambda)
splits as a direct sum of hyperbolic forms and thus every nonsingular
\mathbb{Z}^{\Lambda}_{k}
-form splits as a sum of rank
2
forms. Thus it is enough to show that every rank
2
form is stably metabolic. So consider a
\mathbb{Z}^{\Lambda}_{k}
-form

(X,\lambda,\mu)=\left(\mathbb{Z}^{2}(x,y),\left[\begin{array}[]{cc}0&1\\
-1&0\end{array}\right],\left(\begin{array}[]{c}p\\
q\end{array}\right)\right),

where
p,q\in\mathbb{Z}_{2^{k}}
. Suppose to begin that one of
p
or
q
is odd; given the symmetry of the form, say that
p
is odd. Since
p
is odd and
k>1
,
p+2^{k-1}
is again odd and so there is an integer
a
such that
a(p+2^{k-1})\equiv-q~{\rm mod}~2^{k}
. It follows that the summand generated by
ax+y
is a lagrangian since

\mu(ax+y)=ap+q+a2^{k-1}=0\in\mathbb{Z}_{2^{k}}.

For the general case one checks that there is an isometry

\left(\mathbb{Z}^{2}(x,y),\left[\begin{array}[]{cc}0&1\\
-1&0\end{array}\right],\left(\begin{array}[]{c}p\\
q\end{array}\right)\right)\oplus\left(\mathbb{Z}^{2}(w,z),\left[\begin{array}[]{cc}0&1\\
-1&0\end{array}\right],\left(\begin{array}[]{c}1\\
0\end{array}\right)\right)\cong

\left(\mathbb{Z}^{2}(x-pw,y),\left[\begin{array}[]{cc}0&1\\
-1&0\end{array}\right],\left(\begin{array}[]{c}0\\
q\end{array}\right)\right)\oplus\left(\mathbb{Z}^{2}(w,z+py),\left[\begin{array}[]{cc}0&1\\
-1&0\end{array}\right],\left(\begin{array}[]{c}1\\
pq\end{array}\right)\right).

By the previous case, the second summand in the bottom line is metabolic and so the form

(X,\lambda,\mu)
above represents zero in
W_{0}(\mathbb{Z}^{\Lambda}_{k})
.

Next we will determine the image of the morphisms under the functor
W_{0}
. First consider the morphism
\alpha\colon Q_{+}\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}^{P}
given by
\left(\begin{smallmatrix}2\\
-1\end{smallmatrix}\right)\colon\mathbb{Z}\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}\oplus\mathbb{Z}
. Let
(X,\lambda,\mu)
be a representative of the generator of
W_{0}(Q_{+})\cong\mathbb{Z}
with
\sigma(\lambda)=8
. Its image under
W_{0}(\alpha)
is represented by
(X,\lambda,\mu^{\prime})
, where
\mu^{\prime}=\alpha\circ\mu
. For this
\mu^{\prime}
we have
\omega^{\prime}=\pi_{\mathbb{Z}^{P}}\circ\mu^{\prime}=\pi_{\mathbb{Z}^{P}}\circ\alpha\circ\mu=S\alpha\circ\pi_{Q_{+}}\circ\mu=0
, because
SQ_{+}=0
. Hence
\widehat{\omega}^{\prime}=0
and
\rho_{\infty}([X,\lambda,\mu^{\prime}])=\frac{\sigma(\lambda)-(\widehat{\omega}^{\prime})^{2}}{8}=1
. Therefore
W_{0}(\alpha)=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}8\\
1\end{smallmatrix}\right)
.

Now let
\alpha\colon\mathbb{Z}^{P}_{k}\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}^{P}_{l}
be a morphism, where we use the notation
\mathbb{Z}^{P}=\mathbb{Z}^{P}_{\infty}
. In Theorems
3.24
and
3.26
we saw that such a morphism exists only if
k\geq l
, and it is given by a matrix
\left(\begin{smallmatrix}1&0\\
n&(2n+1)\end{smallmatrix}\right)
for some
n\in\mathbb{Z}_{2^{l}}
or
n\in\mathbb{Z}
(if
k=l=\infty
). Let
(X,\lambda,\mu)
be a form over
\mathbb{Z}^{P}_{k}
, and define
\omega_{0}
,
\omega
and
\widehat{\omega}
as in Definition
4.2
(or
4.1
). Its image under
W_{0}(\alpha)
is represented by
(X,\lambda,\mu^{\prime})
, where
\mu^{\prime}=\alpha\circ\mu
. Then
\omega_{0}^{\prime}=\pi_{\mathbb{Z}^{P}_{l}}\circ\mu^{\prime}=S\alpha\circ\pi_{\mathbb{Z}^{P}_{k}}\circ\mu=S\alpha\circ\omega_{0}
, and the map
S\alpha\colon S\mathbb{Z}^{P}_{k}\rightarrow S\mathbb{Z}^{P}_{l}
is given by
S\alpha(1)=2n+1
. So we can choose the lift
\omega^{\prime}=(2n+1)\omega
, and then
\widehat{\omega}^{\prime}=(2n+1)\widehat{\omega}
. So

\rho_{l}(X,\lambda,\mu^{\prime})=\frac{\sigma(\lambda)-(\widehat{\omega}^{\prime})^{2}}{8}=\frac{\sigma(\lambda)-(2n+1)^{2}\widehat{\omega}^{2}}{8}=(2n+1)^{2}\frac{\sigma(\lambda)-\widehat{\omega}^{2}}{8}-\frac{n(n+1)}{2}\sigma(\lambda)

Therefore
W_{0}(\alpha)=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}1&0\\
-\frac{n(n+1)}{2}&(2n+1)^{2}\end{smallmatrix}\right)
.
∎

Remark 4.4
.

Note that for the morphisms
i_{k}\colon Q_{-}\hookrightarrow\mathbb{Z}^{\Lambda}_{k}
(
k\geq 2
) given by the injective homomorphisms
\iota_{k}\colon\mathbb{Z}_{2}\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}_{2^{k}}
, the induced maps
W_{0}(i_{k})
are not injective. This corresponds to the following fact: if
(X,\lambda,\mu)
is a
(Q_{-})
-form with Arf invariant
1
then
(X,\lambda,\iota_{k}\circ\mu)
is a stably metabolic
\mathbb{Z}^{\Lambda}_{k}
-form that is not metabolic.

The same phenomenon occurs in the symmetric case,
and we are grateful to a referee for pointing out the following example. The
\mathbb{Z}^{P}_{1}
-form given by

\left(\mathbb{Z}^{2},\left[\begin{array}[]{cc}0&1\\
1&0\end{array}\right],\left(\begin{array}[]{c}(0,1)\\
(0,1)\end{array}\right)\right)

has signature zero,
and so it is stably metabolic by Theorem
4.3
.
But, by inspection, it is not metabolic.

4.2.
The quadratic tensor product

We briefly recall the definition and basic properties of the quadratic tensor product (see Baues
[
Bau1
, §4]
and
[
Bau2
, §8]
), which we will use in Section
4.3
to compute reduced Witt groups.

4.2.1.
Definitions

We will introduce the quadratic tensor product in the more general context of quadratic modules.

Definition 4.5
.

A quadratic module (over
\mathbb{Z}
) is a pair of abelian groups
Q_{ee}
and
Q_{e}
with homomorphisms
{\rm h}\colon Q_{e}\rightarrow Q_{ee}
and
{\rm p}\colon Q_{ee}\rightarrow Q_{e}
such that
{\rm hph}=2{\rm h}
and
{\rm php}=2{\rm p}
.

In particular, a form parameter is a quadratic module with
Q_{ee}=\mathbb{Z}
. The definition of an extended quadratic form over
Q
, the group
\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathbb{Z}}(X,Q)
, and a morphism between form parameters can be generalised to quadratic modules
Q
in the obvious way.

Definition 4.6
.

Let
G
be an abelian group and
Q
a quadratic module over
\mathbb{Z}
.
The
quadratic tensor product
of
G
and
Q
is the abelian group

G\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}Q
which is generated by symbols of the form

g\otimes q\quad\text{and}\quad[g,h]\otimes a,

where
g,h\in G
,
q\in Q_{e}
and
a\in Q_{ee}
.
These symbols satisfy the following relations:

•

(g+h)\otimes q=g\otimes q+h\otimes q+[g,h]\otimes{\rm h}(q)
,

•

[g,g]\otimes a=g\otimes{\rm p}(a)

and
g\otimes q
is linear in
q
and
[g,h]\otimes a
is linear in each variable
g
,
h
and
a
.

It immediately follows from the defining relations that
[g,h]\otimes a+[h,g]\otimes a=[g,h]\otimes{\rm h}({\rm p}(a))
, so if
Q
is a form parameter (i.e.
Q_{ee}=\mathbb{Z}
), then
[h,g]\otimes a=\epsilon_{Q}[g,h]\otimes a
.

The quadratic tensor product is a functor in both
G
and
Q
, i.e. a homomorphism

f\colon G\to H
and a morphism
\alpha\colon Q\to P
induce homomorphisms

f_{*}\colon G\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}Q\to H\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}Q
and
\alpha_{*}\colon G\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}Q\to G\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}P
.

Example 4.7
.

By Baues
[
Bau2
, (8.16)]
for certain indecomposable form parameters
Q
the quadratic tensor product
G\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}Q
can be expressed (up to natural isomorphism) as a well-known quadratic functor of
G
:

Q

G\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}Q

Q_{+}

S^{2}(G)=G\otimes G/\{g\otimes h-h\otimes g\}

\mathbb{Z}^{P}

P^{2}(G)=\Delta(G)/\Delta(G)^{3}

Q^{+}

\Gamma(G)

Q_{-}

\Lambda_{1}(G)=G\otimes G/\{g\otimes h+h\otimes g\}

Q^{-}

\Lambda(G)=G\otimes G/\{g\otimes g\}

Here
\Gamma(G)
denotes Whitehead’s universal quadratic functor and
\Delta(G)
is the augmentation ideal in the group ring
\mathbb{Z}[G]
and
\Delta(G)^{3}
its third power.

4.2.2.
Computing the quadratic tensor product and
\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathbb{Z}}

Next we construct short exact sequences and pullback (or pushout) diagrams which can be used to determine the quadratic tensor product
G\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}Q
and
\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathbb{Z}}(G,Q)
for a symmetric (or anti-symmetric) form parameter
Q
.

Remark 4.8
.

We are going to make use of the following fact, which is easily verified by diagram chasing. Let

\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{A_{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\alpha}
\textstyle{B_{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\beta}
\textstyle{C_{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\gamma}
\textstyle{0}
\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{A_{2}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{B_{2}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{C_{2}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0}

be a commutative diagram between short exact sequences of abelian groups. If
\alpha
is an isomorphism, then the second square is both a pullback and pushout square. If
\gamma
is an isomorphism, then the first square is both a pullback and pushout square.

Definition 4.9
.

For an abelian group
A
we define the quadratic module

I(A):=(A\rightarrow 0\rightarrow A).

Note that
I
defines a functor, and for any abelian group
G
we have isomorphisms
G\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}I(A)\cong G\otimes A
and
\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathbb{Z}}(G,I(A))\cong\textup{Hom}(G,A)
.

Proposition 4.10
.

For every symmetric form parameter
Q=(Q_{e},{\rm h},{\rm p})
there is a sequence of quadratic modules

\textstyle{Q_{+}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{Q\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{I(SQ),}

which is natural in
Q
.

Proof.

The first map is the unique morphism of form parameters
Q_{+}\rightarrow Q
(which is given by
{\rm p}\colon\mathbb{Z}\rightarrow Q_{e}
) and the second one is given by the quotient map
\pi\colon Q_{e}\rightarrow SQ
. For naturality we need to check that a morphism
\alpha\colon Q\rightarrow Q^{\prime}
induces a commutative diagram

\textstyle{Q_{+}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{Q\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\alpha}
\textstyle{I(SQ)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{I(S\alpha)}
\textstyle{Q_{+}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{Q^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{I(SQ^{\prime}).}

The first square commutes by the definition of a morphism of form parameters. The second square commutes because
S\alpha\colon SQ\rightarrow SQ^{\prime}
is induced by
\alpha\colon Q_{e}\rightarrow Q^{\prime}_{e}
.
∎

Proposition 4.11
.

a) For every symmetric form parameter
Q=(Q_{e},{\rm h},{\rm p})
there is a commutative diagram of quadratic modules

\textstyle{Q_{+}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{Q\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{I(SQ)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{Q_{+}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{Q^{+}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{I(\mathbb{Z}_{2}).}

b) Given an abelian group
G
, if we apply the functor
G\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}{-}
to the above diagram, then we get a commutative diagram of abelian groups

\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{S^{2}(G)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{G\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}Q\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{G\otimes SQ\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0}
\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{S^{2}(G)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\Gamma(G)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{G\otimes\mathbb{Z}_{2}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0}

consisting of short exact sequences. In particular the second square in this diagram is a pullback square.

c) Given a free abelian group
G
, if we apply the functor
\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathbb{Z}}(G,{-})
to the diagram in part a), then we get a commutative diagram of abelian groups

\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathbb{Z}}(G,Q_{+})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathbb{Z}}(G,Q)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\textup{Hom}(G,SQ)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0}
\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathbb{Z}}(G,Q_{+})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathbb{Z}}(G,Q^{+})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\textup{Hom}(G,\mathbb{Z}_{2})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0}

consisting of short exact sequences. In particular the second square in this diagram is a pullback square.

Remark 4.12
.

Part b) of Proposition
4.11
was proven by Baues
[
Bau1
, Remark 2.10 (4)]
in the special case
Q=\mathbb{Z}^{P}
(when
G\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\mathbb{Z}^{P}\cong P^{2}(G)
and
G\otimes S\mathbb{Z}^{P}\cong G
).

Proof.

a) We get this by applying Proposition
4.10
to the unique morphism of form parameters
Q\rightarrow Q^{+}
, which is given by
{\rm h}\colon Q_{e}\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}
. Note that
SQ^{+}=\mathbb{Z}_{2}
and the map
I(SQ)\rightarrow I(\mathbb{Z}_{2})
is
I(v_{Q})
, because
v_{Q}
is induced by
{\rm h}
(see also Lemma
2.10
).

b) The exactness of the bottom sequence was proved by Baues. It follows from part a) that the diagram commutes. This also implies that the map
S^{2}(G)\rightarrow G\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}Q
is injective (because its composition with
\textup{Id}_{G}\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}{\rm h}\colon G\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}Q\rightarrow\Gamma(G)
is the injective map
S^{2}(G)\rightarrow\Gamma(G)
).

The map
S^{2}(G)\rightarrow G\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}Q
sends
gh
to
[g,h]\otimes 1
, so
G\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}Q/S^{2}(G)
is the group generated by the symbols
g\otimes q
, linear in both
g\in G
and
q\in Q_{e}
, and subject to the relation
g\otimes{\rm p}(1)=0
, therefore
G\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}Q/S^{2}(G)\cong G\otimes SQ
. Moreover, this isomorphism is induced by the map
G\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}Q\rightarrow G\otimes SQ
, because it sends
[g,h]\otimes 1
to
0
and
g\otimes q
to
g\otimes\pi(q)
. Therefore the top sequence is exact too.

c) First we show that the bottom sequence is exact. An element
(G,\lambda,\mu)
of
\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathbb{Z}}(G,Q^{+})
is determined by the symmetric bilinear form
(G,\lambda)
(because
\mu
satisfies
\mu(x)=\lambda(x,x)
). The image of
(G,\lambda)
is
f\in\textup{Hom}(G,\mathbb{Z}_{2})
given by
f(x)=\lambda(x,x)
mod
2
. For a free
G
the map
\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathbb{Z}}(G,Q^{+})\rightarrow\textup{Hom}(G,\mathbb{Z}_{2})
is surjective, because for a homomorphism
f
and a basis
x_{1},\ldots,x_{k}
of
G
we can define a
\lambda
in the preimage of
f
by
\lambda(x_{i},x_{i})=0
if
f(x_{i})=0
,
\lambda(x_{i},x_{i})=1
if
f(x_{i})=1
and
\lambda(x_{i},x_{j})=0
if
i\neq j
. The kernel of this map consists of even forms on
G
, which are exactly the elements of
\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathbb{Z}}(G,Q_{+})
(because if
(G,\lambda,\mu^{\prime})\in\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathbb{Z}}(G,Q_{+})
, then
2\mu^{\prime}(x)=\lambda(x,x)
). Moreover, the map
\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathbb{Z}}(G,Q_{+})\rightarrow\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathbb{Z}}(G,Q^{+})
sends
(G,\lambda,\mu^{\prime})
to
(G,\lambda,\mu)
(where
2\mu^{\prime}(x)=\lambda(x,x)=\mu(x)
), therefore the bottom sequence is exact.

By Lemma
2.29
the second square is a pullback square (note that
\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathbb{Z}}(G,Q^{+}\oplus SQ)\cong\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathbb{Z}}(G,Q^{+})\oplus\textup{Hom}(G,SQ)
). It follows that the map
\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathbb{Z}}(G,Q)\rightarrow\textup{Hom}(G,SQ)
is surjective too, and its kernel is also
\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathbb{Z}}(G,Q_{+})
, therefore the top sequence is exact too.
∎

Corollary 4.13
.

For every symmetric form parameter
Q
and abelian group
G
there is a short exact sequence

\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{S^{2}(G)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{G\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}Q\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{G\otimes SQ\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0.}

For every symmetric form parameter
Q
and free abelian group
G
there is a short exact sequence

\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathbb{Z}}(G,Q_{+})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathbb{Z}}(G,Q)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\textup{Hom}(G,SQ)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0.}

These sequences are natural in
Q
and
G
.

Proof.

These sequences are obtained from the sequence of Proposition
4.10
by applying
G\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}{-}
and
\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathbb{Z}}(G,{-})
respectively. So they are natural in
Q
by Proposition
4.10
and they are natural in
G
, because
G\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}{-}
and
\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathbb{Z}}(G,{-})
are bifunctors. Exactness was proven in Proposition
4.11
.
∎

Proposition 4.14
.

For every anti-symmetric form parameter
Q=(Q_{e},{\rm h},{\rm p})
there is a sequence of quadratic modules

\textstyle{I(Q_{e})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{Q\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{Q^{-},}

which is natural in
Q
.

Proof.

The first map is given by
\textup{Id}_{Q_{e}}
and the second one is the unique morphism of form parameters
Q\rightarrow Q^{-}
, both of these are natural.
∎

Proposition 4.15
.

For every anti-symmetric form parameter
Q=(Q_{e},{\rm h},{\rm p})
and abelian group
G
there are short exact sequences

\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{G\otimes Q_{e}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{G\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}Q\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\Lambda(G)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0}

and

\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\textup{Hom}(G,Q_{e})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathbb{Z}}(G,Q)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathbb{Z}}(G,Q^{-})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0.}

These sequences are natural in
Q
and
G
.

Proof.

These sequences are obtained from the sequence of Proposition
4.14
by applying
G\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}{-}
and
\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathbb{Z}}(G,{-})
respectively, so naturality follows as in Corollary
4.13
. It remains to prove the exactness of the sequences:

a) The map
G\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}Q\rightarrow\Lambda(G)
sends
g\otimes q
to
0
and
[g,h]\otimes a
to
ag\wedge h
, hence it is surjective. The map
G\otimes Q_{e}\rightarrow G\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}Q
sends
g\otimes q
to
g\otimes q
, so its cokernel is generated by the symbols
[g,h]\otimes a
(linear in all variables) subject to the relation
[g,g]\otimes a=0
, hence it is isomorphic to
\Lambda(G)
. This shows that the sequence is exact at
G\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}Q
. If
G
is written as the direct sum of cyclic groups with generators
g_{1},\ldots,g_{k}
, then we can define a map
G\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}Q\rightarrow G\otimes Q_{e}
by
g\otimes q\mapsto g\otimes q
(for all
g
),
[g_{i},g_{j}]\otimes a\mapsto 0
if
i\neq j
and
[g_{i},g_{i}]\otimes a\mapsto g_{i}\otimes{\rm p}(a)
.
This map is a left inverse of
G\otimes Q_{e}\rightarrow G\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}Q
, so the sequence is also exact at
G\otimes Q_{e}
.

b) The homomorphism
\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathbb{Z}}(G,Q)\rightarrow\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathbb{Z}}(G,Q^{-})
is the forgetful map
(G,\lambda,\mu)\mapsto(G,\lambda)
. It is surjective, because for every bilinear form
\lambda
on
G
with
\lambda(x,x)=0
we can define a refinement
\mu\colon G\rightarrow Q_{e}
. Namely, if
G
is written as the direct sum of cyclic groups with generators
g_{1},\ldots,g_{k}
, then let
\mu(\sum_{i}a_{i}g_{i})={\rm p}(\sum_{i<j}a_{i}a_{j}\lambda(g_{i},g_{j}))
. This is well-defined (because if
g_{i}
has infinite order, then
a_{i}\in\mathbb{Z}
is well-defined, and
\lambda(g_{i},g_{j})=0
otherwise) and we can check that it satisfies
\mu(x+y)=\mu(x)+\mu(y)+{\rm p}(\lambda(x,y))
(using that
{\rm p}\circ\lambda\colon G\times G\rightarrow Q_{e}
is symmetric, because
2{\rm p}=0
).

The kernel of the map
\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathbb{Z}}(G,Q)\rightarrow\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathbb{Z}}(G,Q^{-})
consists of forms
(G,\lambda,\mu)
with
\lambda=0
and
\mu\colon G\rightarrow Q_{e}
a homomorphism. Therefore the sequence is exact.
∎

Corollary 4.16
.

a) For every anti-symmetric form parameter
Q=(Q_{e},{\rm h},{\rm p})
there is a commutative diagram of quadratic modules

\textstyle{I(\mathbb{Z}_{2})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{Q_{-}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{Q^{-}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{I(Q_{e})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{Q\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{Q^{-}}

b) Given an abelian group
G
, if we apply the functor
G\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}{-}
to the above diagram, then we get a commutative diagram of abelian groups

\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{G\otimes\mathbb{Z}_{2}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\Lambda_{1}(G)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\Lambda(G)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0}
\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{G\otimes Q_{e}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{G\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}Q\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\Lambda(G)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0}

consisting of short exact sequences. In particular the first square in this diagram is a pushout square.

c) Given an abelian group
G
, if we apply the functor
\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathbb{Z}}(G,{-})
to the diagram in part a), then we get a commutative diagram of abelian groups

\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\textup{Hom}(G,\mathbb{Z}_{2})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathbb{Z}}(G,Q_{-})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathbb{Z}}(G,Q^{-})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0}
\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\textup{Hom}(G,Q_{e})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathbb{Z}}(G,Q)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathbb{Z}}(G,Q^{-})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0}

consisting of short exact sequences. In particular the first square in this diagram is a pushout square.

Proof.

This follows from Propositions
4.14
and
4.15
, applied to the unique morphism of form parameters
Q_{-}\rightarrow Q
given by
v^{\prime}_{Q}\colon\mathbb{Z}_{2}\rightarrow Q_{e}
.
∎

4.2.3.
An aside on the quadratic tensor product and
\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathbb{Z}}

The quadratic tensor product and
\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathbb{Z}}
can be described in terms of a tensor product operation and morphisms of quadratic modules, respectively. These notions, in turn, are special cases of tensor products and morphisms of functors.

We start by observing that for an abelian group
A
, the group
P^{2}(A)=A\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\mathbb{Z}^{P}
is generated by symbols
\overline{[a,b]}
(bilinear in
a,b\in A
) and
\overline{a}
, satisfying
\overline{a+b}=\overline{a}+\overline{b}+\overline{[a,b]}
. Define the quadratic module

J(A)=(P^{2}(A)\xrightarrow{\overline{\textup{h}}}A\otimes A\xrightarrow{\overline{\textup{p}}}P^{2}(A))

where
\overline{\textup{h}}(\overline{[a,b]})=a\otimes b+b\otimes a
,
\overline{\textup{h}}(\overline{a})=a\otimes a
and
\overline{\textup{p}}(a\otimes b)=\overline{[a,b]}
. Then we have

\textup{Hom}(J(A),Q)\cong\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathbb{Z}}(A,Q)

where a morphism
(F\colon A\otimes A\to Q_{ee},G\colon P^{2}(A)\to Q_{e})
corresponds to the form
(A,\lambda,\mu)
with
\lambda(a,b)=F(a\otimes b)
and
\mu(a)=G(\overline{a})
.

To get a similar isomorphism involving the quadratic tensor product, we define the tensor product
Q\otimes Q^{\prime}
of quadratic modules
Q=(Q_{ee},Q_{e},\textup{h},\textup{p})
and
Q^{\prime}=(Q^{\prime}_{ee},Q^{\prime}_{e},\textup{h}^{\prime},\textup{p}^{\prime})
as follows. It is the abelian group generated by the (bilinear) symbols
q_{1}\otimes q^{\prime}_{1}
and
q_{2}\otimes q^{\prime}_{2}
(where
q_{1}\in Q_{e}
,
q_{2}\in Q_{ee}
,
q^{\prime}_{1}\in Q^{\prime}_{e}
,
q^{\prime}_{2}\in Q^{\prime}_{ee}
), subject to the relations
\textup{h}(q_{1})\otimes q^{\prime}_{2}=q_{1}\otimes\textup{p}^{\prime}(q^{\prime}_{2})
and
\textup{p}(q_{2})\otimes q^{\prime}_{1}=q_{2}\otimes\textup{h}^{\prime}(q^{\prime}_{1})
. Then

J(A)\otimes Q\cong A\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}Q

with
\overline{a}\otimes q_{1}
,
\overline{[a,b]}\otimes q_{1}
and
(a\otimes b)\otimes q_{2}
corresponding to
a\otimes q_{1}
,
[a,b]\otimes\textup{h}(q_{1})
and
[a,b]\otimes q_{2}
, respectively.

The notions of a tensor product and a morphism of quadratic modules are themselves special cases of corresponding notions for functors. To see this, we let
\mathcal{C}
be the category on two objects,
X
and
Y
, generated by two morphisms,
\alpha:X\to Y
and
\beta:Y\to X
. Then a quadratic module
Q
determines a functor
F_{Q}:\mathcal{C}\to\mathbf{Ab}
,
F_{Q}(X)=Q_{ee}
,
F_{Q}(Y)=Q_{e}
,
F_{Q}(\alpha)=\textup{p}
,
F_{Q}(\beta)=\textup{h}
. At the same time, any quadratic module
Q^{\prime}
determines a functor
G_{Q^{\prime}}:\mathcal{C}^{\text{op}}\to\mathbf{Ab}
,
G_{Q^{\prime}}(X)=Q^{\prime}_{ee}
,
G_{Q^{\prime}}(Y)=Q^{\prime}_{e}
,
G_{Q^{\prime}}(\alpha)=\textup{h}^{\prime}
,
G_{Q^{\prime}}(\beta)=\textup{p}^{\prime}
. The tensor product
Q\otimes Q^{\prime}
defined above coincides with the tensor product of the functors
F_{Q}
and
G_{Q^{\prime}}
. Of course, a morphism
Q\to Q^{\prime}
between quadratic modules is just a morphism
F_{Q}\to F_{Q^{\prime}}
between functors (i.e. a natural transformation).

4.3.
Reduced Witt groups and the split norm

Now we consider a split form parameter
Q\oplus G
, where
Q
is a form parameter and
G
is an abelian group. This subsection is about the problem of computing
W_{0}(Q\oplus G)
, assuming that
W_{0}(Q)
is known.
We have seen that taking Witt groups defines a functor
W_{0}\colon\mathbf{FP}\to\mathbf{Ab}
.
Now there are obvious morphisms
i\colon Q\to Q\oplus G
and

r\colon Q\oplus G\to Q
with
ri={\rm Id}_{Q}
,
and so
W_{0}(Q)
is naturally identified with
a summand of
W_{0}(Q\oplus G)
.

Definition 4.17
.

We define the
reduced Witt group
of
Q\oplus G
by

W_{0}^{Q}(G):={\rm Ker}(W_{0}(r))\leq W_{0}(Q\oplus G).

Evidently
W_{0}(Q\oplus G)\cong W_{0}(Q)\oplus W_{0}^{Q}(G)
and our problem reduces to computing
W_{0}^{Q}(G)
.

A split morphism
\alpha=\alpha_{Q}\oplus\alpha_{G}\colon Q_{1}\oplus G_{1}\to Q_{2}\oplus G_{2}
induces a commutative diagram

\textstyle{Q_{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\alpha_{Q}}
\scriptstyle{i_{1}}
\textstyle{Q_{1}\oplus G_{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\alpha}
\scriptstyle{r_{1}}
\textstyle{Q_{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\alpha_{Q}}
\textstyle{Q_{2}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{i_{2}}
\textstyle{Q_{2}\oplus G_{2}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{r_{2}}
\textstyle{Q_{2}}

of form parameters. If we apply the functor
W_{0}
to this, we get an analogous commutative diagram between the Witt groups. Therefore the map
W_{0}(\alpha)
is of the form
W_{0}(\alpha_{Q})\oplus f\colon W_{0}(Q_{1})\oplus W_{0}^{Q_{1}}(G_{1})\rightarrow W_{0}(Q_{2})\oplus W_{0}^{Q_{2}}(G_{2})
for some homomorphism
f\colon W_{0}^{Q_{1}}(G_{1})\rightarrow W_{0}^{Q_{2}}(G_{2})
, showing that
W_{0}^{Q}(G)
is a functor in both
Q
and
G
.

To study reduced Witt groups, we introduce the
split norm
of a nonsingular
(Q\oplus G)
-form, for any form parameter
Q
and a finitely generated abelian group
G
, which is an element of
G\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}Q
.
The split norm is invariant under isomorphism of
(Q\oplus G)
-forms (and even Witt equivalence, see Proposition
4.23
), and hence induces a function

F\colon\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathbb{Z}}^{\rm ns}(Q\oplus G)\rightarrow G\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}Q.

We will define this invariant in two different ways, and then show that the two definitions are equivalent. Then, in Theorem
4.28
, we will use
F
to identify
W_{0}^{Q}(G)
with
G\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}Q
.

Definition 4.18

(Split norm, first definition)
.

Suppose that
(X,\lambda,(\mu_{Q},\mu_{G}))
is a nonsingular
(Q\oplus G)
-form. Choose any basis
x_{1},\ldots,x_{k}
of
X
and let
y_{1},\ldots,y_{k}
be the basis of
X
which is dual to
x_{1},\ldots,x_{k}
with respect to
\lambda
(i.e.
\lambda(x_{i},y_{i})=1
and
\lambda(x_{i},y_{j})=0
if
i\neq j
). We set

F(X,\lambda,(\mu_{Q},\mu_{G})):=\sum_{i<j}\left[\mu_{G}(x_{i}),\mu_{G}(x_{j})\right]\otimes\lambda(y_{i},y_{j})+\sum_{i}\mu_{G}(x_{i})\otimes\mu_{Q}(y_{i})\in G\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}Q.

Proposition 4.19
.

F(X,\lambda,(\mu_{Q},\mu_{G}))
is well-defined; i.e. it does not depend on the choice of the basis.
Moreover, if
(X,\lambda,(\mu_{Q},\mu_{G}))\cong(X^{\prime},\lambda^{\prime},(\mu^{\prime}_{Q},\mu^{\prime}_{G}))
, then
F(X,\lambda,(\mu_{Q},\mu_{G}))=F(X^{\prime},\lambda^{\prime},(\mu^{\prime}_{Q},\mu^{\prime}_{G}))
, so that
F
induces a well-defined function
F\colon\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathbb{Z}}^{\rm ns}(Q\oplus G)\rightarrow G\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}Q
on the set of isomorphism classes.

Proof.

If suffices to check that the value of
F
does not change when we apply elementary transformations to the basis
x_{1},\ldots,x_{k}
.

If we swap
x_{1}
and
x_{2}
, then
y_{1}
and
y_{2}
also get swapped. In
F
the only change is that
[\mu_{G}(x_{1}),\mu_{G}(x_{2})]\otimes\lambda(y_{1},y_{2})
is replaced with
[\mu_{G}(x_{2}),\mu_{G}(x_{1})]\otimes\lambda(y_{2},y_{1})
, and these are equal, because
[\cdot,\cdot]
and
\lambda
are both
\epsilon_{Q}
-symmetric.

If we replace
x_{1}
with
-x_{1}
, then
y_{1}
is also replaced with
-y_{1}
. In the first part of
F
the terms
[\mu_{G}(x_{1}),\mu_{G}(x_{j})]\otimes\lambda(y_{1},y_{j})
for
j\geq 2
are replaced with

[\mu_{G}(-x_{1}),\mu_{G}(x_{j})]\otimes\lambda(-y_{1},y_{j})=[\mu_{G}(x_{1}),\mu_{G}(x_{j})]\otimes\lambda(y_{1},y_{j}).

In the second part
\mu_{G}(x_{1})\otimes\mu_{Q}(y_{1})
is replaced with
\mu_{G}(-x_{1})\otimes\mu_{Q}(-y_{1})
, we will show that these are equal. We have
0=\mu_{Q}(y_{1}-y_{1})=\mu_{Q}(y_{1})+\mu_{Q}(-y_{1})+{\rm p}(\lambda(y_{1},-y_{1}))
and also
\lambda(y_{1},-y_{1})=-\lambda(-y_{1},-y_{1})=-{\rm h}(\mu_{Q}(-y_{1}))
.
Hence

0=\mu_{G}(x_{1})\otimes\mu_{Q}(y_{1}-y_{1})=\mu_{G}(x_{1})\otimes\mu_{Q}(y_{1})+\mu_{G}(x_{1})\otimes\mu_{Q}(-y_{1})-\mu_{G}(x_{1})\otimes{\rm p}({\rm h}(\mu_{Q}(-y_{1}))).

We also have
[\mu_{G}(x_{1}),\mu_{G}(-x_{1})]\otimes{\rm h}(\mu_{Q}(-y_{1}))=-[\mu_{G}(x_{1}),\mu_{G}(x_{1})]\otimes{\rm h}(\mu_{Q}(-y_{1}))=-\mu_{G}(x_{1})\otimes{\rm p}({\rm h}(\mu_{Q}(-y_{1})))
, hence

0=(\mu_{G}(x_{1})+\mu_{G}(-x_{1}))\otimes\mu_{Q}(-y_{1})=\mu_{G}(x_{1})\otimes\mu_{Q}(-y_{1})+\mu_{G}(-x_{1})\otimes\mu_{Q}(-y_{1})-\mu_{G}(x_{1})\otimes{\rm p}({\rm h}(\mu_{Q}(-y_{1}))).

By comparing these two equalities we see that
\mu_{G}(x_{1})\otimes\mu_{Q}(y_{1})=\mu_{G}(-x_{1})\otimes\mu_{Q}(-y_{1})
.

Finally if we replace
x_{1}
with
x_{1}+x_{2}
, then
y_{2}
is replaced with
y_{2}-y_{1}
. In
F
the terms

[\mu_{G}(x_{1}),\mu_{G}(x_{j})]\otimes\lambda(y_{1},y_{j})+[\mu_{G}(x_{2}),\mu_{G}(x_{j})]\otimes\lambda(y_{2},y_{j})

are replaced by

[\mu_{G}(x_{1}+x_{2}),\mu_{G}(x_{j})]\otimes\lambda(y_{1},y_{j})+[\mu_{G}(x_{2}),\mu_{G}(x_{j})]\otimes\lambda(y_{2}-y_{1},y_{j})=\\
[\mu_{G}(x_{1}),\mu_{G}(x_{j})]\otimes\lambda(y_{1},y_{j})+[\mu_{G}(x_{2}),\mu_{G}(x_{j})]\otimes\lambda(y_{2},y_{j})

for every
j\geq 3
. Further,
[\mu_{G}(x_{1}),\mu_{G}(x_{2})]\otimes\lambda(y_{1},y_{2})+\mu_{G}(x_{1})\otimes\mu_{Q}(y_{1})+\mu_{G}(x_{2})\otimes\mu_{Q}(y_{2})
is replaced by
[\mu_{G}(x_{1}+x_{2}),\mu_{G}(x_{2})]\otimes\lambda(y_{1},y_{2}-y_{1})+\mu_{G}(x_{1}+x_{2})\otimes\mu_{Q}(y_{1})+\mu_{G}(x_{2})\otimes\mu_{Q}(y_{2}-y_{1})
. The difference is

[\mu_{G}(x_{2}),\mu_{G}(x_{2})]\otimes\lambda(y_{1},y_{2})+[\mu_{G}(x_{1}),\mu_{G}(x_{2})]\otimes\lambda(y_{1},-y_{1})+[\mu_{G}(x_{2}),\mu_{G}(x_{2})]\otimes\lambda(y_{1},-y_{1})+\\
+\mu_{G}(x_{2})\otimes\mu_{Q}(y_{1})+[\mu_{G}(x_{1}),\mu_{G}(x_{2})]\otimes{\rm h}(\mu_{Q}(y_{1}))+\mu_{G}(x_{2})\otimes\mu_{Q}(-y_{1})+\mu_{G}(x_{2})\otimes{\rm p}(\lambda(y_{2},-y_{1}))=\\
=[\mu_{G}(x_{2}),\mu_{G}(x_{2})]\otimes\lambda(y_{1},-y_{1})+\mu_{G}(x_{2})\otimes\mu_{Q}(y_{1})+\mu_{G}(x_{2})\otimes\mu_{Q}(-y_{1})=\\
=\mu_{G}(x_{2})\otimes{\rm p}(\lambda(y_{1},-y_{1}))+\mu_{G}(x_{2})\otimes\mu_{Q}(y_{1})+\mu_{G}(x_{2})\otimes\mu_{Q}(-y_{1})=\mu_{G}(x_{2})\otimes\mu_{Q}(y_{1}-y_{1})=0,

where we used that
{\rm h}(\mu_{Q}(y_{1}))=-\lambda(y_{1},-y_{1})
and
{\rm p}(\lambda(y_{2},-y_{1}))=-{\rm p}(\lambda(y_{1},y_{2}))
.

Hence the value of
F
does not change when we change the basis.

If
(X,\lambda,(\mu_{Q},\mu_{G}))\cong(X^{\prime},\lambda^{\prime},(\mu^{\prime}_{Q},\mu^{\prime}_{G}))
, then we can see that
F
takes the same value on both forms by choosing compatible bases in
X
and
X^{\prime}
. Therefore the split norm is an isomorphism invariant.
∎

We present a second definition of the split norm, which does not rely on a choice of basis.
We do this for completeness, added understanding of the split norm and possible future applications.
In order to present the second definition of the split norm, we need the following result of Baues

[
Bau2
, Lemma (8.3)]
(see also
[
Bau1
, Lemma 5.4]
): If
X
is torsion free, then there is a natural isomorphism

\chi\colon X\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}Q\to\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathbb{Z}}(X^{*},Q),

which is defined as follows.
For
x,y\in X
,
q\in Q_{e}
and
n\in\mathbb{Z}

\chi(x\otimes q)=(X^{*},\lambda_{x\otimes q},\mu_{x\otimes q})\qquad\text{and}\qquad\chi([x,y]\otimes n)=(X^{*},\lambda_{[x,y]\otimes n},\mu_{[x,y]\otimes n}),

where for
a,b\in X^{*}
,
\lambda_{x\otimes q}(a,b)=a(x)\textup{h}(q)b(x)
and
\mu_{x\otimes q}(a)=a(x)\cdot q
using the
\mathbb{Z}
-action given in (
6
), and
\lambda_{[x,y]\otimes n}(a,b)=a(x)nb(y)+\epsilon_{Q}a(y)nb(x)
and
\mu_{[x,y]\otimes n}(a)=\textup{p}(a(x)na(y))
.

Definition 4.20

(Split norm, second definition)
.

Let
\underline{\mu}=(X,\lambda,(\mu_{Q},\mu_{G}))
be a nonsingular
(Q\oplus G)
-form.
We set

F\bigl(\underline{\mu}\bigr):=(\mu_{G})_{*}(\chi^{-1}(\underline{\mu}_{Q}^{-1}))\in G\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}Q,

where

\bullet

\underline{\mu}_{Q}:=r_{*}(\underline{\mu})=(X,\lambda,\mu_{Q})\in\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathbb{Z}}^{\rm ns}(X,Q)
;

\bullet

\lambda^{-1}\colon X^{*}\times X^{*}\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}
denotes the adjoint of
\hat{\lambda}^{-1}\colon X^{*}\rightarrow X=(X^{*})^{*}
, where
\hat{\lambda}\colon X\to X^{*}
is the adjoint of
\lambda
;

\bullet

\underline{\mu}_{Q}^{-1}:=(\hat{\lambda}^{-1})^{*}(\underline{\mu}_{Q})=(X^{*},\lambda^{-1},\mu_{Q}\circ\hat{\lambda}^{-1})\in\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathbb{Z}}^{\rm ns}(X^{*},Q)
;

\bullet

(\mu_{G})_{*}\colon X\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}Q\rightarrow G\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}Q
is the map induced by
\mu_{G}
.

That is,
F(\underline{\mu})=C_{\underline{\mu}}(\underline{\mu})
, where
C_{\underline{\mu}}
is the composition

C_{\underline{\mu}}\colon\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathbb{Z}}^{\rm ns}(X,Q\oplus G)\xrightarrow{r_{*}}\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathbb{Z}}^{\rm ns}(X,Q)\xrightarrow{(\hat{\lambda}^{-1})^{*}}\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathbb{Z}}^{\rm ns}(X^{*},Q)\xrightarrow{\chi^{-1}}X\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}Q\xrightarrow{(\mu_{G})_{*}}G\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}Q.

Lemma 4.21
.

The two definitions of the split norm
F
coincide.

Proof.

Suppose that
\underline{\mu}=(X,\lambda,(\mu_{Q},\mu_{G}))
is a nonsingular
(Q\oplus G)
-form. Let
\underline{\mu}_{X}=(X,\lambda,(\mu_{Q},\textup{Id}_{X}))\in\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathbb{Z}}^{\rm ns}(X,Q\oplus X)
, so that
\underline{\mu}=(\textup{Id}_{Q}\oplus\mu_{G})_{*}(\underline{\mu}_{X})
. Then it is enough to show that the two definitions of
F(\underline{\mu}_{X})\in X\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}Q
coincide, because both versions of
F(\underline{\mu})
can be obtained from that by applying
(\mu_{G})_{*}
.

Let
x_{1},\ldots,x_{k}
be a basis of
X
, let
y_{1},\ldots,y_{k}
be the dual basis of
X
with respect to
\lambda
, and let
x_{1}^{*},\ldots,x_{k}^{*}
be the dual basis of
X^{*}
. We need to show that

\chi\left(\sum_{i<j}\left[x_{i},x_{j}\right]\otimes\lambda(y_{i},y_{j})+\sum_{i}x_{i}\otimes\mu_{Q}(y_{i})\right)=(X^{*},\lambda^{-1},\mu_{Q}\circ\hat{\lambda}^{-1}).

For brevity, we will denote the left hand side by
(X^{*},\overline{\lambda},\overline{\mu})
.
By the definition of
\chi
above we have

\displaystyle\overline{\lambda}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i<j}\lambda_{[x_{i},x_{j}]\otimes\lambda(y_{i},y_{j})}+\sum_{i}\lambda_{x_{i}\otimes\mu_{Q}(y_{i})}

\displaystyle\overline{\mu}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i<j}\mu_{[x_{i},x_{j}]\otimes\lambda(y_{i},y_{j})}+\sum_{i}\mu_{x_{i}\otimes\mu_{Q}(y_{i})}

Therefore
\overline{\lambda}(x_{i}^{*},x_{j}^{*})
is
\lambda(y_{i},y_{j})
if
i<j
,
\textup{h}(\mu_{Q}(y_{i}))=\lambda(y_{i},y_{i})
if
i=j
and
\epsilon_{Q}\lambda(y_{j},y_{i})=\lambda(y_{i},y_{j})
if
i>j
. That is,
\overline{\lambda}(x_{i}^{*},x_{j}^{*})=\lambda(y_{i},y_{j})
in all cases. We also have
\overline{\mu}(x_{i}^{*})=1\cdot\mu_{Q}(y_{i})=\mu_{Q}(y_{i})
for all
i
.

On the right hand side, since
\hat{\lambda}(y_{i})=x_{i}^{*}
, we have
\lambda^{-1}(x_{i}^{*},x_{j}^{*})=\lambda(y_{i},y_{j})
for all
i,j
, therefore
\lambda^{-1}=\overline{\lambda}
. Also
\mu_{Q}\circ\hat{\lambda}^{-1}(x_{i}^{*})=\mu_{Q}(y_{i})=\overline{\mu}(x_{i}^{*})
for all
i
, and since the quadratic refinement
\overline{\mu}
is uniquely determined by its values on a basis, this means that
\mu_{Q}\circ\hat{\lambda}^{-1}=\overline{\mu}
, as required.
∎

Since the second definition does not depend on the choice of a basis, and in the proof of Lemma
4.21
the basis
x_{1},\ldots,x_{k}
can be chosen arbitrarily, this argument also serves as an alternative proof for Proposition
4.19
. From now on we will use the first definition.

Proposition 4.22
.

F
is a monoid homomorphism.

Proof.

Given nonsingular
(Q\oplus G)
-forms
(X,\lambda,(\mu_{Q},\mu_{G}))
and
(X^{\prime},\lambda^{\prime},(\mu^{\prime}_{Q},\mu^{\prime}_{G}))
,
let
x_{1},\ldots,x_{k}
be a basis of
X
and
x^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,x^{\prime}_{l}
be a basis of
X^{\prime}
(with dual bases
y_{1},\ldots,y_{k}
and
y^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,y^{\prime}_{l}
, respectively). Then

F((X,\lambda,(\mu_{Q},\mu_{G}))\oplus(X^{\prime},\lambda^{\prime},(\mu^{\prime}_{Q},\mu^{\prime}_{G})))=\\
F(X,\lambda,(\mu_{Q},\mu_{G}))+F(X^{\prime},\lambda^{\prime},(\mu^{\prime}_{Q},\mu^{\prime}_{G}))+\sum_{i\leq k,j\leq l}[\mu_{G}(x_{i}),\mu^{\prime}_{G}(x^{\prime}_{j})]\otimes(\lambda\oplus\lambda^{\prime})(y_{i},y^{\prime}_{j})=\\
F(X,\lambda,(\mu_{Q},\mu_{G}))+F(X^{\prime},\lambda^{\prime},(\mu^{\prime}_{Q},\mu^{\prime}_{G})),

because
(\lambda\oplus\lambda^{\prime})(y,y^{\prime})=\lambda(y,0)+\lambda^{\prime}(0,y^{\prime})=0
for every
y\in X
and
y^{\prime}\in X^{\prime}
.
∎

Proposition 4.23
.

The map
F
induces a homomorphism
\tilde{F}\colon W_{0}(Q\oplus G)\rightarrow G\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}Q
.

Proof.

It is enough to prove that
F
vanishes on metabolic forms. So suppose that
(X,\lambda,(\mu_{Q},\mu_{G}))
is a metabolic form over
Q\oplus G
, let
L<X
be a lagrangian and fix an identification
X\cong L\oplus L^{*}
.
Let
x_{1},\ldots,x_{k}
be a basis of
L
, and let
\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{k}
be the dual basis of
L^{*}
.
Then
x_{1},\ldots,x_{k},\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{k}
is a basis of
X
, and the dual basis of
X
with respect to
\lambda
is
y_{1},\ldots,y_{k},\beta_{1},\ldots,\beta_{k}
, where
y_{i}=\alpha_{i}+z_{i}
for some
z_{i}\in L
and
\beta_{i}=\epsilon_{Q}x_{i}
. Since
L
is a lagrangian,
\mu_{G}(x_{i})=0
,
\lambda(\beta_{i},\beta_{j})=0
and
\mu_{Q}(\beta_{i})=0
for every
i,j
, therefore in
F(X,\lambda,(\mu_{Q},\mu_{G}))
every term vanishes.
∎

Definition 4.24
.

We define a homomorphism
\gamma\colon G\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}Q\rightarrow W_{0}(Q\oplus G)
by

\gamma([g_{1},g_{2}]\otimes 1):=\left(\mathbb{Z}^{2},\left[\begin{array}[]{cc}0&1\\
\epsilon_{Q}&0\end{array}\right],\left(\begin{array}[]{c}g_{1}\\
g_{2}\end{array}\right)\right)\quad\text{and}\quad\gamma(g\otimes q):=\left(\mathbb{Z}^{2},\left[\begin{array}[]{cc}0&1\\
\epsilon_{Q}&-{\rm h}(q)\end{array}\right],\left(\begin{array}[]{c}g\\
R(q)\end{array}\right)\right),

where
R=\textup{Id}-{\rm p}{\rm h}\colon Q_{e}\rightarrow Q_{e}
.

Proposition 4.25
.

The map
\gamma
is a well-defined homomorphism.

Proof.

These triples denote
(Q\oplus G)
-forms, because
{\rm h}(g_{1})={\rm h}(g_{2})={\rm h}(g)=0
and
{\rm h}(R(q))=-{\rm h}(q)
;
see Remark
2.20
.
The matrices are nonsingular, so these
(Q\oplus G)
-forms represent elements of
W_{0}(Q\oplus G)
. It remains to check that the values of
\gamma
satisfy the relations coming from the relations in
G\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}Q
.

The first relation we consider is
[g_{1},g_{2}]\otimes 1+[g_{1}^{\prime},g_{2}]\otimes 1=[g_{1}+g_{1}^{\prime},g_{2}]\otimes 1
. Since

\left(\mathbb{Z}^{2}\left<e_{1},f_{1}\right>,\left[\begin{array}[]{cc}0&1\\
\epsilon_{Q}&0\end{array}\right],\left(\begin{array}[]{c}g_{1}\\
g_{2}\end{array}\right)\right)\oplus\left(\mathbb{Z}^{2}\left<e_{2},f_{2}\right>,\left[\begin{array}[]{cc}0&1\\
\epsilon_{Q}&0\end{array}\right],\left(\begin{array}[]{c}g_{1}^{\prime}\\
g_{2}\end{array}\right)\right)=\\
\left(\mathbb{Z}^{2}\left<e_{1}+e_{2},f_{1}\right>,\left[\begin{array}[]{cc}0&1\\
\epsilon_{Q}&0\end{array}\right],\left(\begin{array}[]{c}g_{1}+g_{1}^{\prime}\\
g_{2}\end{array}\right)\right)\oplus\left(\mathbb{Z}^{2}\left<e_{2},f_{2}-f_{1}\right>,\left[\begin{array}[]{cc}0&1\\
\epsilon_{Q}&0\end{array}\right],\left(\begin{array}[]{c}g_{1}^{\prime}\\
0\end{array}\right)\right)\sim\\
\left(\mathbb{Z}^{2}\left<e_{1}+e_{2},f_{1}\right>,\left[\begin{array}[]{cc}0&1\\
\epsilon_{Q}&0\end{array}\right],\left(\begin{array}[]{c}g_{1}+g_{1}^{\prime}\\
g_{2}\end{array}\right)\right)

(where
\mathbb{Z}^{2}\left<e_{1},f_{1}\right>
denotes a copy of the group
\mathbb{Z}^{2}
with standard basis
e_{1},f_{1}
), we get that

\gamma([g_{1},g_{2}]\otimes 1)+\gamma([g_{1}^{\prime},g_{2}]\otimes 1)=\gamma([g_{1}+g_{1}^{\prime},g_{2}]\otimes 1),

i.e. the corresponding relation is satisfied by the values of
\gamma
.
Similarly, we get that

\gamma([g_{1},g_{2}]\otimes 1)+\gamma([g_{1},g_{2}^{\prime}]\otimes 1)=\gamma([g_{1},g_{2}+g_{2}^{\prime}]\otimes 1).

The above calculation also shows that for any
n\in\mathbb{Z}
we have

\gamma([g_{1},g_{2}]\otimes n)=\left(\mathbb{Z}^{2},\left[\begin{array}[]{cc}0&1\\
\epsilon_{Q}&0\end{array}\right],\left(\begin{array}[]{c}ng_{1}\\
g_{2}\end{array}\right)\right).

We also have
\gamma(g\otimes q)+\gamma(g\otimes q^{\prime})=\gamma(g\otimes(q+q^{\prime}))
, because

\left(\mathbb{Z}^{2}\left<e_{1},f_{1}\right>,\left[\begin{array}[]{cc}0&1\\
\epsilon_{Q}&-{\rm h}(q)\end{array}\right],\left(\begin{array}[]{c}g\\
R(q)\end{array}\right)\right)\oplus\left(\mathbb{Z}^{2}\left<e_{2},f_{2}\right>,\left[\begin{array}[]{cc}0&1\\
\epsilon_{Q}&-{\rm h}(q^{\prime})\end{array}\right],\left(\begin{array}[]{c}g\\
R(q^{\prime})\end{array}\right)\right)=\\
\left(\mathbb{Z}^{2}\left<e_{1},f_{1}+f_{2}\right>,\left[\begin{array}[]{cc}0&1\\
\epsilon_{Q}&-{\rm h}(q+q^{\prime})\end{array}\right],\left(\begin{array}[]{c}g\\
R(q+q^{\prime})\end{array}\right)\right)\oplus\\
\left(\mathbb{Z}^{2}\left<e_{2}-e_{1},f_{2}+{\rm h}(q^{\prime})e_{2}\right>,\left[\begin{array}[]{cc}0&1\\
\epsilon_{Q}&\epsilon_{Q}{\rm h}(q^{\prime})\end{array}\right],\left(\begin{array}[]{c}0\\
q^{\prime}+{\rm h}(q^{\prime})g\end{array}\right)\right)\sim\\
\left(\mathbb{Z}^{2}\left<e_{1},f_{1}+f_{2}\right>,\left[\begin{array}[]{cc}0&1\\
\epsilon_{Q}&-{\rm h}(q+q^{\prime})\end{array}\right],\left(\begin{array}[]{c}g\\
R(q+q^{\prime})\end{array}\right)\right).

Next, we have
\gamma([g,g]\otimes 1)=\gamma(g\otimes{\rm p}(1))
, because

\left(\mathbb{Z}^{2}\left<e,f\right>,\left[\begin{array}[]{cc}0&1\\
\epsilon_{Q}&0\end{array}\right],\left(\begin{array}[]{c}g\\
g\end{array}\right)\right)=\left(\mathbb{Z}^{2}\left<e,f-e\right>,\left[\begin{array}[]{cc}0&1\\
\epsilon_{Q}&-(1+\epsilon_{Q})\end{array}\right],\left(\begin{array}[]{c}g\\
-{\rm p}(1)\end{array}\right)\right)

and
-(1+\epsilon_{Q})=-{\rm h}{\rm p}(1)
and
-{\rm p}(1)=R({\rm p}(1))
.

Finally
\gamma(g\otimes q)+\gamma(g^{\prime}\otimes q)+\gamma([g,g^{\prime}]\otimes{\rm h}(q))=\gamma((g+g^{\prime})\otimes q)
, because

\left(\mathbb{Z}^{2}\left<e_{1},f_{1}\right>,\left[\begin{array}[]{cc}0&1\\
\epsilon_{Q}&-{\rm h}(q)\end{array}\right],\left(\begin{array}[]{c}g\\
R(q)\end{array}\right)\right)\oplus\left(\mathbb{Z}^{2}\left<e_{2},f_{2}\right>,\left[\begin{array}[]{cc}0&1\\
\epsilon_{Q}&-{\rm h}(q)\end{array}\right],\left(\begin{array}[]{c}g^{\prime}\\
R(q)\end{array}\right)\right)\oplus\\
\left(\mathbb{Z}^{2}\left<e_{3},f_{3}\right>,\left[\begin{array}[]{cc}0&1\\
\epsilon_{Q}&0\end{array}\right],\left(\begin{array}[]{c}{\rm h}(q)g\\
g^{\prime}\end{array}\right)\right)=\left(\mathbb{Z}^{2}\left<e_{1}+e_{2},f_{1}\right>,\left[\begin{array}[]{cc}0&1\\
\epsilon_{Q}&-{\rm h}(q)\end{array}\right],\left(\begin{array}[]{c}g+g^{\prime}\\
R(q)\end{array}\right)\right)\oplus\\
\left(\mathbb{Z}^{4}\left<e_{2},f_{2}-f_{1}-{\rm h}(q)e_{1},e_{3},f_{3}\right>,\left[\begin{array}[]{cccc}0&1&0&0\\
\epsilon_{Q}&0&0&0\\
0&0&0&1\\
0&0&\epsilon_{Q}&0\end{array}\right],\left(\begin{array}[]{c}g^{\prime}\\
-{\rm h}(q)g\\
{\rm h}(q)g\\
g^{\prime}\end{array}\right)\right)\sim\\
\left(\mathbb{Z}^{2}\left<e_{1}+e_{2},f_{1}\right>,\left[\begin{array}[]{cc}0&1\\
\epsilon_{Q}&-{\rm h}(q)\end{array}\right],\left(\begin{array}[]{c}g+g^{\prime}\\
R(q)\end{array}\right)\right),

where we used that
(\epsilon_{Q}-1){\rm h}(q)=0
(because
{\rm h}=0
if
\epsilon_{Q}=-1
) and that
\left<e_{2}-f_{3},f_{2}-f_{1}-{\rm h}(q)e_{1}+e_{3}\right>
is a lagrangian in
\mathbb{Z}^{4}\left<e_{2},f_{2}-f_{1}-{\rm h}(q)e_{1},e_{3},f_{3}\right>
.

So the values of
\gamma
satisfy the appropriate relations and therefore we have a well-defined homomorphism
G\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}Q\rightarrow W_{0}(Q\oplus G)
.
∎

Having shown that
\gamma
is a homomorphism, we next determine its image.

Proposition 4.26
.

The homomorphism
\gamma\colon G\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}Q\rightarrow W_{0}(Q\oplus G)
has image
\mathrm{Im}(\gamma)=W_{0}^{Q}(G)
.

Proof.

The natural map
\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathbb{Z}}^{\rm ns}(Q\oplus G)\rightarrow\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathbb{Z}}^{\rm ns}(Q)
sends both
\gamma([g_{1},g_{2}]\otimes 1)
and
\gamma(g\otimes q)
to metabolic forms, so the composition of
\gamma
with the natural map
W_{0}(Q\oplus G)\rightarrow W_{0}(Q)
is trivial, therefore
\mathrm{Im}(\gamma)\subseteq W_{0}^{Q}(G)
.

For the other direction recall that every element of
W_{0}^{Q}(G)
has a representative
(X,\lambda,(\mu_{Q},\mu_{G}))
such that
(X,\lambda,\mu_{Q})
is metabolic and that every metabolic
Q
-form can be written as a sum of metabolic rank-
2

Q
-forms, so
(X,\lambda,(\mu_{Q},\mu_{G}))
is the sum of
(Q\oplus G)
-forms of the form

\left(\mathbb{Z}^{2},\left[\begin{array}[]{cc}0&1\\
\epsilon_{Q}&{\rm h}(q)\end{array}\right],\left(\begin{array}[]{c}g_{1}\\
q+g_{2}\end{array}\right)\right).

These elements are contained in
\mathrm{Im}(\gamma)
, because

\gamma([g_{1},g_{2}]\otimes 1)+\gamma(g_{1}\otimes R(q))=\\
\left(\mathbb{Z}^{2}\left<e_{1},f_{1}\right>,\left[\begin{array}[]{cc}0&1\\
\epsilon_{Q}&0\end{array}\right],\left(\begin{array}[]{c}g_{1}\\
g_{2}\end{array}\right)\right)\oplus\left(\mathbb{Z}^{2}\left<e_{2},f_{2}\right>,\left[\begin{array}[]{cc}0&1\\
\epsilon_{Q}&{\rm h}(q)\end{array}\right],\left(\begin{array}[]{c}g_{1}\\
q\end{array}\right)\right)=\\
\left(\mathbb{Z}^{2}\left<e_{1},f_{1}+f_{2}\right>,\left[\begin{array}[]{cc}0&1\\
\epsilon_{Q}&{\rm h}(q)\end{array}\right],\left(\begin{array}[]{c}g_{1}\\
q+g_{2}\end{array}\right)\right)\oplus\\
\left(\mathbb{Z}^{2}\left<e_{2}-e_{1},f_{2}-{\rm h}(q)e_{2}\right>,\left[\begin{array}[]{cc}0&1\\
\epsilon_{Q}&-\epsilon_{Q}{\rm h}(q)\end{array}\right],\left(\begin{array}[]{c}0\\
R(q)-{\rm h}(q)g_{1}\end{array}\right)\right)\sim\\
\left(\mathbb{Z}^{2}\left<e_{1},f_{1}+f_{2}\right>,\left[\begin{array}[]{cc}0&1\\
\epsilon_{Q}&{\rm h}(q)\end{array}\right],\left(\begin{array}[]{c}g_{1}\\
q+g_{2}\end{array}\right)\right).

Therefore
\mathrm{Im}(\gamma)\supseteq W_{0}^{Q}(G)
.
∎

We now relate the homomorphisms

\tilde{F}
and
\gamma
.

Proposition 4.27
.

The homomorphisms
\tilde{F}
and
\gamma
fit into the following split short exact sequence:

\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{W_{0}(Q)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{W_{0}(i)}
\textstyle{W_{0}(Q\oplus G)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\tilde{F}}
\scriptstyle{W_{0}(r)}
\textstyle{G\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}Q\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\gamma}
\textstyle{0}

Proof.

We already noted at the start of this subsection that the morphisms
i\colon Q\rightarrow Q\oplus G
and
r\colon Q\oplus G\rightarrow Q
of form parameters are such that
W_{0}(r)\colon W_{0}(Q\oplus G)\to W_{0}(Q)
splits

W_{0}(i)\colon W_{0}(Q)\to W_{0}(Q\oplus G)
.
It follows immediately from the definition that for any nonsingular
Q
-form
(X,\lambda,\mu_{Q})
we have
F(X,\lambda,(\mu_{Q},0))=0
, therefore the composition
W_{0}(Q)\rightarrow W_{0}(Q\oplus G)\rightarrow G\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}Q
is trivial. A straightforward calculation shows that
\tilde{F}\circ\gamma=\textup{Id}_{G\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}Q}
, i.e.
\gamma
is a splitting map. This implies that
\tilde{F}
is surjective, and that the restriction of
\tilde{F}
to
\mathrm{Im}(\gamma)=W_{0}^{Q}(G)
is injective, so
\mathrm{Ker}(\tilde{F})=W_{0}(Q)
, which means that the sequence is exact.
∎

By observing that the map
F
is natural in
Q
and
G
, Proposition
4.27
immediately gives the main result of this section, which is Theorem
1.2
from the introduction (see also Proposition
4.31
):

Theorem 4.28
.

Let
Q
be a form parameter and
G
be an abelian group. Then the restriction of
\tilde{F}
and
\gamma
define isomorphisms

W_{0}^{Q}(G)\cong G\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}Q,

which are natural in
G
and
Q
.
∎

4.4.
Computing Witt groups via splittings of form parameters

By combining the results of Sections
4.1
and
4.3
we can calculate the Witt group
W_{0}(P)
for any form parameter
P
. The necessary ingredients are summarised in the following table:

Table 1

\begin{array}[]{c|cccc}~~~Q&\mathbb{Z}\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}Q&\mathbb{Z}_{n}\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}Q&~~~~~G\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}Q&~~~~~W_{0}(Q)\\
\hline\cr\hline\cr Q_{+}&\mathbb{Z}&\mathbb{Z}_{n}&S^{2}(G)&\mathbb{Z}\\
\mathbb{Z}^{P}&\mathbb{Z}\oplus\mathbb{Z}&\mathbb{Z}_{(n,2)n}\oplus\mathbb{Z}_{n/(2,n)}&P^{2}(G)&\mathbb{Z}\oplus\mathbb{Z}\\
\mathbb{Z}^{P}_{k}&~~~\mathbb{Z}\oplus\mathbb{Z}_{2^{k}}&\mathbb{Z}_{(n,2)n}\oplus\mathbb{Z}_{(n/(2,n),2^{k})}&-&\qquad\!\mathbb{Z}\oplus\mathbb{Z}_{2^{k-1}}\\
Q^{+}&\mathbb{Z}&\mathbb{Z}_{(2,n)n}&\Gamma(G)&\mathbb{Z}\\
\hline\cr Q_{-}&\mathbb{Z}_{2}&\mathbb{Z}_{(2,n)}&\Lambda_{1}(G)&\mathbb{Z}_{2}\\
\mathbb{Z}^{\Lambda}_{k}&\mathbb{Z}_{2^{k}}&\mathbb{Z}_{(2^{k},n)}&-&0\\
Q^{-}&0&0&\Lambda(G)&0\\
\end{array}

Table 1 lists the values of the quadratic tensor product
G\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}Q
for every cyclic group
G
and indecomposable form parameter
Q
(where
(a,b)
denotes the greatest common divisor of
a
and
b
), which can be computed directly from Definition
4.6
(or using the results of Section
4.2
). In some cases we also have a simple description of the functor
{-}\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}Q
(see Example
4.7
). Finally, we include the Witt groups
W_{0}(Q)
which were computed in Theorem
4.3
.

Now suppose that
P
is an arbitrary form parameter. By Theorem
3.3
it has a maximal splitting
P\cong Q\oplus G
(and the isomorphism classes of
Q
and
G
are uniquely determined by that of
P
). Then we have the following:

Theorem 4.29
.

a) A splitting
P\cong Q\oplus G
of a form parameter
P

defines an isomorphism

W_{0}(P)\cong W_{0}(Q\oplus G)\cong W_{0}(Q)\oplus W_{0}^{Q}(G)\cong W_{0}(Q)\oplus(G\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}Q).

b) For any pair of abelian groups
G_{1}
and
G_{2}
, and any form parameter
Q
we have

(G_{1}\oplus G_{2})\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}Q\cong(G_{1}\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}Q)\oplus(G_{2}\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}Q)\oplus(G_{1}\otimes G_{2}).

Proof.

Part a) follows from Theorem
4.28
, and Part b) follows from
[
Bau1
, Proposition 4.2 (3)]
, since a form parameter is a quadratic module
Q
with
Q_{ee}=\mathbb{Z}
.
∎

Next we give some simple examples of computations using Theorem
4.29
.

Example 4.30
.

For a positive integer
l
, define
\bar{\delta}(l),\delta(l)\in\mathbb{Z}^{+}
by

\bar{\delta}(l)=\begin{cases}1&\text{if $l$ is odd,}\\
2&\text{if $l$ is even,}\end{cases}\qquad\text{and}\qquad\delta(l)=\bar{\delta}(l)l.

a)

There is an isomorphism
W_{0}(Q_{+}\oplus\mathbb{Z}_{l})\cong\mathbb{Z}(8\sigma^{*})\oplus\mathbb{Z}_{l}
, where the torsion summand is generated by the Witt class of the form

\left(\mathbb{Z}^{2},\left[\begin{array}[]{cc}0&1\\
1&0\end{array}\right],\left(\begin{array}[]{c}(0,1)\\
(0,1)\end{array}\right)\right).

b)

There is an isomorphism
W_{0}(Q^{+}\oplus\mathbb{Z}_{l})\cong\mathbb{Z}(\sigma^{*})\oplus\mathbb{Z}_{\delta(l)}
, where the torsion summand is generated by the Witt class of the form

\bigl((\mathbb{Z},[1],(1,0))\oplus(\mathbb{Z},[-1],(-1,1)\bigr)\cong\left(\mathbb{Z}^{2},\left[\begin{array}[]{cc}0&1\\
1&1\end{array}\right],\left(\begin{array}[]{c}(0,1)\\
(1,0)\end{array}\right)\right).

c)

There is an isomorphism
W_{0}(Q_{-}\oplus\mathbb{Z}_{l})\cong\mathbb{Z}_{2}(c^{*})\oplus\mathbb{Z}_{\bar{\delta}(l)}
, where the second summand is generated by the Witt class of the form

\left(\mathbb{Z}^{2},\left[\begin{array}[]{cc}0&1\\
-1&0\end{array}\right],\left(\begin{array}[]{c}(0,1)\\
(0,1)\end{array}\right)\right).

d)

We have
W_{0}(Q^{-}\oplus\mathbb{Z}_{l})=0
.

To consider the naturality of the isomorphisms in Theorem
4.29
,
we recall from Definition
2.12
that a morphism
\alpha\colon Q_{1}\oplus G_{1}\to Q_{2}\oplus G_{2}
of split form parameters
is split if
\alpha=\alpha_{Q}\oplus\alpha_{G}
for a morphism
\alpha_{Q}\colon Q_{1}\to Q_{2}
and a homomorphism
\alpha_{G}\colon G_{1}\to G_{2}
. A morphism

\alpha\colon P_{1}\to P_{2}
is splittable
if there are splittings of
P_{1}
and
P_{2}
with respect to which
\alpha
is split. By the naturality of the isomorphism in Theorem
4.28
(and the discussion after Definition
4.17
), we get the following:

Proposition 4.31
.

Let
\alpha\colon P_{1}\to P_{2}
be a splittable morphism of form parameters and let

\alpha=\alpha_{Q}\oplus\alpha_{G}
be a splitting of
\alpha

with respect to splittings
P_{1}\cong Q_{1}\oplus G_{1}
and
P_{2}=Q_{2}\oplus G_{2}
.
Then

W_{0}(\alpha)=W_{0}(\alpha_{Q})\oplus(\alpha_{G}\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\alpha_{Q})\colon W_{0}(P_{1})\cong W_{0}(Q_{1})\oplus(G_{1}\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}Q_{1})\to W_{0}(P_{2})\cong W_{0}(Q_{2})\oplus(G_{2}\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}Q_{2}).\qed

Remark
.

If the splittings of
P_{1}
and
P_{2}
in Proposition
4.31
are maximal,
then
\alpha_{Q}
is the composition of some standard morphisms and isomorphisms by Theorem
3.24
, so
W_{0}(\alpha_{Q})
can be determined using Theorem
4.3
.

Example 4.32
.

The morphism of form parameters
Q_{-}\oplus\mathbb{Z}_{2}\xrightarrow{\textup{i}\oplus{\rm Id}}\mathbb{Z}^{\Lambda}_{2}\oplus\mathbb{Z}_{2}
induces the zero map

W_{0}(Q_{-}\oplus\mathbb{Z}_{2})\cong(\mathbb{Z}_{2}(c^{*})\oplus\mathbb{Z}_{2})\xrightarrow{~0~}(0\oplus\mathbb{Z}_{2})\cong W_{0}(\mathbb{Z}^{\Lambda}_{2}\oplus\mathbb{Z}_{2}).

5.
A natural description of Witt groups

By the results of the previous section we can determine the isomorphism type of
W_{0}(P)
for any form parameter
P
, by choosing a maximal splitting. Proposition
4.31
can also be used to determine the homomorphism between the Witt groups induced by a splittable morphism between form parameters. However, as Example
2.13
shows, not all morphisms are splittable (with respect to some non-trivial splittings of the form parameters).

In this section we give a natural description of the group
W_{0}(P)
, for any form parameter
P
, which, in particular, allows us to determine the homomorphism induced by any morphism of form parameters.

We begin by outlining the strategy.
Recall that for any symmetric form parameter
P
there is an extended symmetrisation morphism,

\textup{es}\colon P\to Q^{+}\oplus SP
.
In fact, the morphisms
es
define a natural transformation from the identity to the extended symmetrisation functor
\textup{ES}\colon\mathbf{FP}_{+}\rightarrow\mathbf{FP}_{+}
, where
\textup{ES}(P):=Q^{+}\oplus SP
on objects and
\textup{ES}(\alpha):=\textup{Id}_{Q^{+}}\oplus S\alpha
on morphisms. By Theorem
4.29
, there is a natural isomorphism
W_{0}(Q^{+}\oplus SP)\cong\mathbb{Z}\oplus\Gamma(SP)
,
and we will show that the induced homomorphism

W_{0}(\textup{es})\colon W_{0}(P)\to W_{0}(Q^{+}\oplus SP)\cong\mathbb{Z}\oplus\Gamma(SP)

is injective. Then we will determine
\mathrm{Im}(W_{0}(\textup{es}))
as a natural subgroup of
\mathbb{Z}\oplus\Gamma(SP)
.
Then, for any morphism
\alpha\colon P\rightarrow P^{\prime}
, we can compute the induced map
W_{0}(\alpha)\colon W_{0}(P)\to W_{0}(P^{\prime})
as the restriction of
\textup{Id}_{\mathbb{Z}}\oplus\Gamma(S\alpha)\colon\mathbb{Z}\oplus\Gamma(SP)\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}\oplus\Gamma(SP)
,
which is a homomorphism
\mathrm{Im}(W_{0}(\textup{es}_{P}))\rightarrow\mathrm{Im}(W_{0}(\textup{es}_{P^{\prime}}))
.

Dually, for any anti-symmetric form parameter
P
there is an extended quadratic lift morphism,

\textup{eql}\colon Q_{-}\oplus P_{e}\to P
,
and the morphisms
eql
define a natural transformation from the extended quadratic lift functor

EQL
to the identity, where
\textup{EQL}\colon\mathbf{FP}_{-}\rightarrow\mathbf{FP}_{-}
has
\textup{EQL}(P):=Q_{-}\oplus P_{e}
on objects and
\textup{EQL}(\alpha):=\textup{Id}_{Q_{-}}\oplus\alpha
on morphisms.
By Theorem
4.29
, there is a natural isomorphism
W_{0}(Q_{-}\oplus P_{e})\cong\mathbb{Z}_{2}\oplus\Lambda_{1}(P_{e})
. We will show that the induced homomorphism

W_{0}(\textup{eql})\colon W_{0}(Q_{-}\oplus P_{e})\cong\mathbb{Z}_{2}\oplus\Lambda_{1}(P_{e})\to W_{0}(P)

is surjective and determine
\mathrm{Ker}(W_{0}(\textup{eql}))
as a natural subgroup of
\mathbb{Z}_{2}\oplus\Lambda_{1}(P_{e})
.
Then, for any morphism
\alpha\colon P\rightarrow P^{\prime}
, the homomorphism

W_{0}(\alpha)\colon W_{0}(P)\to W_{0}(P^{\prime})
can be computed by identifying it with the homomorphism
\mathbb{Z}_{2}\oplus\Lambda_{1}(P_{e})/\mathrm{Ker}(W_{0}(\textup{eql}_{P}))\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}_{2}\oplus\Lambda_{1}(P^{\prime}_{e})/\mathrm{Ker}(W_{0}(\textup{eql}_{P^{\prime}}))
,
which is induced by the homomorphism
\textup{Id}_{\mathbb{Z}_{2}}\oplus\Lambda_{1}(\alpha)\colon\mathbb{Z}_{2}\oplus\Lambda_{1}(P_{e})\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}_{2}\oplus\Lambda_{1}(P^{\prime}_{e})
.

5.1.
Symmetric form parameters

First we consider the restriction of the Witt group functor to the category of symmetric form parameters,
W_{0}\colon\mathbf{FP}_{+}\to\mathbf{Ab}
.

Lemma 5.1
.

If
Q
is an indecomposable symmetric form parameter, then the induced homomorphism

W_{0}(\textup{es})\colon W_{0}(Q)\to W_{0}(Q^{+}\oplus SQ)
is injective.

Proof.

Since
Q^{+}
is the terminal object, the composition
Q\rightarrow Q^{+}\oplus SQ\rightarrow Q^{+}
is the unique morphism,
and the induced map
W_{0}(Q)\to W_{0}(Q^{+}\oplus SQ)\cong W_{0}(Q^{+})\oplus W_{0}^{Q^{+}}(SQ)\rightarrow W_{0}(Q^{+})

is given by Theorem
4.3
. To compute the other component
W_{0}(Q)\rightarrow W_{0}^{Q^{+}}(SQ)\cong SQ\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}Q^{+}

we will use the split norm
F
from Definition
4.18
, which induces the isomorphism
\tilde{F}
(Theorem
4.28
).

If
Q=Q_{+}
, then
SQ_{+}=0
, and the map
W_{0}(Q_{+})\to W_{0}(Q^{+}\oplus SQ_{+})\cong W_{0}(Q^{+})
is equal to
the inclusion
\mathbb{Z}(8\sigma^{*})\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}(\sigma^{*})
.

If
Q=Q^{+}
, then
SQ^{+}=\mathbb{Z}_{2}
. The composition
W_{0}(Q^{+})\to W_{0}(Q^{+}\oplus SQ^{+})\rightarrow W_{0}(Q^{+})
is of course the identity, hence the map
W_{0}(Q^{+})\to W_{0}(Q^{+}\oplus SQ^{+})
is injective.

If
Q=\mathbb{Z}^{P}
, then
S\mathbb{Z}^{P}=\mathbb{Z}
and
W_{0}^{Q^{+}}(S\mathbb{Z}^{P})\cong\mathbb{Z}\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}Q^{+}\cong\mathbb{Z}
by Theorem
4.29
. The generators of
W_{0}(\mathbb{Z}^{P})\cong\mathbb{Z}(\sigma^{*})\oplus\mathbb{Z}(\rho_{\infty}^{*})
are (the equivalence classes of)
\sigma^{*}=(\mathbb{Z},1,(1,0))
and

\rho_{\infty}^{*}=\left(\mathbb{Z}^{2},\left[\begin{smallmatrix}0&1\\
1&0\end{smallmatrix}\right],\left(\begin{smallmatrix}(0,1)\\
(0,-1)\end{smallmatrix}\right)\right)

(it is easy to verify that for these forms we have
\sigma(\sigma^{*})=\rho_{\infty}(\rho_{\infty}^{*})=1
and
\rho_{\infty}(\sigma^{*})=\sigma(\rho_{\infty}^{*})=0
). The extended symmetrisation map is given by the homomorphism
\left(\begin{smallmatrix}1&0\\
1&2\end{smallmatrix}\right)\colon\mathbb{Z}^{P}_{e}=\mathbb{Z}\oplus\mathbb{Z}\rightarrow(Q^{+}\oplus S\mathbb{Z}^{P})_{e}=\mathbb{Z}\oplus\mathbb{Z}
, so the generators are mapped to
(\mathbb{Z},1,(1,1))
and
(\mathbb{Z}^{2},\left[\begin{smallmatrix}0&1\\
1&0\end{smallmatrix}\right],\left(\begin{smallmatrix}(0,2)\\
(0,-2)\end{smallmatrix}\right))
respectively. Their images under
F
are
1\otimes 1
and
[2,-2]\otimes 1
, and under the isomorphism
\mathbb{Z}\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}Q^{+}\cong\mathbb{Z}
these correspond to
1
and
-8
. Therefore the extended symmetrisation map induces the homomorphism
\left(\begin{smallmatrix}1&0\\
1&-8\end{smallmatrix}\right)\colon W_{0}(\mathbb{Z}^{P})=\mathbb{Z}\oplus\mathbb{Z}\rightarrow W_{0}(Q^{+}\oplus S\mathbb{Z}^{P})=\mathbb{Z}\oplus\mathbb{Z}
, and this is injective.

If
Q=\mathbb{Z}^{P}_{k}
, then
S\mathbb{Z}^{P}_{k}=\mathbb{Z}_{2^{k+1}}
and
W_{0}^{Q^{+}}\!\!(S\mathbb{Z}^{P}_{k})\cong\mathbb{Z}_{2^{k+1}}\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}Q^{+}\cong\mathbb{Z}_{2^{k+2}}
by Theorem
4.29
. The generators of
W_{0}(\mathbb{Z}^{P}_{k})\cong\mathbb{Z}(\sigma^{*})\oplus\mathbb{Z}_{2^{k-1}}(\rho_{k}^{*})
are
\sigma^{*}=(\mathbb{Z},1,(1,0))
and
\rho_{k}^{*}=(\mathbb{Z}^{2},\left[\begin{smallmatrix}0&1\\
1&0\end{smallmatrix}\right],\left(\begin{smallmatrix}(0,1)\\
(0,-1)\end{smallmatrix}\right))
. The extended symmetrisation map is given by

\left(\begin{smallmatrix}1&0\\
1&2\end{smallmatrix}\right)\colon(\mathbb{Z}^{P}_{k})_{e}=\mathbb{Z}\oplus\mathbb{Z}_{2^{k}}\rightarrow(Q^{+}\oplus S\mathbb{Z}^{P}_{k})_{e}=\mathbb{Z}\oplus\mathbb{Z}_{2^{k+1}}
, so the generators are mapped to
(\mathbb{Z},1,(1,1))
and
(\mathbb{Z}^{2},\left[\begin{smallmatrix}0&1\\
1&0\end{smallmatrix}\right],\left(\begin{smallmatrix}(0,2)\\
(0,-2)\end{smallmatrix}\right))
respectively. Their images under
F
are
1\otimes 1
and
[2,-2]\otimes 1
, and under the isomorphism
\mathbb{Z}_{2^{k+1}}\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}Q^{+}\cong\mathbb{Z}_{2^{k+2}}
these correspond to
1
and
-8
. Therefore the extended symmetrisation map induces

\left(\begin{smallmatrix}1&0\\
1&-8\end{smallmatrix}\right)\colon W_{0}(\mathbb{Z}^{P}_{k})=\mathbb{Z}\oplus\mathbb{Z}_{2^{k-1}}\rightarrow W_{0}(Q^{+}\oplus S\mathbb{Z}^{P}_{k})=\mathbb{Z}\oplus\mathbb{Z}_{2^{k+2}}
, and this map is also injective.
∎

Lemma 5.2
.

If
P
is a symmetric form parameter, then

W_{0}(\textup{es})\colon W_{0}(P)\to W_{0}(Q^{+}\oplus SP)
is injective.

Proof.

By Theorem
3.3
it is enough to prove the statement for form parameters of the form
P=Q\oplus G
, where
Q
is an indecomposable form parameter and
G
is an abelian group.

We have
SP\cong SQ\oplus G
, and the
map

\textup{es}\colon P=Q\oplus G\rightarrow Q^{+}\oplus SP\cong(Q^{+}\oplus SQ)\oplus G
splits as the sum of
\textup{es}\colon Q\rightarrow Q^{+}\oplus SQ
and
\textup{Id}_{G}
. Therefore the induced map

W_{0}(P)=W_{0}(Q)\oplus W_{0}^{Q}(G)\rightarrow W_{0}(Q^{+}\oplus SP)\cong W_{0}(Q^{+}\oplus SQ)\oplus W_{0}^{Q^{+}\oplus SQ}(G)

splits as the sum of
W_{0}(Q)\rightarrow W_{0}(Q^{+}\oplus SQ)
and the map

W_{0}^{Q}(G)\cong G\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}Q\rightarrow W_{0}^{Q^{+}\oplus SQ}(G)\cong G\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}(Q^{+}\oplus SQ)\cong(G\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}Q^{+})\oplus(G\otimes SQ)\cong\Gamma(G)\oplus(G\otimes SQ).

The former is injective by Lemma
5.1
.
By Proposition
4.11
b) there is a pullback diagram

\textstyle{G\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}Q\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{G\otimes SQ\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\Gamma(G)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{G\otimes\mathbb{Z}_{2}.}

The map
