W_{0}^{Q}(G)\cong G\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}Q\rightarrow W_{0}^{Q^{+}\oplus SQ}(G)\cong\Gamma(G)\oplus(G\otimes SQ)
is the direct sum of the maps
G\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}Q\rightarrow\Gamma(G)
and
G\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}Q\rightarrow G\otimes SQ
in this diagram, hence it is injective too.
∎

Next we determine the image of
W_{0}(\textup{es})\colon W_{0}(P)\to W_{0}(Q^{+}\oplus SP)\cong\mathbb{Z}\oplus\Gamma(SP)
. Given an abelian group
A
, we will use the identification
\Gamma(A)\cong A\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}Q^{+}
to describe elements of
\Gamma(A)
(in particular,
\Gamma(A)
is generated by the symbols
a\otimes 1
and
[a,b]\otimes 1
for
a,b\in A
). Note that there is a short exact sequence

\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{S^{2}(A)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\Gamma(A)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{A\otimes\mathbb{Z}_{2}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0}

where the first map sends
ab
to
[a,b]\otimes 1
, and the second map sends
[a,b]\otimes 1
to
0
and
a\otimes 1
to
a\otimes 1
(see Proposition
4.11
b) or
[
Bau1
, Remark 2.10]
).

Definition 5.3
.

Let
v\colon A\to\mathbb{Z}_{2}
be a homomorphism. We define the subgroup

\Sigma(v):=\left<(1,x\otimes 1),(0,[k_{1},k_{2}]\otimes 1),(8,0)\bigm|x\in v^{-1}(1),k_{1},k_{2}\in\mathrm{Ker}(v)\right>\leq\mathbb{Z}\oplus\Gamma(A).

We note that if
\alpha\colon v_{1}\rightarrow v_{2}
is a morphism in
\mathbf{Ab}/\mathbb{Z}_{2}
, then the induced map
\textup{Id}_{\mathbb{Z}}\oplus\Gamma(\alpha)\colon\mathbb{Z}\oplus\Gamma(A_{1})\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}\oplus\Gamma(A_{2})
sends the generators of
\Sigma(v_{1})
to generators of
\Sigma(v_{2})
. This shows that
\Sigma
defines a functor
\mathbf{Ab}/\mathbb{Z}_{2}\to\mathbf{Ab}
.

Lemma 5.4
.

Let
v\colon A\to\mathbb{Z}_{2}
be a homomorphism.

a) If
v=0
, then
\Sigma(v)=\left<(0,[k_{1},k_{2}]\otimes 1),(8,0)\bigm|k_{1},k_{2}\in\mathrm{Ker}(v)=A\right>
.

b) If
v\neq 0
, then
\Sigma(v)=\left<(1,x\otimes 1)\bigm|x\in v^{-1}(1)\right>
.

Proof.

Part a) holds, because
v^{-1}(1)=\emptyset
.

b) Let
\Sigma^{\prime}(v)
denote the right hand side, we need to show that it contains the remaining generators of
\Sigma(v)
. First note that if
x\in v^{-1}(1)
and
k\in\mathrm{Ker}(v)
, then
[k,x]\otimes 1+k\otimes 1=(x+k)\otimes 1-x\otimes 1
, hence
(0,[k,x]\otimes 1+k\otimes 1)\in\Sigma^{\prime}(v)
. We have
[k_{1},k_{2}]\otimes 1=([k_{1},x+k_{2}]\otimes 1+k_{1}\otimes 1)-([k_{1},x]\otimes 1+k_{1}\otimes 1)
for any
k_{1},k_{2}\in\mathrm{Ker}(v)
and
x\in v^{-1}(1)
, therefore
(0,[k_{1},k_{2}]\otimes 1)\in\Sigma^{\prime}(v)
. Finally, for any
y\in A
, we have
[2y,2y]\otimes 1=2y\otimes 2=y\otimes 2+y\otimes 2+[y,y]\otimes 2=y\otimes 8
. So, with any
x\in v^{-1}(1)
, we have
(8,0)=8(1,x\otimes 1)-(0,x\otimes 8)\in\Sigma^{\prime}(v)
.
∎

Theorem 5.5
.

Let
P
be a symmetric form parameter. Then under the identification
W_{0}(Q^{+}\oplus SP)\cong\mathbb{Z}\oplus\Gamma(SP)
we have
\mathrm{Im}(W_{0}(\textup{es}))=\Sigma(v_{P})
.

Proof of Theorem
5.5
.

First assume that
v_{P}=0
, i.e.
P\cong Q_{+}\oplus SP
. It follows from Lemmas
5.1
and
5.2
that the map
W_{0}(\textup{es})\colon W_{0}(P)\cong W_{0}(Q_{+}\oplus SP)\cong W_{0}(Q_{+})\oplus W_{0}^{Q_{+}}(SP)\rightarrow W_{0}(Q^{+}\oplus SP)\cong W_{0}(Q^{+})\oplus W_{0}^{Q^{+}}(SP)
splits as the sum of the inclusions
W_{0}(Q_{+})\cong\mathbb{Z}(8\sigma^{*})\to W_{0}(Q^{+})\cong\mathbb{Z}(\sigma^{*})
and
W_{0}^{Q_{+}}(SP)\cong S^{2}(SP)\rightarrow W_{0}^{Q^{+}}(SP)\cong\Gamma(SP)
. Therefore the image of
W_{0}(\textup{es})
is generated by
(8,0)
and the elements
(0,[k_{1},k_{2}]\otimes 1)
for
k_{1},k_{2}\in SP=\mathrm{Ker}(v_{P})
, so it is equal to
\Sigma(v_{P})
by Lemma
5.4
a).

Now assume that
v_{P}\neq 0
. By adding the metabolic
P
-form

\left(\mathbb{Z}^{2},\left[\begin{array}[]{cc}0&1\\
1&{\rm h}(q)\end{array}\right],\left(\begin{array}[]{c}0\\
q\end{array}\right)\right)

(where
q\in v_{P}^{-1}(1)
) we get that every element of
W_{0}(P)
has a representative
(X,\lambda,\mu)
such that
\lambda
is indefinite and odd. By the classification of nonsingular indefinite forms (see
[
M-H
, Ch. II. Theorem (5.3)]
),
(X,\lambda,\mu)
is isomorphic to the sum of
P
-forms of the form
(\mathbb{Z},[\pm 1],q)
(where
{\rm h}(q)=\pm 1
). Therefore elements of this form generate
W_{0}(P)
. The image of such a form under extended symmetrisation is
(\mathbb{Z},[\pm 1],(\pm 1,\pi_{P}(q)))
, which corresponds to
(\pm 1,\pi_{P}(q)\otimes(\pm 1))=\pm(1,\pi_{P}(q)\otimes 1)
under the isomorphism
W_{0}(Q_{+}\oplus SP)\cong\mathbb{Z}\oplus\Gamma(SP)
(see Definition
4.18
). Since
\{\pi_{P}(q)\mid{\rm h}(q)=\pm 1\}=v_{P}^{-1}(1)
, this implies that
\mathrm{Im}(W_{0}(\textup{es}))=\left<(1,x\otimes 1)\mid x\in v_{P}^{-1}(1)\right>
. This is equal to
\Sigma(v_{P})
by Lemma
5.4
b).
∎

Using Lemma
5.2
, Theorem
5.5
and the fact that
extended symmetrisation
is a natural transformation, we get

Corollary 5.6
.

The homomorphisms induced by extended symmetrisation define a natural isomorphism
W_{0}\cong\Sigma\circ v_{(-)}:\mathbf{FP}_{+}\to\mathbf{Ab}
. ∎

To give a more explicit description of the group
\Sigma(v)
, we include Theorem
5.8
below, which requires the following

Definition 5.7
.

Let
v\colon A\to\mathbb{Z}_{2}
be a homomorphism. We define the subgroups

\displaystyle\Phi(v)

\displaystyle:=\left<x\otimes 1,[k,x]\otimes 1+k\otimes 1,[k_{1},k_{2}]\otimes 1\bigm|x\in v^{-1}(1),k,k_{1},k_{2}\in\mathrm{Ker}(v)\right>\leq\Gamma(A),

\displaystyle\Psi(v)

\displaystyle:=\left<[k,x]\otimes 1+k\otimes 1,[k_{1},k_{2}]\otimes 1\bigm|x\in v^{-1}(1),k,k_{1},k_{2}\in\mathrm{Ker}(v)\right>\leq\Phi(v).

Note that, by the formula in the proof of Lemma
5.4
, the generators
[k,x]\otimes 1+k\otimes 1
of
\Phi(v)
could be omitted, and that if
v=0
, then
\Phi(v)=\Psi(v)
. We also define the groups

\displaystyle C(v)

\displaystyle:=\begin{cases}\mathbb{Z}_{8}&\text{if }v=0,\\
\Phi(v)/\Psi(v)&\text{if }v\neq 0,\end{cases}

\displaystyle\Upsilon(v)

\displaystyle:=\begin{cases}\mathbb{Z}_{8}\oplus\Gamma(A)/\Psi(v)&\text{if }v=0,\\
\Gamma(A)/\Psi(v)&\text{if }v\neq 0.\end{cases}

Theorem 5.8
.

Let
v\colon A\to\mathbb{Z}_{2}
be a homomorphism.

a)

We have

C(v)\cong\begin{cases}\mathbb{Z}_{4}&\text{if $v\cong 1_{1}\oplus G$ for some $G$,}\\
\mathbb{Z}_{8}&\text{otherwise}.\end{cases}

Moreover, if
v\neq 0
, then the elements of
C(v)
of the form
[x\otimes 1]
with
x\in v^{-1}(1)
are all equal to each other, and this element generates
C(v)
.

b)

There is a commutative diagram with short exact rows and columns

\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\Sigma(v)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathbb{Z}\oplus\Phi(v)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\iota-q}
\textstyle{C(v)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0}
\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\Sigma(v)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathbb{Z}\oplus\Gamma(A)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{0+u_{v}}
\scriptstyle{\iota-\bar{q}}
\textstyle{\Upsilon(v)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\tilde{u}_{v}}
\textstyle{0}
\textstyle{\mathrm{Ker}(v_{2})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathrm{Ker}(v_{2})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0}
\textstyle{0,}

where the following hold:

\bullet

v_{2}=v\otimes\textup{Id}_{\mathbb{Z}_{2}}\colon A\otimes\mathbb{Z}_{2}\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}_{2}\otimes\mathbb{Z}_{2}=\mathbb{Z}_{2}
, hence
\mathrm{Ker}(v_{2})\leq A\otimes\mathbb{Z}_{2}
;

\bullet

The homomorphism
\iota\colon\mathbb{Z}\rightarrow C(v)\leq\Upsilon(v)
is defined by

\iota(1)=\begin{cases}1&\text{if }v=0,\\
[x\otimes 1]\text{~for some $x\in v^{-1}(1)$}&\text{if }v\neq 0;\end{cases}

\bullet

The homomorphism
q\colon\Phi(v)\rightarrow C(v)
is the quotient map
\Phi(v)\rightarrow\Phi(v)/\Psi(v)
if
v\neq 0
, and trivial if
v=0
;

\bullet

The homomorphism
\bar{q}\colon\Gamma(A)\rightarrow\Upsilon(v)
is the quotient map
\Gamma(A)\rightarrow\Gamma(A)/\Psi(v)
;

\bullet

The homomorphism
u_{v}\colon\Gamma(A)\rightarrow A\otimes\mathbb{Z}_{2}
is given by
u_{v}([a_{1},a_{2}]\otimes 1)=a_{1}\otimes v(a_{2})+a_{2}\otimes v(a_{1})
and
u_{v}(a\otimes 1)=a\otimes 1+a\otimes v(a)
;

\bullet

The homomorphism
\tilde{u}_{v}\colon\Upsilon(v)\rightarrow A\otimes\mathbb{Z}_{2}
is the map
\Gamma(A)/\Psi(v)\rightarrow A\otimes\mathbb{Z}_{2}
induced by
u_{v}
(precomposed with the projection
\mathbb{Z}_{8}\oplus\Gamma(A)/\Psi(v)\rightarrow\Gamma(A)/\Psi(v)
if
v=0
);

\bullet

The unlabelled maps are the obvious inclusions.

Remark 5.9
.

Theorem
5.8
shows that
\Sigma(v)
can be described as the kernel of a map
\mathbb{Z}\oplus\Gamma(A)\rightarrow\Upsilon(v)
, where
\Upsilon(v)
is an extension of the
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-vector space
\mathrm{Ker}(v_{2})
by the cyclic group
C(v)
. Alternatively, it is the subgroup of elements
(n,\alpha)\in\mathbb{Z}\oplus\Phi(v)
such that
n\equiv q(\alpha)
mod
4
or
8
(depending on
C(v)
), where
\Phi(v)
is the kernel of the surjective map
u_{v}\colon\Gamma(A)\rightarrow\mathrm{Ker}(v_{2})
. In particular, we see that
\Sigma(v)\cap(0\oplus\Gamma(A))=\mathrm{Ker}(\bar{q})=\Psi(v)
, and that the image of the composition
\Sigma(v)\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}\oplus\Gamma(A)\rightarrow\Gamma(A)
is
\Phi(v)
.

Proof of Theorem
5.8
.

a) If
v=0
, then the statement holds by definition. In the rest of this proof we restrict to the
v\neq 0
case. Then the elements
[x\otimes 1]
with
v(x)=1
generate
\Phi(v)/\Psi(v)
. If
v(x)=v(y)=1
, then
x\otimes 1-y\otimes 1=(x-y)\otimes 1+[y,x-y]\otimes 1\in\Psi(v)
, so all of these generators coincide. Hence
\Phi(v)/\Psi(v)
is cyclic.

By Theorem
3.14

v
is isomorphic to
v_{0}\oplus G
for some abelian group
G
, where
v_{0}\colon A_{0}\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}_{2}
is either
1_{k}\colon\mathbb{Z}_{2^{k}}\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}_{2}
for some positive integer
k
or
1_{\infty}\colon\mathbb{Z}\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}_{2}
. Since
\Phi(v)
and
\Psi(v)
depend functorially on
v
, the morphism
v_{0}\rightarrow v_{0}\oplus G\cong v
induces a homomorphism
C(v_{0})\rightarrow C(v)
. This map is injective, because the composition
v_{0}\rightarrow v_{0}\oplus G\rightarrow v_{0}
is the identity, and surjective, because it maps a generator of the cyclic group
C(v_{0})
(of the form
[x\otimes 1]
) into a generator of
C(v)
. Therefore
C(v)\cong C(v_{0})
.

We have
\Gamma(A_{0})\cong\mathbb{Z}_{2^{k+1}}
or
\Gamma(A_{0})\cong\mathbb{Z}
, generated by
1\otimes 1
. So from the definitions we see that in all cases
\Phi(v_{0})=\Gamma(A_{0})
and
\Psi(v_{0})=8\Gamma(A_{0})
. Moreover,
\Gamma(A_{0})/8\Gamma(A_{0})\cong\mathbb{Z}_{4}
if
k=1
, and
\Gamma(A_{0})/8\Gamma(A_{0})\cong\mathbb{Z}_{8}
otherwise.

b) First consider the top horizontal sequence. It follows from the definitions that
\Sigma(v)\leq\mathbb{Z}\oplus\Phi(v)
. The map
\iota
is well-defined, because if
v(x)=v(y)=1
, then
x\otimes 1-y\otimes 1=(x-y)\otimes 1+[x-y,y]\otimes 1\in\Psi(v)
. Moreover,
\iota
is surjective, hence
\iota-q
is surjective too. We need to prove that the sequence is exact at
\mathbb{Z}\oplus\Phi(v)
. If
v=0
, then
\Sigma(v)=8\mathbb{Z}\oplus\Phi(v)=\mathrm{Ker}(\iota-q)
by Lemma
5.4
a), so the sequence is exact. If
v\neq 0
, then
\Sigma(v)
is generated by the elements
(1,x\otimes 1)
with
x\in v^{-1}(1)
, so
\Sigma(v)\leq\mathrm{Ker}(\iota-q)
. Every element of
\mathbb{Z}\oplus\Phi(v)
can be written as
n(1,x\otimes 1)+(0,\alpha)
for some
n\in\mathbb{Z}
and
\alpha\in\Phi(v)
, and such an element is in
\mathrm{Ker}(\iota-q)
if and only if
\alpha\in\mathrm{Ker}(q)=\Psi(v)
. In the proof of Lemma
5.4
b) we saw that
0\oplus\Psi(v)\leq\Sigma(v)
, and this implies that
\Sigma(v)\geq\mathrm{Ker}(\iota-q)
. Therefore the sequence is exact.

Next consider the middle horizontal sequence. The map
\iota-\bar{q}
is surjective, because
\bar{q}
is surjective onto
\Gamma(A)/\Psi(v)
, and if
v=0
, then
\iota
is surjective onto
\mathbb{Z}_{8}
. If
v=0
, then
\Sigma(v)=8\mathbb{Z}\oplus\Phi(v)=8\mathbb{Z}\oplus\Psi(v)=\mathrm{Ker}(\iota-\bar{q})
. If
v\neq 0
, then, using the observations from the top sequence,
\Sigma(v)
and
\mathrm{Ker}(\iota-\bar{q})
are both equal to the subgroup generated by the elements
(1,x\otimes 1)
(where
x\in v^{-1}(1)
) and
0\oplus\Psi(v)
. Therefore the middle sequence is exact too.

Next we consider the vertical sequence in the middle. The homomorphism
u_{v}
is well-defined, because
u_{v}([a,a]\otimes 1)=0=u_{v}(a\otimes 2)
and
u_{v}((a_{1}+a_{2})\otimes 1)=(a_{1}+a_{2})\otimes 1+(a_{1}+a_{2})\otimes v(a_{1}+a_{2})=u_{v}(a_{1}\otimes 1)+u_{v}(a_{2}\otimes 1)+u_{v}([a_{1},a_{2}]\otimes 1)
for every
a,a_{1},a_{2}\in A
. Moreover,
\mathrm{Im}(u_{v})\leq\mathrm{Ker}(v_{2})
, because
v_{2}(a_{1}\otimes v(a_{2})+a_{2}\otimes v(a_{1}))=v(a_{1})\otimes v(a_{2})+v(a_{2})\otimes v(a_{1})=0\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}
and
v_{2}(a\otimes 1+a\otimes v(a))=v(a)\otimes(1+v(a))=0
.

For the exactness of the sequence we need to show that
\Phi(v)\leq\mathrm{Ker}(u_{v})
and that the induced homomorphism
\Gamma(A)/\Phi(v)\rightarrow\mathrm{Ker}(v_{2})
is an isomorphism. The former is verified by evaluating
u_{v}
on the generators of
\Phi(v)
. For the latter, we show that the induced homomorphism has an inverse
\mathrm{Ker}(v_{2})\rightarrow\Gamma(A)/\Phi(v)
. Namely, we have
\mathrm{Ker}(v_{2})=\{k\otimes 1\mid k\in\mathrm{Ker}(v)\}
and the inverse map is given by
k\otimes 1\mapsto[k\otimes 1]
. This map is well-defined, because
k_{1}\otimes 1=k_{2}\otimes 1\in A\otimes\mathbb{Z}_{2}
if and only if
k_{2}=k_{1}+2a
for some
a\in A
, and
(k_{1}+2a)\otimes 1-k_{1}\otimes 1=2a\otimes 1+[k_{1},2a]\otimes 1\in\Phi(v)
. Here we used that
2a\in\mathrm{Ker}(v)
, so
[k_{1},2a]\otimes 1\in\Phi(v)
, and
2a\otimes 1=4(a\otimes 1)=2([a,a]\otimes 1)\in\Phi(v)
for both
a\in v^{-1}(1)
and
a\in\mathrm{Ker}(v)
. This map is a homomorphism, because
[(k_{1}+k_{2})\otimes 1]-[k_{1}\otimes 1]-[k_{2}\otimes 1]=[[k_{1},k_{2}]\otimes 1]=0\in\Gamma(A)/\Phi(v)
. It is the inverse of the homomorphism induced by
u_{v}
, because
u_{v}(k\otimes 1)=k\otimes 1
for
k\in\mathrm{Ker}(v)
, and the elements
[k\otimes 1]
generate
\Gamma(A)/\Phi(v)
. Therefore
\Gamma(A)/\Phi(v)\cong\mathrm{Ker}(v_{2})
and the sequence is exact.

Finally consider the vertical sequence on the right. Since
u_{v}
induces an isomorphism
\Gamma(A)/\Phi(v)\cong\mathrm{Ker}(v_{2})
and
\Psi(v)\leq\Phi(v)
, the map
\tilde{u}_{v}
is well-defined. If
v=0
, then
\Psi(v)=\Phi(v)
, hence the restriction of
\tilde{u}_{v}
to
\Gamma(A)/\Psi(v)\leq\Upsilon(v)
is an isomorphism, so the sequence is exact. It is also exact if
v\neq 0
, because then it is isomorphic to the sequence
\Phi(v)/\Psi(v)\rightarrow\Gamma(A)/\Psi(v)\rightarrow\Gamma(A)/\Phi(v)
.
∎

Remark 5.10
.

We can give an alternative description of the map
u_{v}
in terms of
(Q^{+}\oplus A)
-forms, using that
\Gamma(A)\cong W_{0}^{Q^{+}}(A)
by Theorem
4.28
. Let
(X,\lambda,(\mu_{0},\mu_{A}))
be a nonsingular
(Q^{+}\oplus A)
-form and
\chi,\bar{\chi}\in A
be characteristic elements for
\lambda
and
v\circ\mu_{A}
respectively; i.e. for all
x\in X
,
\lambda(x,x)\equiv\lambda(\chi,x)
mod
2
and
v(\mu_{A}(x))=\varrho_{2}(\lambda(\bar{\chi},x))
. Since
\lambda
is nonsingular,
\chi
and
\bar{\chi}
are well-defined modulo
2X
. We have

v(\mu_{A}(\chi-\bar{\chi}))=\varrho_{2}(\lambda(\bar{\chi},\chi-\bar{\chi}))=\varrho_{2}(\lambda(\bar{\chi},\chi)-\lambda(\bar{\chi},\bar{\chi}))=0

so
\mu_{A}(\chi-\bar{\chi})\otimes 1\in\mathrm{Ker}(v_{2})
. By comparing the definitions we can see that the image of
[X,\lambda,(\mu_{0},\mu_{A})]\in W_{0}^{Q^{+}}(A)\cong\Gamma(A)
is
u_{v}([X,\lambda,(\mu_{0},\mu_{A})])=\mu_{A}(\chi-\bar{\chi})\otimes 1
. In particular, if
v\circ\mu_{A}
is characteristic for
\lambda
(see Definition
2.27
), then
\chi\equiv\bar{\chi}
mod
2X
, showing that, if
v=v_{P}\colon A=SP\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}_{2}
for some form parameter
P
, then the image of the composition
W_{0}(P)\xrightarrow{W_{0}(\textup{es})}W_{0}(Q^{+}\oplus A)\rightarrow W_{0}^{Q^{+}}(A)
is in
\mathrm{Ker}(u_{v})
(see Lemma
2.29
).

5.2.
Anti-symmetric form parameters

Next we consider the restriction of the Witt group functor to the category of anti-symmetric form parameters,
W_{0}\colon\mathbf{FP}_{-}\to\mathbf{Ab}
.

Lemma 5.11
.

If
Q
is an indecomposable anti-symmetric form parameter, then the induced homomorphism

W_{0}(\textup{eql})\colon W_{0}(Q_{-}\oplus Q_{e})\to W_{0}(Q)
is surjective.

Proof.

If
Q=Q_{-}
, then the composition
Q_{-}\rightarrow Q_{-}\oplus\mathbb{Z}_{2}\rightarrow Q_{-}
is the identity, and if
Q\neq Q_{-}
, then
W_{0}(Q)\cong 0
by Theorem
4.3
.
∎

Lemma 5.12
.

If
P
is an anti-symmetric form parameter,
then
W_{0}(\textup{eql})\colon W_{0}(Q_{-}\oplus P_{e})\to W_{0}(P)
is surjective.

Proof.

Again we prove this using a maximal splitting
P=Q\oplus G
.
We have
P_{e}=Q_{e}\oplus G
, and the induced homomorphism

W_{0}(Q_{-}\oplus Q_{e}\oplus G)\cong W_{0}(Q_{-}\oplus Q_{e})\oplus W_{0}^{Q_{-}\oplus Q_{e}}(G)\rightarrow W_{0}(P)=W_{0}(Q)\oplus W_{0}^{Q}(G)

splits as the sum of
W_{0}(Q_{-}\oplus Q_{e})\rightarrow W_{0}(Q)
and

W_{0}^{Q_{-}\oplus Q_{e}}(G)\cong G\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}(Q_{-}\oplus Q_{e})\cong(G\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}Q_{-})\oplus(G\otimes Q_{e})\cong\Lambda_{1}(G)\oplus(G\otimes Q_{e})\rightarrow W_{0}^{Q}(G)\cong G\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}Q.

The former is surjective by Lemma
5.11
. By Corollary
4.16
b) there is a pushout diagram

\textstyle{G\otimes\mathbb{Z}_{2}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\Lambda_{1}(G)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{G\otimes Q_{e}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{G\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}Q.}

The map
W_{0}^{Q_{-}\oplus Q_{e}}(G)\cong\Lambda_{1}(G)\oplus(G\otimes Q_{e})\rightarrow W_{0}^{Q}(G)\cong G\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}Q
is the sum of the maps
\Lambda_{1}(G)\rightarrow G\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}Q
and
G\otimes Q_{e}\rightarrow G\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}Q
in this diagram, hence it is surjective too.
∎

Remark 5.13
.

Lemma
5.12
has the following alternative proof:
Since every nonsingular anti-symmetric bilinear form is isomorphic to a sum of hyperbolic forms, it is easy to see that the induced homomorphism
\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathbb{Z}}^{\rm ns}(X,\textup{eql})\colon\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathbb{Z}}^{\rm ns}(X,Q_{-}\oplus P_{e})\to\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathbb{Z}}^{\rm ns}(X,P)
is surjective for any torsion free abelian group
X
, and hence
W_{0}(\textup{eql})
induces a surjection on Witt groups.

Let
A
be an abelian group. Recall that

\Lambda_{1}(A)=(A\otimes A)/\bigl\langle{x\otimes y+y\otimes x\mid x,y\in A}\bigr\rangle
. We will denote the image of
x\otimes y\in A\otimes A
in the quotient
\Lambda_{1}(A)
by
x\mathop{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{}_{\wedge}}}{{\otimes}}}\nolimits y
. Recall from Example
4.7
that
A\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}Q_{-}\cong\Lambda_{1}(A)
, under this identification
a\otimes 1
and
[a,b]\otimes 1
correspond to
a\mathop{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{}_{\wedge}}}{{\otimes}}}\nolimits a
and
a\mathop{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{}_{\wedge}}}{{\otimes}}}\nolimits b
respectively. By Proposition
4.15
there is a short exact sequence

\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{A\otimes\mathbb{Z}_{2}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\Lambda_{1}(A)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\Lambda(A)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0}

where the first map sends
a\otimes 1
to
a\mathop{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{}_{\wedge}}}{{\otimes}}}\nolimits a
, and the second map sends
a\mathop{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{}_{\wedge}}}{{\otimes}}}\nolimits b
to
a\wedge b
. By decomposing
A
as a direct sum of cyclic groups, we can see that this short exact sequence splits, but the splitting is not natural.

Definition 5.14
.

Let
v^{\prime}\colon\mathbb{Z}_{2}\to A
be a homomorphism. We define the subgroup

K(v^{\prime})=\bigl\langle{(1,v^{\prime}(1)\mathop{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{}_{\wedge}}}{{\otimes}}}\nolimits v^{\prime}(1)),(0,x\mathop{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{}_{\wedge}}}{{\otimes}}}\nolimits x+x\mathop{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{}_{\wedge}}}{{\otimes}}}\nolimits v^{\prime}(1))\mid x\in A}\bigr\rangle\leq\mathbb{Z}_{2}\oplus\Lambda_{1}(A)

and the quotient group

\Lambda(v^{\prime})=(\mathbb{Z}_{2}\oplus\Lambda_{1}(A))/K(v^{\prime}).

If
\alpha\colon v^{\prime}_{1}\rightarrow v^{\prime}_{2}
is a morphism in
\mathbb{Z}_{2}/\mathbf{Ab}
, then the induced map
\textup{Id}_{\mathbb{Z}_{2}}\oplus\Lambda_{1}(\alpha)\colon\mathbb{Z}_{2}\oplus\Lambda_{1}(A_{1})\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}_{2}\oplus\Lambda_{1}(A_{2})
sends the generators of
K(v^{\prime}_{1})
to generators of
K(v^{\prime}_{2})
. This shows that
K
, and hence
\Lambda
, are functors
\mathbb{Z}_{2}/\mathbf{Ab}\to\mathbf{Ab}
. Also note that if
v^{\prime}=0\colon\mathbb{Z}_{2}\rightarrow A
is the trivial homomorphism, then
\Lambda(v^{\prime})\cong\Lambda(A)
.

Lemma 5.15
.

Let
v^{\prime}\colon\mathbb{Z}_{2}\to A
be a homomorphism and suppose that
v^{\prime}\cong v^{\prime}_{0}\oplus C\colon\mathbb{Z}_{2}\rightarrow B\oplus C
is a maximal splitting of
v^{\prime}
. Then

K(v^{\prime})\cong\begin{cases}\mathbb{Z}_{2}\oplus(C\otimes\mathbb{Z}_{2})&\text{if $v^{\prime}_{0}\cong\iota_{1}$, }\\
\mathbb{Z}_{2}\oplus(A\otimes\mathbb{Z}_{2})&\text{otherwise. }\end{cases}

Proof.

If
v^{\prime}=0
, then
K(v^{\prime})
is generated by
(1,0)
and the elements
(0,x\mathop{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{}_{\wedge}}}{{\otimes}}}\nolimits x)
for every
x\in A
, therefore it is the direct sum of the subgroups
\mathbb{Z}_{2}\oplus 0
and
0\oplus A\otimes\mathbb{Z}_{2}
.

If
v^{\prime}_{0}\cong\iota_{1}
, then
\Lambda_{1}(A)\cong\Lambda_{1}(\mathbb{Z}_{2}\oplus C)\cong\mathbb{Z}_{2}\oplus\mathbb{Z}_{2}\otimes C\oplus\Lambda_{1}(C)
, and
v^{\prime}(1)\mathop{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{}_{\wedge}}}{{\otimes}}}\nolimits v^{\prime}(1)
is the generator of the first component
\Lambda_{1}(\mathbb{Z}_{2})\cong\mathbb{Z}_{2}
. So the subgroup
K(v^{\prime})\leq\mathbb{Z}_{2}\oplus(\mathbb{Z}_{2}\oplus\mathbb{Z}_{2}\otimes C\oplus\Lambda_{1}(C))
is generated by
(1,(1,0,0))
and the elements
(0,(0,1\otimes x,x\mathop{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{}_{\wedge}}}{{\otimes}}}\nolimits x))
for every
x\in C
. Since the map
\mathbb{Z}_{2}\otimes C\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}_{2}\oplus(\mathbb{Z}_{2}\oplus\mathbb{Z}_{2}\otimes C\oplus\Lambda_{1}(C))
,
1\otimes x\mapsto(0,(0,1\otimes x,x\mathop{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{}_{\wedge}}}{{\otimes}}}\nolimits x))
is an isomorphism onto its image, we have
K(v^{\prime})\cong\mathbb{Z}_{2}\oplus\mathbb{Z}_{2}\otimes C
.

If
v^{\prime}_{0}\cong\iota_{k}
,
k\geq 2
, then
\Lambda_{1}(A)\cong\Lambda_{1}(\mathbb{Z}_{2^{k}}\oplus C)\cong\mathbb{Z}_{2}\oplus\mathbb{Z}_{2^{k}}\otimes C\oplus\Lambda_{1}(C)
. We have
v^{\prime}(1)=2^{k-1}\in\mathbb{Z}_{2^{k}}
, so
v^{\prime}(1)\mathop{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{}_{\wedge}}}{{\otimes}}}\nolimits v^{\prime}(1)=0\in\Lambda_{1}(\mathbb{Z}_{2^{k}})\cong\mathbb{Z}_{2}
. For the generator
1\in\mathbb{Z}_{2^{k}}
we have that
1\mathop{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{}_{\wedge}}}{{\otimes}}}\nolimits 1+1\mathop{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{}_{\wedge}}}{{\otimes}}}\nolimits v^{\prime}(1)=1\mathop{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{}_{\wedge}}}{{\otimes}}}\nolimits 1
is the generator of
\Lambda_{1}(\mathbb{Z}_{2^{k}})\cong\mathbb{Z}_{2}
. Therefore
K(v^{\prime})
is generated by
(1,(0,0,0))
,
(0,(1,0,0))
and the elements
(0,(0,2^{k-1}\otimes x,x\mathop{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{}_{\wedge}}}{{\otimes}}}\nolimits x))
for every
x\in C
. Moreover, the map
\mathbb{Z}_{2}\otimes A\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}_{2}\oplus\Lambda_{1}(A)
,
1\otimes x\mapsto(0,x\mathop{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{}_{\wedge}}}{{\otimes}}}\nolimits x+x\mathop{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{}_{\wedge}}}{{\otimes}}}\nolimits v^{\prime}(1))
is an isomorphism onto the subgroup generated by
(0,(1,0,0))
and the elements
(0,(0,2^{k-1}\otimes x,x\mathop{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{}_{\wedge}}}{{\otimes}}}\nolimits x))
for every
x\in C
, showing that

K(v^{\prime})\cong\mathbb{Z}_{2}\oplus(\mathbb{Z}_{2}\otimes A)
.
∎

Lemma 5.16
.

Let
P
be an anti-symmetric form parameter and suppose that
P\cong Q\oplus G
is a maximal splitting of
P
. Then

\mathrm{Ker}\bigl(W_{0}(\textup{eql})\colon W_{0}(Q_{-}\oplus P_{e})\to W_{0}(P)\bigr)\cong\begin{cases}\mathbb{Z}_{2}\oplus(G\otimes\mathbb{Z}_{2})&\text{if $Q\cong Q_{-}$, }\\
\mathbb{Z}_{2}\oplus(P_{e}\otimes\mathbb{Z}_{2})&\text{otherwise. }\end{cases}

Proof.

Recall from Lemma
5.12
that
W_{0}(\textup{eql})\colon W_{0}(Q_{-}\oplus P_{e})\to W_{0}(P)
is the sum of
W_{0}(Q_{-}\oplus Q_{e})\rightarrow W_{0}(Q)
and
W_{0}^{Q_{-}\oplus Q_{e}}(G)\rightarrow W_{0}^{Q}(G)
, where the second map is the sum of the two maps in the pushout square of Corollary
4.16
b). Since this is also a pullback square (see Remark
4.8
), we get that the kernel of the second map is isomorphic to
G\otimes\mathbb{Z}_{2}
. For the first map we have
W_{0}(Q_{-}\oplus Q_{e})\cong W_{0}(Q_{-})\oplus W_{0}^{Q_{-}}(Q_{e})\cong\mathbb{Z}_{2}\oplus\Lambda_{1}(Q_{e})
and
W_{0}(Q)\cong\mathbb{Z}_{2}
if
Q\cong Q_{-}
and
W_{0}(Q)\cong 0
otherwise.

If
Q=Q^{-}
, then the first map is
\mathbb{Z}_{2}\rightarrow 0
, so
\mathrm{Ker}(W_{0}(\textup{eql}))\cong\mathbb{Z}_{2}\oplus G\otimes\mathbb{Z}_{2}=\mathbb{Z}_{2}\oplus P_{e}\otimes\mathbb{Z}_{2}
.

If
Q=Q_{-}
, then the first map is
\mathbb{Z}_{2}\oplus\mathbb{Z}_{2}\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}_{2}
, which is the identity on the first component, which implies that
\mathrm{Ker}(W_{0}(\textup{eql}))\cong\mathbb{Z}_{2}\oplus G\otimes\mathbb{Z}_{2}
. We note that the second component is generated by
1\mathop{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{}_{\wedge}}}{{\otimes}}}\nolimits 1\in\Lambda_{1}(Q_{e})
, and by Definition
4.24
the corresponding element of
W_{0}^{Q_{-}}(Q_{e})
is represented by the
(Q_{-}\oplus Q_{e})
-form

\left(\mathbb{Z}^{2},\left[\begin{array}[]{cc}0&1\\
-1&0\end{array}\right],\left(\begin{array}[]{c}(0,1)\\
(0,1)\end{array}\right)\right)

Its image under
W_{0}(\textup{eql})
has Arf-invariant
1
, so it is the generator of
W_{0}(Q)
. Therefore the map
\mathbb{Z}_{2}\oplus\mathbb{Z}_{2}\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}_{2}
restricts to the identity on both components.

Finally, if
Q=\mathbb{Z}^{\Lambda}_{k}
,
k\geq 2
, then the first map is
\mathbb{Z}_{2}\oplus\mathbb{Z}_{2}\rightarrow 0
, so
\mathrm{Ker}(W_{0}(\textup{eql}))\cong\mathbb{Z}_{2}\oplus\mathbb{Z}_{2}\oplus(G\otimes\mathbb{Z}_{2})\cong\mathbb{Z}_{2}\oplus(P_{e}\otimes\mathbb{Z}_{2})
.
∎

Theorem 5.17
.

Let
P
be an anti-symmetric form parameter.
Then under the identification
W_{0}(Q_{-}\oplus P_{e})\cong\mathbb{Z}_{2}\oplus\Lambda_{1}(P_{e})
we have
\mathrm{Ker}(W_{0}(\textup{eql}))=K(v^{\prime}_{P})
.

Proof.

First we consider
(1,v^{\prime}_{P}(1)\mathop{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{}_{\wedge}}}{{\otimes}}}\nolimits v^{\prime}_{P}(1))\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}\oplus\Lambda_{1}(P_{e})
. If we take a maximal splitting
P\cong Q\oplus G
of
P
as in Lemma
5.16
, then this element is in the first component of
W_{0}(Q_{-}\oplus P_{e})\cong W_{0}(Q_{-}\oplus Q_{e})\oplus W_{0}^{Q_{-}\oplus Q_{e}}(G)
, so it is mapped to
W_{0}(Q)
by
W_{0}(\textup{eql})
. If
Q\not\cong Q_{-}
, then
W_{0}(Q)\cong 0
. If
Q\cong Q_{-}
, then
(1,v^{\prime}_{P}(1)\mathop{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{}_{\wedge}}}{{\otimes}}}\nolimits v^{\prime}_{P}(1))
corresponds to
(1,1)\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}\oplus\mathbb{Z}_{2}\cong W_{0}(Q_{-})\oplus W_{0}^{Q_{-}}(Q_{e})
, which generates the kernel of the map to
\mathbb{Z}_{2}\cong W_{0}(Q)
by the observations in the proof of Lemma
5.16
. Therefore
(1,v^{\prime}_{P}(1)\mathop{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{}_{\wedge}}}{{\otimes}}}\nolimits v^{\prime}_{P}(1))\in\mathrm{Ker}(W_{0}(\textup{eql}))
in both cases.

Next consider
x\mathop{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{}_{\wedge}}}{{\otimes}}}\nolimits x+x\mathop{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{}_{\wedge}}}{{\otimes}}}\nolimits v^{\prime}_{P}(1)=x\mathop{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{}_{\wedge}}}{{\otimes}}}\nolimits(x+v^{\prime}_{P}(1))\in\Lambda_{1}(P_{e})
for some
x\in P_{e}
. By Definition
4.24
the corresponding element of
W_{0}^{Q_{-}}(P_{e})
is represented by the
(Q_{-}\oplus P_{e})
-form

\left(\mathbb{Z}^{2}\left<e,f\right>,\left[\begin{array}[]{cc}0&1\\
-1&0\end{array}\right],\left(\begin{array}[]{c}(0,x)\\
(0,x+v^{\prime}_{P}(1))\end{array}\right)\right).

This is mapped by
W_{0}(\textup{eql})
to the element of
W_{0}(P)
represented by the
P
-form

\left(\mathbb{Z}^{2}\left<e,f\right>,\left[\begin{array}[]{cc}0&1\\
-1&0\end{array}\right],\left(\begin{array}[]{c}x\\
x+v^{\prime}_{P}(1)\end{array}\right)\right)

which is metabolic, because
\left<e-f\right>
is a lagrangian. This shows that
W_{0}(\textup{eql})
sends every generator of
K(v^{\prime}_{P})
to
0
, hence
K(v^{\prime}_{P})\leq\mathrm{Ker}(W_{0}(\textup{eql}))
. Lemmas
5.15
and
5.16
show that
K(v^{\prime}_{P})
and
\mathrm{Ker}(W_{0}(\textup{eql}_{P}))
are isomorphic finite groups, therefore
K(v^{\prime}_{P})=\mathrm{Ker}(W_{0}(\textup{eql}))
.
∎

Using Lemma
5.12
, Theorem
5.17
and the fact that
eql
is a natural transformation, we get the following:

Corollary 5.18
.

The homomorphisms
W_{0}(\textup{eql})
induce a natural isomorphism
\Lambda\circ v^{\prime}_{(-)}\cong W_{0}:\mathbf{FP}_{-}\to\mathbf{Ab}
.

We end this section with Theorem
5.20
below, which is the dual of Theorem
5.8
, and gives an explicit description of
\Lambda(v^{\prime})
.

Definition 5.19
.

Let
v^{\prime}\colon\mathbb{Z}_{2}\to A
be a homomorphism. We define the subgroup

L(v^{\prime})=\bigl\langle{x\mathop{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{}_{\wedge}}}{{\otimes}}}\nolimits x+x\mathop{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{}_{\wedge}}}{{\otimes}}}\nolimits v^{\prime}(1)\mid x\in A}\bigr\rangle\leq\Lambda_{1}(A)

and the quotient group

\Xi(v^{\prime})=\Lambda_{1}(A)/L(v^{\prime}).

Note that since
0\oplus L(v^{\prime})\leq K(v^{\prime})
, the quotient map
\mathbb{Z}_{2}\oplus\Lambda_{1}(A)\rightarrow\Lambda(v^{\prime})
factors as a composition
\mathbb{Z}_{2}\oplus\Lambda_{1}(A)\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}_{2}\oplus\Xi(v^{\prime})\rightarrow\Lambda(v^{\prime})
.

Theorem 5.20
.

Let
v^{\prime}\colon\mathbb{Z}_{2}\to A
be a homomorphism. There is a commutative diagram with short exact rows and columns

\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\textup{Coker}(v^{\prime}_{2})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{u^{\prime}_{v^{\prime}}}
\textstyle{\textup{Coker}(v^{\prime}_{2})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{u^{\prime}_{v^{\prime}}}
\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{K(v^{\prime})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{r}
\textstyle{\mathbb{Z}_{2}\oplus\Lambda_{1}(A)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\Lambda(v^{\prime})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0}
\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathbb{Z}_{2}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\iota}
\textstyle{\mathbb{Z}_{2}\oplus\Xi(v^{\prime})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\Lambda(v^{\prime})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0}
\textstyle{0}
\textstyle{0}

where

\bullet

v^{\prime}_{2}=v^{\prime}\otimes\textup{Id}_{\mathbb{Z}_{2}}\colon\mathbb{Z}_{2}\otimes\mathbb{Z}_{2}=\mathbb{Z}_{2}\rightarrow A\otimes\mathbb{Z}_{2}
, hence
v^{\prime}_{2}(1)=v^{\prime}(1)\otimes 1
;

\bullet

The homomorphism
\iota\colon\mathbb{Z}_{2}\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}_{2}\oplus\Xi(v^{\prime})
is given by
\iota(1)=(1,[v^{\prime}(1)\mathop{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{}_{\wedge}}}{{\otimes}}}\nolimits v^{\prime}(1)])
;

\bullet

The homomorphism
r\colon K(v^{\prime})\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}_{2}
is the composition
K(v^{\prime})\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}_{2}\oplus\Lambda_{1}(A)\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}_{2}
of the natural inclusion and projection maps;

\bullet

The homomorphism
u^{\prime}_{v^{\prime}}\colon\textup{Coker}(v^{\prime}_{2})\rightarrow K(v^{\prime})\leq\mathbb{Z}_{2}\oplus\Lambda_{1}(A)
is induced by the map
A\otimes\mathbb{Z}_{2}\rightarrow K(v^{\prime})
,
x\otimes 1\mapsto(0,x\mathop{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{}_{\wedge}}}{{\otimes}}}\nolimits x+x\mathop{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{}_{\wedge}}}{{\otimes}}}\nolimits v^{\prime}(1))
;

\bullet

The unlabelled maps are the obvious inclusions/quotient maps.

Proof.

First consider the vertical sequence on the left. The map
A\otimes\mathbb{Z}_{2}\rightarrow K(v^{\prime})
sends
v^{\prime}(1)\otimes 1
to
(0,v^{\prime}(1)\mathop{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{}_{\wedge}}}{{\otimes}}}\nolimits v^{\prime}(1)+v^{\prime}(1)\mathop{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{}_{\wedge}}}{{\otimes}}}\nolimits v^{\prime}(1))=0
, hence
u^{\prime}_{v^{\prime}}
is well-defined. Suppose that
v^{\prime}\cong v^{\prime}_{0}\oplus C\colon\mathbb{Z}_{2}\rightarrow B\oplus C
is a maximal splitting of
v^{\prime}
. If
v^{\prime}_{0}\cong\iota_{1}
, then
A\otimes\mathbb{Z}_{2}\cong\mathbb{Z}_{2}\oplus C\otimes\mathbb{Z}_{2}
and
\textup{Coker}(v^{\prime}_{2})\cong C\otimes\mathbb{Z}_{2}
. If
v^{\prime}=0
or
v^{\prime}_{0}\cong\iota_{k}
, then
v^{\prime}_{2}(1)=v^{\prime}(1)\otimes 1=0
, so
\textup{Coker}(v^{\prime}_{2})\cong A\otimes\mathbb{Z}_{2}
. Moreover, the proof of Lemma
5.15
shows that
u^{\prime}_{v^{\prime}}
maps
\textup{Coker}(v^{\prime}_{2})
isomorphically onto
0\oplus L(v^{\prime})\leq K(v^{\prime})
. The quotient
K(v^{\prime})/(0\oplus L(v^{\prime}))
is generated by
[1,v^{\prime}(1)\mathop{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{}_{\wedge}}}{{\otimes}}}\nolimits v^{\prime}(1)]
, and
r
induces an isomorphism onto
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
, showing that the sequence is exact. The map
\mathbb{Z}_{2}\rightarrow K(v^{\prime})
,
1\mapsto(1,v^{\prime}(1)\mathop{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{}_{\wedge}}}{{\otimes}}}\nolimits v^{\prime}(1))
splits
r
.

The vertical sequence in the middle is exact, because the kernel of
\mathbb{Z}_{2}\oplus\Lambda_{1}(A)\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}_{2}\oplus\Xi(v^{\prime})
is
0\oplus L(v^{\prime})
. The middle horizontal sequence is exact by the definition of
K(v^{\prime})
and
\Lambda(v^{\prime})
. Finally, the bottom horizontal sequence is exact, because
K(v^{\prime})=(0\oplus L(v^{\prime}))\oplus\bigl\langle{(1,v^{\prime}(1)\mathop{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{}_{\wedge}}}{{\otimes}}}\nolimits v^{\prime}(1))}\bigr\rangle
.
∎

The projection
\mathbb{Z}_{2}\oplus\Xi(v^{\prime})\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}_{2}
splits the bottom exact sequence, hence
\Lambda(v^{\prime})
is naturally isomorphic to
\Xi(v^{\prime})
.

If
v^{\prime}=0
, then
L(v^{\prime})
is the image of the natural embedding
A\otimes\mathbb{Z}_{2}\rightarrow\Lambda_{1}(A)
, so
\Xi(v^{\prime})=\Lambda(A)
.

If
v^{\prime}\cong\iota_{1}\oplus C
, then, by the proof of Lemma
5.15
,
L(v^{\prime})
is the image of the map
\mathbb{Z}_{2}\otimes C\rightarrow\Lambda_{1}(A)\cong\mathbb{Z}_{2}\oplus\mathbb{Z}_{2}\otimes C\oplus\Lambda_{1}(C)
,
1\otimes x\mapsto(0,1\otimes x,x\mathop{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{}_{\wedge}}}{{\otimes}}}\nolimits x)
. This map can be extended to an automorphism of
\Lambda_{1}(A)
by taking the identity on
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
and
\Lambda_{1}(C)
. This automorphism shows that
\Xi(v^{\prime})=\Lambda_{1}(A)/L(v^{\prime})\cong\Lambda_{1}(A)/\mathbb{Z}_{2}\otimes C\cong\mathbb{Z}_{2}\oplus\Lambda_{1}(C)
. Note that
\Lambda_{1}(C)\cong 0\oplus C\otimes\mathbb{Z}_{2}\oplus\Lambda(C)\cong\Lambda(\mathbb{Z}_{2}\oplus C)\cong\Lambda(A)
, hence
\Xi(v^{\prime})\cong\mathbb{Z}_{2}\oplus\Lambda(A)
, but this isomorphism is not natural.

If
v^{\prime}\cong\iota_{k}\oplus C
,
k\geq 2
, then
L(v^{\prime})
is the direct sum of the image of the map
\mathbb{Z}_{2}\otimes C\rightarrow\Lambda_{1}(A)\cong\mathbb{Z}_{2}\oplus\mathbb{Z}_{2^{k}}\otimes C\oplus\Lambda_{1}(C)
,
1\otimes x\mapsto(0,2^{k-1}\otimes x,x\mathop{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{}_{\wedge}}}{{\otimes}}}\nolimits x)
and
\mathbb{Z}_{2}\oplus 0\oplus 0
. This time we regard
\mathbb{Z}_{2}\otimes C
as a subgroup of
\Lambda_{1}(C)
via a splitting
\Lambda_{1}(C)\cong\mathbb{Z}_{2}\otimes C\oplus\Lambda(C)
, and extend the above map to an automorphism of
\Lambda_{1}(A)
by taking the identity on
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
,
\mathbb{Z}_{2^{k}}\otimes C
and
\Lambda(C)
. We get that
\Xi(v^{\prime})=\Lambda_{1}(A)/L(v^{\prime})\cong\Lambda_{1}(A)/(\mathbb{Z}_{2}\oplus\mathbb{Z}_{2}\otimes C)\cong\mathbb{Z}_{2^{k}}\otimes C\oplus\Lambda(C)\cong\Lambda(\mathbb{Z}_{2^{k}}\oplus C)\cong\Lambda(A)
, but, again, this isomorphism is not natural.

6.
Computing Grothendieck-Witt groups and characterising absorbing forms

In this section we recall the definition of the Grothendieck-Witt group of a form parameter
Q
and describe its relationship to the Witt group of
Q
. We also define and characterise absorbing
Q
-forms.

6.1.
The groups
GW_{0}(Q)
and
W_{0}(Q)

Let
Q
be a form parameter,
\underline{\mu}
a nonsingular
Q
-form and
\{\underline{\mu}\}
the isometry class of
\underline{\mu}
.
Recall from Section
1.2
that
\mathrm{Hom}^{\rm ns}_{\mathbb{Z}}(Q)=\big\{\{\underline{\mu}\}\big\}
is the set of isomorphism classes of nonsingular
Q
-forms, which is a monoid under orthogonal sum,
and that the
Grothendieck-Witt group of
Q
is the Grothendieck group of this monoid:

GW_{0}(Q):={\mathcal{G}r}\big(\mathrm{Hom}^{\rm ns}_{\mathbb{Z}}(Q),\oplus\big).

There is a natural surjection

\pi\colon GW_{0}(Q)\to W_{0}(Q),\quad\bigl(\{\underline{\mu}_{0}\},\{\underline{\mu}_{1}\}\bigr)\mapsto[\underline{\mu}_{0}]-[\underline{\mu}_{1}],

and by definition, there is a short exact sequence of abelian groups

0\to\mathrm{Ker}(\pi)\xrightarrow{}GW_{0}(Q)\xrightarrow{\pi}W_{0}(Q)\to 0.

(8)

This exact sequence allows us to compute
GW_{0}(Q)
in terms of
W_{0}(Q)
, using the following definition and lemma.

Definition 6.1
.

For every form parameter
Q
, there is a well-defined rank homomorphism

\textup{rk}\colon GW_{0}(Q)\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}\text{\,,}\quad\big(\{\underline{\mu_{0}}\},\{\underline{\mu_{1}}\}\big)\mapsto\textup{rk}(\underline{\mu_{0}})-\textup{rk}(\underline{\mu_{1}}).

If
Q
is symmetric, then there is also a signature homomorphism

\sigma\colon GW_{0}(Q)\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}\text{\,,}\quad\big(\{\underline{\mu_{0}}\},\{\underline{\mu_{1}}\}\big)\mapsto\sigma(\underline{\mu_{0}})-\sigma(\underline{\mu_{1}}).

It is well known that
\mathrm{Ker}(\pi)\cong\mathbb{Z}
is generated by
\big(\{H_{\epsilon}(\mathbb{Z})\},\{\underline{0}\}\big)
, where we recall that
\underline{0}
is the
Q
-form on the trivial group;
see e.g.
[
S
, Lemma 2.31]
.
Indeed Schlichting
[
S
, Definition 2.14]
defines
W_{0}(Q)
to be the quotient of
GW_{0}(Q)
by the subgroup of formal differences of hyperbolic forms.
Hence we have

Lemma 6.2
.

For every form parameter
Q
the restriction of the rank homomorphism
\textup{rk}\colon GW_{0}(Q)\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}
yields an isomorphism

\textup{rk}\big|_{\mathrm{Ker}(\pi)}\colon\mathrm{Ker}(\pi)\xrightarrow{\cong}2\mathbb{Z}.\qed

Remark.
We present an
a priori
alternative proof of
Lemma
6.2
below.

Theorem 6.3
.

a) For every form parameter
Q
the map
\textup{rk}\oplus\pi\colon GW_{0}(Q)\to\mathbb{Z}\oplus W_{0}(Q)
is injective.

b) If
Q
is symmetric, then
(n,[\underline{\mu}])\in\mathrm{Im}(\textup{rk}\oplus\pi)
if and only if
\sigma([\underline{\mu}])\equiv n~\mathrm{mod}~2
. If
Q
is anti-symmetric, then
\mathrm{Im}(\textup{rk}\oplus\pi)=2\mathbb{Z}\oplus W_{0}(Q)
.

c) The short exact sequence (
8
) splits.

Proof.

a) The restriction of
\textup{rk}\colon GW_{0}(Q)\to\mathbb{Z}
to
\mathrm{Ker}(\pi)
is injective by Lemma
6.2
.

b) If
Q
is symmetric, then we have
\sigma(\underline{\mu})\equiv\textup{rk}(\underline{\mu})~\mathrm{mod}~2
for every
Q
-form
\underline{\mu}
, therefore
\mathrm{Im}(\textup{rk}\oplus\pi)\subseteq\left\{(n,[\underline{\mu}])\mid\sigma([\underline{\mu}])\equiv n~\mathrm{mod}~2\right\}
. If
Q
is anti-symmetric, then every nonsingular form over
Q
has even rank, so
\mathrm{Im}(\textup{rk}\oplus\pi)\subseteq 2\mathbb{Z}\oplus W_{0}(Q)
.
In the other direction, for any form parameter
Q
, every nonsingular
Q
-form
\underline{\mu}

and
k\geq 0
, we have
(\textup{rk}\oplus\pi)([\underline{\mu}\oplus H_{\epsilon_{Q}}(\mathbb{Z}^{k}),\underline{0}])=(\textup{rk}(\underline{\mu})+2k,[\underline{\mu}])
, and so equality holds for both symmetries.

c) If
Q
is symmetric, then the signature map
\sigma\colon GW_{0}(Q)\to\mathrm{Im}(\sigma)
has a splitting
s\colon\mathrm{Im}(\sigma)\rightarrow GW_{0}(Q)
(because
\mathrm{Im}(\sigma)\subseteq\mathbb{Z}
), and
GW_{0}(Q)=\mathrm{Ker}(\sigma)\oplus\mathrm{Im}(s)
. Since
\mathrm{Ker}(\pi)\subseteq\mathrm{Ker}(\sigma)
and every form with zero signature has even rank, we can define a splitting map
f\colon GW_{0}(Q)\rightarrow\mathrm{Ker}(\pi)\cong 2\mathbb{Z}
by
f\big|_{\mathrm{Ker}(\sigma)}=\textup{rk}\big|_{\mathrm{Ker}(\sigma)}
and
f\big|_{\mathrm{Im}(s)}=0
.

If
Q
is anti-symmetric and
\mathrm{Ker}(\pi)
is identified with
2\mathbb{Z}
using Lemma
6.2
, then the rank homomorphism
\textup{rk}\colon GW_{0}(Q)\to 2\mathbb{Z}
is a canonical splitting of the inclusion
\mathrm{Ker}(\pi)\to GW_{0}(Q)
.
∎

We conclude this subsection with an alternative proof of Lemma
6.2
. Our main motivation
for doing this is that we believe Lemma
6.4
is of independent interest in the study
of
Q
-forms; see Remark
1.11
.

Lemma 6.4
.

Let
\underline{\mu}=(X,\lambda,\mu)
and
\underline{\mu}^{\prime}=(X^{\prime},\lambda^{\prime},\mu^{\prime})
be full metabolic
Q
-forms on isomorphic free abelian groups
X\cong X^{\prime}
. Then for some integer
m\geq 0
,
\underline{\mu}\oplus H_{\epsilon_{Q}}(\mathbb{Z}^{m})
and
\underline{\mu}^{\prime}\oplus H_{\epsilon_{Q}}(\mathbb{Z}^{m})
are isometric
Q
-forms.

Proof.

If
Q
is symmetric, then by Lemma
2.29
we can regard a
Q
-form
(X,\lambda,\mu)
as a
(Q^{+}\oplus SQ)
-form
(X,\lambda,(\mu_{0},\mu_{1}))
such that
v(\mu_{1}(x))\equiv\lambda(x,x)
mod
2
. The analogous statement for such
(Q^{+}\oplus SQ)
-forms is proved in
[
N2
, Theorem 2.22 a)]
.

Here we consider the anti-symmetric case. As
\underline{\mu}
and
\underline{\mu}^{\prime}
are metabolic, they have lagrangians
L<X
and
L^{\prime}<X^{\prime}
. Let
e_{1},\ldots,e_{k},f_{1},\ldots,f_{k}
and
e^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,e^{\prime}_{k},f^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,f^{\prime}_{k}
be symplectic bases for the bilinear forms
(X,\lambda)
and
(X^{\prime},\lambda^{\prime})
respectively such that
L=\left<e_{1},\ldots,e_{k}\right>
and
L^{\prime}=\left<e_{1}^{\prime},\ldots,e_{k}^{\prime}\right>
, and let
N=\left<f_{1},\ldots,f_{k}\right>
and
N^{\prime}=\left<f^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,f^{\prime}_{k}\right>
. Since
L
and
L^{\prime}
are lagrangians, we have
S\mu\big|_{L}=0
and
S\mu^{\prime}\big|_{L^{\prime}}=0
, so
S\mu\big|_{N}\colon N\rightarrow SQ
and
S\mu^{\prime}\big|_{N^{\prime}}\colon N^{\prime}\rightarrow SQ
are surjective (because
\underline{\mu}
and
\underline{\mu}^{\prime}
are full). By
[
N2
, Lemma 2.21 a)]
there is an
m\geq 0
and an isomorphism
h\colon N\oplus\mathbb{Z}^{m}\rightarrow N^{\prime}\oplus\mathbb{Z}^{m}
such that
(S\mu\big|_{N}+0)=(S\mu^{\prime}\big|_{N^{\prime}}+0)\circ h\colon N\oplus\mathbb{Z}^{m}\rightarrow SQ
.

Let
\tilde{X}=X\oplus\mathbb{Z}^{2m}
,
\tilde{L}=L\oplus\mathbb{Z}^{m}
and
\tilde{N}=N\oplus\mathbb{Z}^{m}
, so that
\tilde{X}=\tilde{L}\oplus\tilde{N}
and if
H_{-}(\mathbb{Z}^{m})=(\mathbb{Z}^{m}\oplus\mathbb{Z}^{m},\lambda_{H},\mu_{H})
(where each copy of
\mathbb{Z}^{m}
is a lagrangian), then
\underline{\mu}\oplus H_{-}(\mathbb{Z}^{m})=(\tilde{X},\lambda\oplus\lambda_{H},\mu\oplus\mu_{H})
and
\tilde{L}
is a lagrangian in
\underline{\mu}\oplus H_{-}(\mathbb{Z}^{m})
. Let
l=k+m
and suppose that
e_{k+1},\ldots,e_{l},f_{k+1},\ldots,f_{l}
is a symplectic basis of
H_{-}(\mathbb{Z}^{m})
(with
\left<e_{k+1},\ldots,e_{l}\right>
and
\left<f_{k+1},\ldots,f_{l}\right>
being the standard lagrangians). Then
\tilde{L}=\left<e_{1},\ldots,e_{l}\right>
,
\tilde{N}=\left<f_{1},\ldots,f_{l}\right>
and
e_{1},\ldots,e_{l},f_{1},\ldots,f_{l}
is a symplectic basis of
(\tilde{X},\lambda\oplus\lambda_{H})
. Similarly let
\tilde{X}^{\prime}=X^{\prime}\oplus\mathbb{Z}^{2m}
,
\tilde{L}^{\prime}=L^{\prime}\oplus\mathbb{Z}^{m}
and
\tilde{N}^{\prime}=N^{\prime}\oplus\mathbb{Z}^{m}
so that
\underline{\mu}^{\prime}\oplus H_{-}(\mathbb{Z}^{m})=(\tilde{X}^{\prime},\lambda^{\prime}\oplus\lambda_{H},\mu^{\prime}\oplus\mu_{H})
.

The map
h
can be regarded as an isomorphism
\tilde{N}\rightarrow\tilde{N}^{\prime}
with
S(\mu\oplus\mu_{H}\big|_{\tilde{N}})=S(\mu^{\prime}\oplus\mu_{H}\big|_{\tilde{N}^{\prime}})\circ h\colon\tilde{N}\rightarrow SQ
. For every
1\leq i\leq l
let
f^{\prime\prime}_{i}=h(f_{i})
, then
f^{\prime\prime}_{1},\ldots,f^{\prime\prime}_{l}
is a basis of
\tilde{N}^{\prime}
. Since the adjoint of
\lambda^{\prime}\oplus\lambda_{H}
determines an isomorphism
\tilde{L}^{\prime}\cong(\tilde{N}^{\prime})^{*}
, we get a basis
e^{\prime\prime}_{1},\ldots,e^{\prime\prime}_{l}
of
\tilde{L}^{\prime}
such that
e^{\prime\prime}_{1},\ldots,e^{\prime\prime}_{l},f^{\prime\prime}_{1},\ldots,f^{\prime\prime}_{l}
is a symplectic basis of
(\tilde{X}^{\prime},\lambda^{\prime}\oplus\lambda_{H})
. For every
1\leq i\leq l
we have
S(\mu\oplus\mu_{H})(f_{i})=S(\mu^{\prime}\oplus\mu_{H})(f^{\prime\prime}_{i})\in SQ=Q_{e}/\mathrm{Im}({\rm p})
, so
(\mu^{\prime}\oplus\mu_{H})(f^{\prime\prime}_{i})\in Q_{e}
is either
(\mu\oplus\mu_{H})(f_{i})
or
(\mu\oplus\mu_{H})(f_{i})+{\rm p}(1)
. We define
f^{\prime\prime\prime}_{i}:=f^{\prime\prime}_{i}
in the case where
(\mu^{\prime}\oplus\mu_{H})(f^{\prime\prime}_{i})=(\mu\oplus\mu_{H})(f_{i})
and
f^{\prime\prime\prime}_{i}:=f^{\prime\prime}_{i}+e^{\prime\prime}_{i}
otherwise.

Then
e^{\prime\prime}_{1},\ldots,e^{\prime\prime}_{l},f^{\prime\prime\prime}_{1},\ldots,f^{\prime\prime\prime}_{l}
is again a symplectic basis of
(\tilde{X}^{\prime},\lambda^{\prime}\oplus\lambda_{H})
. Moreover, we have the equalities
(\mu\oplus\mu_{H})(e_{i})=0=(\mu^{\prime}\oplus\mu_{H})(e^{\prime\prime}_{i})
and
(\mu\oplus\mu_{H})(f_{i})=(\mu^{\prime}\oplus\mu_{H})(f^{\prime\prime\prime}_{i})
by the definition of
f^{\prime\prime\prime}_{i}
. Therefore the isomorphism
I\colon\tilde{X}\rightarrow\tilde{X}^{\prime}
given by
I(e_{i})=e^{\prime\prime}_{i}
and
I(f_{i})=f^{\prime\prime\prime}_{i}
is an isometry between
\underline{\mu}\oplus H_{-}(\mathbb{Z}^{m})
and
\underline{\mu}^{\prime}\oplus H_{-}(\mathbb{Z}^{m})
.
∎

Recall from Definition
2.26
that a
Q
-form

(X,\lambda,\mu)
is called full if its linearisation
S\mu\colon X\to SQ
is surjective.

Lemma 6.5
.

For any form parameter
Q
there exists a full metabolic
Q
-form.

Proof.

Let
Q=(Q_{e},{\rm h},{\rm p})
and suppose that
q_{1},q_{2},\ldots,q_{k}
is a generating system for
Q_{e}
. Consider the triple

\left(\mathbb{Z}^{k}\oplus\mathbb{Z}^{k},\left[\begin{array}[]{cc}0&I_{k}\\
\epsilon_{Q}I_{k}&D\end{array}\right],(0,\ldots,0,q_{1},\ldots,q_{k})^{\rm t}\right),

where
I_{k}
is the
k\times k
identity matrix,
D
is a
k\times k
diagonal matrix with entries
{\rm h}(q_{1}),\ldots,{\rm h}(q_{k})
on the diagonal and
(\cdot)^{\rm t}
denotes the transpose. By Remark
2.20
this data defines a
Q
-form,
which is metabolic because the matrix is nonsingular and

\mathbb{Z}^{k}\oplus 0
a lagrangian. It is also full, because if
\pi\colon Q_{e}\rightarrow SQ
denotes the quotient homomorphism, then
\pi(q_{1}),\ldots,\pi(q_{k})
is a generating system for
SQ
.
∎

Alternative proof of Lemma
6.2
.

Metabolic forms have even rank, and so
\textup{rk}(\mathrm{Ker}(\pi))\subseteq 2\mathbb{Z}
. Moreover, we have
\textup{rk}([H_{\epsilon_{Q}}(\mathbb{Z}^{m}),\underline{0}])=2m
,
and so
\textup{rk}\colon\mathrm{Ker}(\pi)\to 2\mathbb{Z}
is surjective. Finally assume that

\underline{\mu_{0}}
and
\underline{\mu_{1}}
are metabolic
Q
-forms of equal rank, and consider
\big(\{\underline{\mu_{0}}\},\{\underline{\mu_{1}}\}\big)\in\mathrm{Ker}(\textup{rk}\big|_{\mathrm{Ker}(\pi)})
.
By Lemma
6.5
there is a full metabolic
Q
-form
\underline{\mu^{\prime}}
, and by applying Lemma
6.4
to
\underline{\mu_{0}}\oplus\underline{\mu^{\prime}}
and
\underline{\mu_{1}}\oplus\underline{\mu^{\prime}}
we get that
\underline{\mu_{0}}\oplus\underline{\mu^{\prime}}\oplus H_{\epsilon_{Q}}(\mathbb{Z}^{m})\cong\underline{\mu_{1}}\oplus\underline{\mu^{\prime}}\oplus H_{\epsilon_{Q}}(\mathbb{Z}^{m})
for some
m
. This shows that

\big(\{\underline{\mu_{0}}\},\{\underline{\mu_{1}}\}\big)=0
,
and therefore
\textup{rk}\big|_{\mathrm{Ker}(\pi)}\colon\mathrm{Ker}(\pi)\to 2\mathbb{Z}
is an isomorphism.
∎

6.2.
Absorbing
Q
-forms

In this subsection we characterise absorbing
Q
-forms.

Definition 6.6
.

a)

Let
\underline{\mu}
and
\underline{\eta}
be nonsingular
Q
-forms. We say that
\underline{\mu}

absorbs

\underline{\eta}
if
\underline{\eta}
can be embedded in
k\underline{\mu}=\underline{\mu}\oplus\ldots\oplus\underline{\mu}
for some
k\geq 1
.

b)

A nonsingular
Q
-form
\underline{\mu}
is called
absorbing
, if it absorbs all nonsingular
Q
-forms.

Theorem 6.7
.

A nonsingular
Q
-form is absorbing if and only if it is indefinite and full.

Proof.

A definite form cannot absorb any indefinite form, and a non-full form cannot absorb any full form, so the conditions are necessary.

For the other direction first note that
\underline{\mu}
absorbs
\underline{\eta}\oplus\underline{\eta}^{\prime}
if and only if it absorbs both
\underline{\eta}
and
\underline{\eta}^{\prime}
. As we saw before Definition
2.31
,
\underline{\eta}\oplus(-\underline{\eta})
is metabolic for every nonsingular
\underline{\eta}
.
Moreover, it elementary to prove that every metabolic form can be written as a direct sum of rank-
2
metabolic forms,
and therefore a
Q
-form is absorbing if and only if it absorbs every rank-
2
metabolic form.

Now let
\underline{\mu}=(X,\lambda,\mu)
be a full indefinite nonsingular form over
Q=(Q_{e},{\rm h},{\rm p})
, and let
\pi\colon Q_{e}\rightarrow SQ
denote the projection. Let
\underline{\eta}
be a rank-
2
metabolic form, it is isomorphic to

\left(\mathbb{Z}^{2}\left<e,f\right>,\left[\begin{array}[]{cc}0&1\\
\epsilon_{Q}&a\end{array}\right],\left(\begin{array}[]{c}0\\
q\end{array}\right)\right)

for some
q\in Q_{e}
and
a={\rm h}(q)\in\mathbb{Z}
. We will show that
\underline{\eta}
embeds into
3\underline{\mu}
.

Since
\underline{\mu}
is indefinite, there is a primitive
x\in X
such that
\lambda(x,x)=0
, and since it is nonsingular, there is a
y\in X
such that
\lambda(x,y)=1
. Finally,
\underline{\mu}
is full, so there is a
z\in X
such that
S\mu(z)=\pi(q)-S\mu(y)
. If
Q
is symmetric, then let
\delta=\frac{1}{2}{\rm h}(q-\mu(y)-\mu(z))
, which is an integer, because
\pi(q-\mu(y)-\mu(z))=\pi(q)-S\mu(y)-S\mu(z)=0
.
It follows that
q-\mu(y)-\mu(z)\in\mathrm{Ker}(\pi)=\mathrm{Im}({\rm p})
, and
{\rm h}\circ{\rm p}=2\textup{Id}_{\mathbb{Z}}
. If
Q
is anti-symmetric, then let
\delta=0
if
\mu(y)+\mu(z)=q
and
\delta=1
otherwise.

We will use the notation
(X^{3},\bar{\lambda},\bar{\mu})
for
3\underline{\mu}
. We define the homomorphism
i\colon\mathbb{Z}^{2}\rightarrow X^{3}
by

i(e)=(x,-x,0)\quad\text{and}\quad i(f)=(y+\delta x,-\delta x,z).

Then
\bar{\lambda}(i(e),i(e))=0
and
\bar{\lambda}(i(e),i(f))=1
.
We also have

\bar{\mu}(i(e))=\mu(x)+\mu(-x)=\mu(x)+\mu(-x)+{\rm p}(\lambda(x,-x))=\mu(x-x)=0

and
S\bar{\mu}(i(f))=S\mu(y)+S\mu(z)=\pi(q)
.
If
Q
is symmetric, then

\bar{\lambda}(i(f),i(f))=\lambda(y,y)+2\delta+\lambda(z,z)={\rm h}(\mu(y))+{\rm h}(q-\mu(y)-\mu(z))+{\rm h}(\mu(z))={\rm h}(q)=a.

This means that
{\rm h}(\bar{\mu}(i(f)))={\rm h}(q)
and
\pi(\bar{\mu}(i(f)))=\pi(q)
, so by Lemma
2.10

\bar{\mu}(i(f))=q
.
If
Q
is anti-symmetric, then
\bar{\lambda}(i(f),i(f))=0=a
. Moreover,

\bar{\mu}(i(f))=\mu(y+\delta x)+\mu(-\delta x)+\mu(z)=\mu(y)+\mu(\delta x)+\delta{\rm p}(1)+\mu(-\delta x)+\mu(z)=\mu(y)+\mu(z)+\delta{\rm p}(1).

The previously established equality
S\mu(y)+S\mu(z)=\pi(q)
implies that
\mu(y)+\mu(z)-q\in\mathrm{Ker}(\pi)=\mathrm{Im}({\rm p})
, so either
\mu(y)+\mu(z)=q
or
\mu(y)+\mu(z)=q+{\rm p}(1)
. By the definition of
\delta
we have
\bar{\mu}(i(f))=q
in both cases.

Therefore
i
is a morphism of
Q
-forms. Since
\underline{\eta}
is nonsingular, this implies that
i
is injective. Therefore
\underline{\eta}
embeds into
3\underline{\mu}
, and by our earlier observations this shows that
\underline{\mu}
is absorbing.
∎

7.
Witt groups and algebraic Poincaré cobordism

Extended quadratic forms and their Witt groups were previously
defined by Ranicki in
[
R1
]
, where they are located in the larger context
of the bordism groups of algebraic Poincaré complexes with structure maps to chain bundles.
In this section we briefly recall this larger setting and relate Ranicki’s algebraic
Wu classes to the quasi-Wu classes of Definition
2.9
.

As the objects and definitions of algebraic surgery are involved, we will not go into
them here in detail. Rather, we assume that the reader is familiar with the theory of chain bundles
(B_{*},\beta)

and algebraic Poincaré complexes with
(B_{*},\beta)
-structure as developed by Weiss and Ranicki
[
We
,
R1
]
;
we use
[
R1
]
as a reference.
We fix the ring
\mathbb{Z}
with trivial involution and let
B_{*}
be a chain complex of finitely generated
free
\mathbb{Z}
-modules with
B_{i}=0
for all
i<0
.
A chain bundle
(B_{*},\beta)
is an algebraic model for a stable vector bundle,
and more generally a stable spherical fibration.
Indeed, if
\xi
is a spherical fibration over a simply connected
CW
-complex
X
,
then
\xi
defines a chain bundle
(C_{*}(X),\beta(\xi))
over the cellular chain complex of
X
.
An
n
-dimensional
(B_{*},\beta)
-Poincaré complex
is an algebraic analogue of closed
n
-dimensional normal
\xi
-manifold and
the
\beta
-twisted
L
-groups,
L^{n}(B_{*},\beta)
, which are the algebraic bordism classes of

n
-dimensional
(B_{*},\beta)
-Poincaré complexes, model
the bordism group of closed
n
-dimensional normal
\xi
-manifolds.

The
\beta
-twisted
L
-groups are natural for maps of chain bundles,
and by definition,
L_{n}(\mathbb{Z})
, the usual quadratic
L
-group of the ring
\mathbb{Z}
(see e.g.
[
R2
, Defns. 11.57 & 12.23]
),
is
L_{n}(0,0)
, where
(0,0)
denotes the unique chain bundle over the zero chain complex.
It follows
that there is a natural map
L_{n}(\mathbb{Z})\to L^{n}(B_{*},\beta)
, which
Weiss
[
We
, 2.21(iii) & (iv)]
places into a long exact sequence of abelian groups

\dots\xrightarrow{}Q_{n+1}(B_{*},\beta)\xrightarrow{}L_{n}(\mathbb{Z})\xrightarrow{}L^{n}(B_{*},\beta)\xrightarrow{}Q_{n}(B_{*},\beta)\xrightarrow{}L_{n-1}(\mathbb{Z})\xrightarrow{}\dots~,

(9)

where
Q_{n}(B_{*},\beta)
is the group of
n
-dimensional
\beta
-twisted quadratic structures on
B_{*}
defined in
[
R1
, §6]
.

The
\beta
-twisted
L
-groups receive homomorphisms from certain Witt groups, as we now explain.
For each non-negative integer
q
, Ranicki defines a
(-1)^{q}
-symmetric form parameter
Q_{(B_{*},\beta)}(q)

[
R1
, §10]
, which for brevity we denote by
Q_{\beta}(q)
.
Moreover, given a nonsingular
Q_{\beta}(q)
-form
(X,\lambda,\mu)
,
Ranicki constructs a
2q
-dimensional

(B_{*},\beta)
-Poincaré complex concentrated in dimension
q

[
R1
, p. 250]
, and proves that this construction induces a natural homomorphism

\eta_{\beta}\colon W_{0}(Q_{\beta}(q))\to L^{2q}(B_{*},\beta).

Below we formulate a conjecture which describes conditions under which the homomorphism
\eta_{\beta}
may be a split injection or an isomorphism. At all events, to use
\eta_{\beta}
it is important to identify the form parameter
Q_{\beta}(q)
by finding the quasi-Wu class of
Q_{\beta}(q)
, and we do this now.
By construction (see
[
R1
, §10]
)
the linearisation of the form parameter
Q_{\beta}(q)
is given by

SQ_{\beta}(q)=H_{q}(B_{*}).

The chain bundle
\beta
also defines algebraic Wu classes
\widehat{v}_{i}(\beta)\in H^{i}(B_{*};\mathbb{Z}_{2})

[
R1
, §4]

such that if
\beta=\beta(\xi)
is the chain bundle defined by a spherical fibration then,

\widehat{v}_{i}(\beta(\xi))=v_{i}(\xi)
, where
v_{i}(\xi)
are the usual Wu classes of
\xi
.
By the Universal Coefficient Theorem, we have a split short exact sequence

0\to\textup{Ext}_{1}^{\mathbb{Z}}(H_{q-1}(B_{*}),\mathbb{Z}_{2})\to H^{q}(B_{*};\mathbb{Z}_{2})\xrightarrow{\mathrm{ev}}\textup{Hom}(H_{q}(B_{*}),\mathbb{Z}_{2})\to 0.

Recalling Definition
2.9
, we note that the quasi-Wu classes of
Q_{\beta}(q)
are such that if
q=2k
is even then
v_{Q_{\beta}(2k)}\in\textup{Hom}(H_{q}(B_{*}),\mathbb{Z}_{2})

and if
q=2k{+}1
is odd, then either
v^{\prime}_{Q_{\beta}(2k+1)}=0
, or
v^{\prime}_{Q_{\beta}(2k+1)}\neq 0

is determined by the extension

[v^{\prime}_{Q_{\beta}(2k+1)}]:=\bigl[0\to\mathbb{Z}_{2}\to(Q_{\beta}(2k{+}1))_{e}\to H_{2k+1}(B_{*})\to 0\bigr].

Proposition 7.1
.

The algebraic Wu classes
\widehat{v}_{*}(\beta)
of a chain bundle
(B_{*},\beta)
determine the quasi-Wu classes
of the form parameter
Q_{\beta}(q)
as follows:

a)

If
q=2k
, then
v_{Q_{\beta}(2k)}=\mathrm{ev}(\widehat{v}_{2k}(\beta))
.

b)

If
q=2k{+}1
and
\mathrm{ev}(\widehat{v}_{2k+2}(\beta))\neq 0
then then
v^{\prime}_{Q_{\beta}(2k+1)}=0
.

c)

If
q=2k{+}1
and
\mathrm{ev}(\widehat{v}_{2k+2}(\beta))=0
, then
\widehat{v}_{2k+2}(\beta)\in\textup{Ext}^{\mathbb{Z}}_{1}(H_{2k+1}(B_{*}),\mathbb{Z}_{2})

and
[v^{\prime}_{Q_{\beta}(2k+1)}]=\widehat{v}_{2k+2}(\beta)
.

Remark 7.2
.

For
q=2k{+}1
odd in Proposition
7.1
, we observe that
v^{\prime}_{Q_{\beta}(q)}=0
if and only if
\hat{v}_{2k+2}(\beta)=0
,
and the classification of anti-symmetric form parameters
Q
shows that
Q_{-}
is a summand of
Q
if and only if

v^{\prime}_{Q}=0
. By Theorem
4.3

the Arf invariant is defined for
Q
-forms if and only if
v^{\prime}_{Q}=0
.
Hence we obtain an algebraic analogue of results of Browder
[
Browd1
, §3]
and Brown
[
Brown
, Corollary 1.22]
, stating that
v_{2q+2}(\xi)=0
is sufficient to define an Arf-invariant for closed
(4q{+}2)
-dimensional normal
\xi
-manifolds, where normal
\xi
-manifolds are defined in Section
1.4
.
Much deeper algebraic analogues of the work of Browder and Brown on quadratic refinements can be found in
[
We
]
.

Proof of Proposition
7.1
.

a) This follows directly by inspecting the formula for
{\rm h}
in
[
R1
, p. 249]
(it is denoted there by
s
),
and the formula for

\widehat{v}_{2k}
on
[
R1
, p. 232]
.

b) The braid
[
R1
, p. 249]
gives
an exact sequence of abelian groups

H_{2k+2}(B)\xrightarrow{\mathrm{ev}(\widehat{v}_{2k+2}(\beta))}(Q_{-})_{e}\xrightarrow{}Q_{\beta}(2k{+}1)_{e}\to H_{2k+1}(B)\to 0,

where
(Q_{-})_{e}\to Q_{\beta}(2k{+}1)

is
v^{\prime}_{Q_{\beta}(2k{+}1)}
. The exactness of this sequence proves
Part b).

c) This follows by inspecting the formula on
[
R1
, p. 249]
for addition in
Q_{\beta}(2k{+}1)
.
∎

With Proposition
7.1
and Theorem
1.5

we can compute the Witt group
W_{0}(Q_{\beta}(q))
from knowledge of
H_{q}(B)
and the algebraic Wu classes of
\beta
. To apply these computations to the
\beta
-twisted
L
-groups and
\beta
-twisted
Q
-groups, we need more information about
the homomorphism
\eta_{\beta}
.

Conjecture 7.3
.

Let
(B_{*},\beta)
be a chain bundle and
q\geq 0
.
If
H_{q-1}(B_{*})
is torsion free, then the natural homomorphism

\eta_{\beta}\colon W_{0}(Q_{\beta}(q))\to L^{2q}(B_{*},\beta)

is split injective.
If, in addition,
H_{i}(B_{*})=0
for all
i<q
, then
\eta_{\beta}
is an isomorphism.

Remark 7.4
.

One idea for constructing an inverse to
\eta_{\beta}
goes as follows.
In
[
R1
, §10]
Ranicki proves that the
q
-th homology group of a
2q
-dimensional
(B_{*},\beta)
-Poincaré complex supports a
Q_{\beta}(q)
-form. If the map from
(B_{*},\beta)
-Poincaré complex to
B_{*}
is
q
-connected, then since
H_{q-1}(B_{*})
is torsion free,
Poincaré duality ensures that this
Q_{\beta}(q)
-form is nonsingular,
and we can take its Witt class a candidate for the value of
\eta_{\beta}^{-1}
.
If the map from the
(B_{*},\beta)
-Poincaré complex to
B_{*}
is not
q
-connected, we expect that algebraic surgery below the middle dimension, e.g.
[
We
, Proposition 5.4]
, can be used to show both that the
(B_{*},\beta)
-Poincaré complex is
(B_{*},\beta)
-cobordant to a
(B_{*},\beta)
-Poincaré complex-connected complex with a
q
-connected map to
B_{*}
, and that the Witt class of the
Q_{\beta}(q)
-forms obtained is well-defined.

To apply Conjecture
7.3
we need a further definition in the symmetric case. This arises since Weiss’s sequence (
9
) involves the quadratic
L
-group
L_{4k}(\mathbb{Z})=W_{0}(Q_{+})
, whereas our methods compare
W_{0}(Q_{\beta}(2k))
with the symmetric
L
-group
W_{0}(Q^{+})=L^{4k}(\mathbb{Z})
.
For
v\colon A\to\mathbb{Z}_{2}
a homomorphism, we recall
\Sigma(v)\subset\mathbb{Z}\oplus\Gamma(A)

from Definition
5.3
.
We make the following mod
8
modification of
\Sigma(v)
.

Definition 7.5
.

Let
\Sigma_{8}(v)\leq\mathbb{Z}/8\oplus\Gamma(A)
denote the subgroup generated by the elements

(1,x\otimes 1),\quad\text{and}\quad(0,[k_{1},k_{2}]\otimes 1),

where
x\in v^{-1}(1)
and
k_{1},k_{2}\in\mathrm{Ker}(v)
.

The next conjecture would be a direct consequence of Conjecture
7.3
, Proposition
7.1
and Theorem
1.5
. Recall that
\iota_{1}\colon\mathbb{Z}_{2}\to\mathbb{Z}_{2}
is the identity. For any homomorphism
v^{\prime}\colon\mathbb{Z}_{2}\to A
, there is a canonical map of homomorphisms
\iota_{1}\to v^{\prime}
, which induces a homomorphism
\Lambda(\iota_{1})\to\Lambda(v^{\prime})
.

Conjecture 7.6
.

Let
(B_{*},\beta)
be a chain bundle with
algebraic Wu class
\widehat{v}_{2k}(\beta)
.

If
(B_{*},\beta)
is
(2k{-}1)
-connected then

a)

L^{4k}(B_{*},\beta)\cong W_{0}(Q_{\beta}(2k))\cong\Sigma\bigl(\mathrm{ev}(\widehat{v}_{2k}(\beta))\bigr)
;

b)

Q_{4k}(B_{*},\beta)\cong\Sigma_{8}\bigl(\mathrm{ev}(\widehat{v}_{2k}(\beta))\bigr)
.

If
(B_{*},\beta)
is
(2k{-}2)
-connected
and
\mathrm{ev}(\widehat{v}_{2k}(\beta))=0
, then
[v^{\prime}_{Q_{\beta}(2k{-}1)}]=\widehat{v}_{2k}(\beta)
and

c)

L^{4k-2}(B_{*},\beta)\cong W_{0}(Q_{\beta}(2k{-}1))\cong\Lambda(v^{\prime}_{Q_{\beta}(2k{-}1)})
;

d)

Q_{4k-2}(B_{*},\beta)\cong\textup{Coker}\bigl(\Lambda(\iota_{1})\to\Lambda(v^{\prime}_{Q_{\beta}(2k{-1})})\bigr)
.

If
(B_{*},\beta)
is
(2k{-}2)
-connected and

\mathrm{ev}(\widehat{v}_{2k}(\beta))\neq 0
then
Q_{\beta}(2k{-1})\cong Q^{-}\oplus H_{2k-1}(B_{*})
and

e)

L^{4k-2}(B_{*},\beta)\cong Q_{4k-2}(B_{*},\beta)\cong W_{0}(Q_{\beta}(2k{-}1))\cong\Lambda(H_{2k-1}(B_{*}))
.

Remark 7.7
.

The conjectural computation of
\beta
-twisted
Q
-groups in Conjecture
7.6

is consistent with the computations of Banagl and Ranicki
[
B-R
, Proposition 52]
.
